# Of Atheism.

<margin>*Atheism*</margin>
Another cause is the *scandal of priests*; when it has come to that which *St. Bernard* says: "It is no longer the case that as the people are, so is the priest; for the people are not even as [bad as] the priest." <note>original: "Non est iam dicere, ut Populus, sic Sacerdos: quia nec sic Populus, ut Sacerdos." St. Bernard of Clairvaux was a 12th-century abbot who famously critiqued the corruption of the clergy.</note> A third is the *custom of profane scoffing* in *holy matters*, which, bit by bit, defaces the *reverence* of *religion*. And lastly, *learned times*, especially during *peace* and *prosperity*; for *troubles* and *adversities* bow *men’s minds* more toward *religion*. Those who deny a *God* destroy *man’s nobility*. For certainly, man is *kin* to the *beasts* by his *body*; and if he is not *kin* to *God* by his *spirit*, he is a *base* and *ignoble creature*. It likewise destroys *magnanimity* <term>nobility of spirit and greatness of soul</term> and the *raising* of *human nature*. For take the *example* of a *dog*, and observe what *generosity* and *courage* he displays when he finds himself supported by a *man*, who to him stands in the place of a *God*, or a *better nature* <note>original: "Melior Natura"</note>. This courage is clearly such that the *creature* could never attain it without that *confidence* in a *nature* better than his own. So *man*, when

<summary>Bacon identifies the causes of atheism—including corrupt clergy and peaceful, prosperous times—and argues that denying God lowers humans to the level of animals, robbing them of their inherent dignity and courage.</summary>

<header>Of Atheism</header>

## Of Atheism

He rests and assures himself upon divine protection and favor, and thereby gathers a power and faith which human nature, in itself, could not obtain. Therefore, just as *atheism* is in all respects hateful, it is especially so in this: it deprives human nature of the means to exalt itself above human frailty. 

As it is with individual persons, so it is with nations. Never was there a state so known for magnanimity <term>greatness of soul, courage, and generosity</term> as *Rome*. Concerning this state, hear what *Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), a renowned Roman statesman, orator, and philosopher</note> says:

"Let us admire ourselves as much as we please, members of the Senate, yet we have not surpassed the Spaniards in number, nor the Gauls in strength, nor the Carthaginians <note>original: 'Pœnos'</note> in cunning, nor the Greeks in the arts, nor finally the Italians and Latins themselves in the inherent and native pride of this very nation and land. But by our piety and religion, and by this one wisdom—that we have perceived that all things are ruled and governed by the power of the immortal gods—it is by this that we have overcome all races and nations." <note>original Latin: "Quam volumus, licet, patres conscripti, nos amemus, tamen nec numero Hispanos, nec robore Gallos, nec calliditate Pœnos, nec artibus Græcos, nec deniq; hoc ipso huius Gentis & Terræ domestico natiuoq; sensu Italos ipsos & Latinos; sed Pietate, ac Religione, atque hac vnâ Sapientiâ, quod Deorum Immortalium Numine, omnia regi, gubernariq; perspeximus, omnes Gentes Nationesq; superauimus."</note>

# Of Superstition.
## XVII.

It would be better to have no opinion of *God* at all than to have an opinion that is unworthy of him; for the one is unbelief, the other is slander <note>original: "contumely"</note>. And certainly, *superstition* is a reproach to the *Deity*. *Plutarch* <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–119 AD) known for his "Moralia" and "Parallel Lives."</note> says well to that purpose: "Surely," he says, "I would much rather have men say there was never any such man as Plutarch at all than have them say there was a Plutarch who would eat his children as soon as they were born, as the poets say of *Saturn* <note>In Roman mythology, the Titan Saturn (Cronus) swallowed his children to prevent them from eventually overthrowing him.</note>." And, just as the slander is greater toward *God*, so the danger is greater toward *men*. *Atheism* leaves a man to his senses, to philosophy, to natural piety <note>The innate sense of duty and moral obligation to one's family and community.</note>, to laws, and to reputation—all of which may be

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that superstition is worse than atheism because it creates a distorted, insulting image of God, whereas atheism still leaves a person guided by reason and social laws.</summary>
<keywords>Superstition, Atheism, Plutarch, Saturn, theology, morality, divine nature</keywords>

# Of Superstition.

be guides to an outward moral virtue, even if *Religion* did not exist; but *Superstition* overthrows all of these and establishes an absolute monarchy in the minds of men. Therefore, *Atheism* has never disturbed states; for it makes men cautious of their own interests, as they look no further <note>meaning they do not look toward an afterlife or divine judgment</note>. And we see that times inclined toward *Atheism* (such as the era of *Augustus Caesar*) were civil and peaceful times. 

But *Superstition* has been the ruin <note>original: "confusion"</note> of many states, and it introduces a new *Primum Mobile* <note>original: *Primum Mobile*—the "first mover," the outermost sphere in the ancient geocentric model of the universe that was believed to drive the motion of all the inner spheres</note> that violently sweeps away all the spheres of government. The master of *Superstition* is the common people; and in all *superstition*, wise men follow fools, and arguments are adapted to suit existing practices in a reversed order <note>meaning instead of theory guiding practice, they invent theories to justify whatever strange things people are already doing</note>. 

It was solemnly said by some of the prelates in the *Council of Trent* <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563) was a major assembly of the Catholic Church that defined its doctrines in response to the Protestant Reformation</note>, where the doctrine of the *Schoolmen* <note>Scholastic theologians of the Middle Ages who used Aristotelian logic</note> held great influence, that the *Schoolmen* were like *astronomers* who invented *eccentrics* and *epicycles* <note>Complex geometric models used in ancient astronomy to explain the seemingly irregular paths of planets</note> and similar *machinery of the spheres* to account for the *phenomena* <note>observable celestial movements</note>, even though they knew no such things existed. ### Of Superstition.

...number of subtle and intricate *axioms* and *theorems* to justify <note>original: "save"</note> the practices of the Church. The *causes* of *superstition* are: pleasing and sensory <note>original: "sensual," meaning appealing to the five senses</note> rites and ceremonies; an excess of outward and hypocritical <term>Pharisaical</term> holiness; an over-great reverence for traditions, which cannot help but burden the Church; the schemes of high-ranking clergy <term>prelates</term> for their own ambition and profit <term>lucre</term>; favoring "good intentions" too much, which opens the gate to whims <note>original: "conceits"</note> and novelties; attempting to understand divine matters through a human lens <note>original: "taking an aim at divine matters by human"</note>, which inevitably breeds a mixture of fantasies; and lastly, uncivilized <note>original: "barbarous"</note> times, especially when joined with calamities and disasters. 

*Superstition*, without a veil, is a deformed thing; for just as it adds to an ape's deformity that it looks so much like a man, so the similarity of *superstition* to *religion* makes it even more hideous. And as wholesome meat rots into little worms, so good forms and orders corrupt into a number of petty rituals <note>original: "observances"</note>. There is a *superstition* in avoiding *superstition*;

# Of Travel.

## XVIII.

**T**ravel, for younger people, is a part of education; for older people, it is a part of experience. He who travels into a country before he has some basic knowledge <note>original: "entrance"</note> of the language goes to <note>The following italicized words are struck through with ink</note> *school*, and not to *travel*. I highly recommend that young men travel under some tutor or experienced <note>original: "grave"</note> servant, provided that the guide knows the language and has been to the country before. In this way, he may be able to tell them what things are worth seeing in the country where they go, what acquaintances they should seek, and what exercises or discipline the place offers <note>original: "yieldeth"</note>. Otherwise, young men will go blindfolded <note>original: "hooded"</note> and see very little of the world. It is a strange thing that in sea voyages, where there is nothing to

Let diaries, therefore, be brought into use. The things to be seen and observed are: the courts of princes, especially when they give audience to ambassadors; the courts of justice while they sit and hear cases, and likewise ecclesiastical consistories <note>Church courts or administrative councils.</note>; churches and monasteries, with the monuments which are still standing within them; the walls and fortifications of cities and towns, and also the havens and harbors; antiquities and ruins; libraries; colleges, scholarly debates <note>original: "disputations"</note>, and lectures, where any are held; shipping and navies; stately homes and gardens of state and pleasure near great cities; armories; arsenals; magazines <note>Stores for weapons or gunpowder.</note>; exchanges; bourses <note>Places where merchants and traders meet.</note>; warehouses; exercises in horsemanship; fencing; the training of soldiers; and the like; *comedies*—specifically those which the better sort of people attend;

After all of which, the tutors or servants <note>In this period, "servants" often referred to educated attendants, secretaries, or personal assistants rather than just domestic laborers.</note> ought to make diligent inquiry. As for triumphs, masques <note>A form of courtly entertainment involving music, dancing, and acting, often with elaborate costumes.</note>, feasts, weddings, funerals, capital executions, and such spectacles, men do not need to be reminded of them; yet they are not to be neglected. If you want a young man to condense his *travels* <note>original: "Travaile"</note> into a small space and gather much knowledge in a short time, you must do this: First, as was said, he must have some basic knowledge of the language before he goes. # Of Travel

Let him change his lodging from one end and part of the town to another, which is a great magnet <note>original: "Adamant"; a stone or mineral once believed to have powerful attracting properties</note> for making new acquaintances. Let him distance <note>original: "sequester"</note> himself from the company of his own countrymen and eat in such places where there is good company of the people of the nation where he travels. Upon his moves <note>original: "Removes"</note> from one place to another, let him obtain letters of recommendation to some person of high social standing <note>original: "Quality"</note> residing in the place where he is going, so that he may use that person's influence <note>original: "Favor"</note> in those things he desires to see or know. In this way, he may shorten <note>original: "abridge"</note> his *travel* with much profit.

As for the acquaintances which are to be sought while *traveling*, the most profitable of all is acquaintance with the secretaries and staff <note>original: "Employed Men"</note> of ambassadors. For in this way, by *traveling* in one country, he shall absorb <note>original: "suck"</note> the experience of many nations. Let him also see and visit eminent persons of all kinds who are of great renown abroad, so that he may be able to judge how their actual life matches their reputation <note>original: "Fame"</note>. # Of Travel.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;[Quarrels are with] care and discretion to be avoided; they are, commonly, for mistresses <note>original: "Miſtreſſes"; romantic interests or women being courted</note>, drinking toasts <note>original: "Healths"</note>, social standing <note>original: "Place"; precedence in rank</note>, and arguments <note>original: "Words"</note>. And let a man beware how he keeps company with choleric <note>original: "Cholerick"; easily angered or irritable</note> and quarrelsome persons, for they will involve him in their own quarrels. When a traveler returns home, let him not leave the countries where he has traveled altogether behind him; but maintain a correspondence by letters with those of his acquaintance who are of most worth. And let his travel appear rather in his discourse <note>original: "Diſcourſe"; conversation</note> than in his apparel <note>original: "Apparrell"; clothing</note> or gesture. And in his conversation, let him be rather thoughtful <note>original: "aduiſed"; deliberate or cautious</note> in his answers than eager to tell stories. And let it appear that he does not change his home country's manners for those of foreign parts, but only grafts <note>original: "prick in"; a gardening term meaning to plant or transplant</note> some flowers of what he has learned abroad into the customs of his own country.

<summary>In this concluding section of his essay, Francis Bacon advises travelers to avoid petty quarrels, maintain international friendships through letters, and integrate foreign wisdom into their native customs without losing their national identity.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, travel, etiquette, quarrels, correspondence, cultural integration, 17th-century manners</keywords>

# Of Empire.
## XIX.

It is a miserable state of mind to have few things to desire and many things to fear. And yet that is commonly the case for kings; who, being at the highest point of power, lack <note>original: "want" meaning to lack or be without</note> objects of desire, which makes their minds more languid <note>original: "languishing"</note>. At the same time, they face many visions of perils and shadows <note>imagined threats</note>, which makes their minds less clear. 

This is also one reason for that effect which the Scripture speaks of: "The king's heart is inscrutable" <note>Proverbs 25:3</note>. For a multitude of jealousies <note>original: "jealousies" meaning suspicions or anxieties</note>, and the lack of some predominant desire that should marshal <note>organize</note> and put all the others in order, makes any man's heart hard to find or fathom <note>original: "sound," meaning to measure the depth of or fully understand</note>. Hence it comes likewise, that

<summary>In this opening of his essay on empire, Francis Bacon explores the psychological state of monarchs, arguing that their absolute power leads to a listless mind plagued by suspicion and anxiety.</summary>
<keywords>monarchy, psychology of power, desire, fear, Francis Bacon, Proverbs 25:3, kingship</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Empire," discussing the psychological tendencies and personal diversions of monarchs who have reached the limits of their power.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

-> ### Of Empire <-

Princes often create desires for themselves and set their hearts upon toys <note>original: "toyes"; here meaning trivialities or frivolous hobbies</note>: sometimes on a building; sometimes on establishing a new order; sometimes on promoting a specific person; sometimes on achieving excellence in some art or physical skill. Examples include *Nero* for playing the harp, *Domitian* for his accuracy with the bow and arrow, *Commodus* for his skill in fencing <note>original: "playing at Fence"; the sport of swordsmanship</note>, *Caracalla* for driving chariots, and the like. 

This seems incredible to those who do not understand the principle: *that the mind of man is more cheered and refreshed by making progress in small things than by standing still in great ones.* 

We also see that kings who have been fortunate conquerors in their early years—since it is impossible for them to advance forever without eventually facing a setback or a halt <note>original: "Check or Arrest"</note> in their fortunes—often turn to superstition and melancholy in their later years. This was the case with *Alexander the Great*, *Diocletian*, and in our own memory, *Charles V* <note>Charles V (1500–1558), Holy Roman Emperor, who famously abdicated his throne to live in a monastery</note>, and others.

<summary>Bacon explains that monarchs often turn to trivial hobbies or fall into melancholy and superstition when they can no longer make progress in grander political or military ambitions.</summary>

<keywords>monarchy, psychology of power, Nero, Domitian, Commodus, Caracalla, Alexander the Great, Diocletian, Charles V, melancholy, ambition</keywords>

For he who is used to moving forward and finds a stop, falls out of favor with himself and is no longer the man he <unclear>was</unclear>.

To speak now of the true balance <note>original: "Temper," referring to a proper mixture of qualities</note> of *Empire*: it is a thing rare and hard to maintain, for both balance and imbalance consist of opposites. But it is one thing to mingle opposites and another to alternate <note>original: "enterchange"</note> between them. The answer of *Apollonius* to *Vespasian* is full of excellent instruction. *Vespasian* asked him, "*What was Nero’s overthrow?*" He answered, "*Nero could touch and tune the harp well; but in government, he sometimes used to wind the pins too high, and sometimes to let them down too low.*" <note>Apollonius of Tyana was a Greek philosopher; Vespasian and Nero were Roman Emperors. The metaphor refers to the tuning pegs of a stringed instrument.</note> And it is certain that nothing destroys authority so much as the unequal and untimely alternation of power being pressed too far and then *relaxed* too much.

This is true: that the wisdom of these recent times in the *affairs of princes* consists more of clever escapes <note>original: "fine Deliveries"</note> and the shifting away of dangers and mischiefs when they are close at hand, rather than solid and well-grounded courses of action to keep those dangers at a distance. But this is only to

# Of Empire.

...try to master Fortune. And let men beware of neglecting or allowing the causes of trouble to build up; for no man can prevent a spark, nor tell from where it might come. The difficulties in the *affairs of Princes* are many and great, but the greatest difficulty is often in their own minds. For it is common for *Princes* (as *Tacitus* <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–c. 120 AD), a Roman historian and politician noted for his analysis of power.</note> says) to desire contradictory things: <term>The wills of kings are usually intense and inconsistent with one another.</term> <note>original Latin: "Sunt plerumque Regum voluntates vehementes, & inter se contrariae."</note> For it is the <term>solecism of power</term> <note>original: "Soloecisme of Power"; in this context, a fundamental error in logic or "bad grammar" in the exercise of authority.</note> to think one can command the end result and yet not tolerate the necessary means.

Kings must deal with their *neighbors*, their *wives*, their *children*, their *prelates* (or *clergy*), their *nobles*, their *lesser-nobles* (or *gentlemen*), their *merchants*, their *common people*, and their *soldiers*. From all of these, dangers arise if care and caution are not used.

First, concerning their *neighbors*: no general rule can be given because circumstances are so variable, except for one that always holds true: that *Princes* do...

# Of Empire.

...keep a constant watch <note>original: "due Centinell"</note> so that none of their *neighbors* grow so powerful (through the increase of territory, the taking over of trade, the advancement of their borders, or similar means) that they become more able to cause harm <note>original: "annoy"</note> than they were before. Generally, it is the task of permanent advisory councils to foresee and prevent this. 

During the time of that *Triumvirate* <note>A group of three powerful leaders holding power together.</note> of kings—*King Henry VIII* of *England*, *Francis I*, *King* of *France*, and *Emperor Charles V*—they kept such a close watch that none of the three could gain even a palm's breadth of ground without the other two immediately balancing the scale, either through an alliance <note>original: "Confederation"</note> or, if necessary, by war. They would not, in any way, "buy" peace at the cost of future security <note>original: "take up Peace at Interest," meaning to accept a temporary peace that would lead to greater danger or "debt" later.</note>. 

A similar strategy was used by that League (which Guicciardini <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), a famous Italian historian and statesman.</note> says was the security of *Italy*) formed between *Ferdinand, King* of *Naples*, *Lorenzo de' Medici*, and *Ludovico Sforza*—the rulers of *Florence* and *Milan* respectively. Nor should the opinion of some of the Scholastics <note>original: "Schoole-Men," referring to medieval theologians and philosophers who debated the ethics of war.</note> be accepted: that a war cannot justly be

As for their *wives*, there are cruel examples of them. *Livia* is infamous <note>Livia Drusilla, wife of the Roman Emperor Augustus, was rumored by ancient historians like Tacitus to have poisoned her husband to clear the path for her son Tiberius.</note> for the poisoning of her husband. *Roxolana*, the wife of *Suleiman* <note>Suleiman the Magnificent, Ottoman Sultan (reigned 1520–1566).</note>, was the cause of the destruction of that renowned prince, *Sultan Mustapha*, and otherwise brought trouble to his house and succession. The Queen of *Edward II* of England <note>Isabella of France, known as the "She-Wolf of France," who led an invasion against her husband.</note> played the principal part in the deposition and murder of her husband. This kind of danger is to be feared especially when *wives* have plots to advance their own children, or else if they are adulteresses <note>original: "aduoutresses"</note>.

As for their *children*, the tragedies involving dangers from them have likewise been many. In general, it has always been unfortunate when fathers begin to suspect their *children*. The destruction of *Mustapha* (whom we mentioned before) was so fatal to *Suleiman’s* line, as

# Of Empire.

The succession of the Turks, from *Solyman* <note>Suleiman the Magnificent (r. 1520–1566)</note> until this day, is suspected to be illegitimate and of foreign blood; for *Selimus* [Selim] II was thought to be supposititious <term>supposititious: substituted at birth, or illegitimate</term>. The destruction of *Crispus*, a young prince of rare potential <note>original: "towardness"</note>, by his father *Constantinus* [Constantine] the Great, was similarly fatal to his house. For both *Constantinus* and *Constans*, his sons, died violent deaths; and *Constantius*, his other son, fared little better, for although he died of illness, it was only after *Julianus* [Julian the Apostate] had taken up arms against him. The destruction of *Demetrius*, son to *Philip* II of Macedon, turned against the father, who died of remorse <note>original: "repentance"</note>. 

And there are many similar examples, but few or none where fathers benefited from such distrust—except in cases where the sons were in open rebellion against them, as *Selim* I was against *Bayezid*, and as were the three sons of *Henry* II, King of England.

As for their *prelates* <term>prelates: high-ranking members of the clergy, such as bishops</term>: when they are proud and powerful, they also pose a danger;

<summary>Bacon argues that monarchs who distrust or destroy their own heirs often invite disaster upon their dynasties, citing Roman, Ottoman, and English history as evidence. He also begins to address the political threat posed by powerful church leaders.</summary>

<page-num>112</page-num>

-># Of Empire.<-

As it was in the times of *Anselm* and *Thomas Becket*, Archbishops of *Canterbury*, who with their *crosiers* <note>a hooked staff carried by a bishop as a symbol of pastoral office</note> almost contended with the *king’s sword*. And yet they had to deal with strong and proud kings: *William Rufus*, *Henry I*, and *Henry II*. The danger <note>the danger the clergy poses to the sovereign</note> is not from that estate itself, but where it is dependent on a foreign authority <note>referring to the Pope in Rome</note>, or where the *churchmen* are appointed and elected not by the *nomination* <note>original: "collation," the legal right of a patron or bishop to appoint a member of the clergy to a position</note> of the king or specific *patrons*, but by the *people*.

As for the *nobles*: keeping them at a distance is not a bad idea. However, to weaken them may make a king more absolute but less safe, and less able to accomplish anything he desires. I have noted this in my history of *King Henry VII* of *England*, who weakened his *nobility*. Consequently, it happened that his reign was full of difficulties and troubles; for the *nobility*, though they remained loyal to him, did not cooperate with him in his affairs of state.

<meta>catchword: So</meta>

<summary>Bacon examines the potential threats to royal power from the clergy (especially when influenced by foreign powers) and the nobility, arguing that while kings should control the nobles, weakening them too much undermines the king's own security and effectiveness.</summary>

# Of Empire.

So that, in effect, he was forced <note>original: "faine"</note> to do everything himself.

Regarding their **second-tier nobility** <note>original: "Second Nobles"; the lesser gentry or lower-ranking aristocrats</note>: there is not much danger from them, as they are a dispersed group. They may sometimes speak ambitiously, but that does little harm. Besides, they act as a counterbalance to the **higher nobility**, preventing them from becoming too powerful. Finally, because they hold the most immediate authority over the **common people**, they are best suited to calm popular unrest.

Regarding their **merchants**: they are the **portal vein** <note>Latin: *vena porta*. In the medical theories of the time, this vein was believed to distribute nourishment throughout the body.</note>. If they do not flourish, a kingdom may have strong limbs but will have empty veins and receive little nourishment. Taxes and duties imposed upon them seldom benefit the **king’s revenue**; for what the king gains in the local district <note>original: "the Hundred," an administrative subdivision of a county</note>, he loses in the county <note>original: "the Shire"</note> at large. While specific tax rates may be increased, the total volume of trade actually decreases.

*Princes* are like *heavenly bodies* <note>stars or planets</note>, which cause good or bad times, and which receive much veneration <note>great respect or worship</note> but have no rest. All instructions concerning kings are essentially contained in these two reminders: *Remember that you are a man* <note>original: "Memento quod es Homo"</note> and *Remember that you are a god* <note>original: "Memento quod es Deus"</note>, or *God's deputy* <note>original: "Vice Dei"</note>. The first restrains their power, and the second restrains their will.

Of Counsel.

# Of Counsel.

## XX.

The greatest trust between man and man is the trust of *giving counsel*. In other types of confidence, people entrust specific parts of their lives: their lands, their goods, their children, their reputation, or some particular affair. But to those whom they make their *counselors*, they entrust everything. For this reason, counselors are all the more obligated to act with total faithfulness and integrity. The wisest *rulers* <note>original: "Princes"</note> need not think it any decrease to their greatness, or a slight to their own competence, to rely upon *counsel*. God himself is not without it; He has made it one of the great names of His blessed Son: *The Counselor*. *Solomon* <note>original: "Salomon"</note> has declared that *in counsel there is stability*. All matters will undergo a first or second *discussion* <note>original: "agitation"</note>; if they are not tossed upon the

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>Of Counsel.</header>

# Of Counsel.

The affairs of *counsel* <note>original: "Arguments of Counsell"</note> will be tossed upon the waves of *Fortune*; and they will be full of inconstancy, doing and undoing, like the reeling of a drunken man. *Solomon's* son <note>A reference to Rehoboam, whose decision to follow the advice of his young companions over his father’s elder advisors led to a civil war and the division of the Kingdom of Israel.</note> felt the force of *counsel*, just as his father saw the necessity of it. For the beloved Kingdom of God was first torn apart and broken by ill *counsel*. Regarding that counsel, two marks are set for our instruction by which *bad counsel* is forever best recognized: first, that it *was young counsel* in terms of the people who gave it; and second, that it was *violent counsel* in terms of the subject matter.

Ancient times illustrate through allegory <note>original: "set forth in Figure"</note> both the integration and the inseparable connection of *counsel* with *kings*, and the wise and political use of *counsel* by *kings*. The first is shown by the myth that *Jupiter* married *Metis* <note>The Greek goddess of wisdom and craft.</note>, which signifies *counsel*. By this, the ancients intended to show that *sovereignty* is married to *counsel*. The second is shown in what follows: they say that after *Jupiter* was married to *Metis*, she conceived a child by him and was pregnant; but *Jupiter* did not allow her to remain <note>original: "stay"; meaning to remain until the birth.</note>

<summary>Bacon warns against the instability of poor advice, citing the biblical example of Rehoboam, and uses the myth of Jupiter and Metis to argue that wise counsel is an essential partner to sovereign power.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mythological origins of political counsel, specifically the Greek myth where Jupiter (Zeus) swallows his wife Metis (Counsel/Wisdom).</meta>

...until she brought forth, but ate her up; whereby he became himself pregnant and was delivered of *Pallas Armed* <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and war, who in mythology sprang fully formed and armored from the forehead of Zeus (Jupiter).</note> out of his head. This monstrous fable contains a secret of *Empire*: how kings are to make use of their *Council of State*. 

First, they ought to refer matters unto them, which is the first "begetting" or "impregnation." But when these matters are elaborated, molded, and shaped in the womb of their *Council*, and grow ripe and ready to be brought forth, the sovereign should then not suffer their *Council* to go through with the resolution and direction as if it depended on them. Instead, they should take the matter back into their own hands and make it appear to the world that the decrees and final directions—which, because they come forth with *Prudence* and *Power*, are likened to *Pallas Armed*—proceeded from themselves. They should show that these come not only from their *Authority* but (to add further to their own reputation) from their own head and *ingenuity* <note>original: "Device"</note>.

Let us now speak of the *inconveniences* <note>Here meaning the disadvantages or drawbacks of using a council.</note>...

<summary>Bacon uses the myth of Jupiter swallowing Metis to argue that while rulers should use councils to develop ideas, they must claim the final decision as their own to maintain authority and a reputation for wisdom.</summary>

# Of Counsel.

of counsel, and of the remedies. The *inconveniences* that have been noted in calling and using counsel are three. First, the *revealing of affairs*, whereby they become less *secret*. Secondly, the *weakening of the authority of princes*, as if they were less capable on *their own* <note>original: "of Themselues"</note>. Thirdly, the *danger of being unfaithfully counseled*, and more for the good of those who *counsel* than for him who is *counseled*. For which *inconveniences*, the *doctrine of Italy* and the *practice of France*, during the reigns of some kings, has introduced *cabinet counsels* <term>Small, private groups of advisors meeting in a "cabinet" or private room, rather than the official Great Council</term>; a *remedy* worse than the *disease*.

As to *secrecy*; *princes* are not bound to communicate all matters with all *counselors*, but may extract and select. Neither is it necessary that he who consults about what he should do should declare what he will do. But let *princes* beware that the *leakage* <note>original: "unsecreting"</note> of their affairs does not come from themselves. And as for *cabinet counsels*, this may be their *motto*: *I am full of leaks* <note>original Latin: "Plenus rimarum sum," a quote from the Roman playwright Terence meaning "I am full of holes"</note>. It is true that there are some **affairs** which require extreme **secrecy**, which will rarely be shared with more than one or two people besides the **King**. This was the case with King **Henry VII** of **England**, who, in his most important business, shared his thoughts with no one except **Morton** and **Fox** <note>John Morton (c. 1420–1500) and Richard Foxe (c. 1448–1528) were bishops and the King's closest, most loyal advisors</note>.

## Of Counsel.

...either an over-greatness in one counselor, or an overly strict combination in several; these are things soon found and remedied <note>original: "holpen"</note>.

For the last *inconvenience*—that men will *give counsel with an eye to their own interests*—certainly, the phrase *He shall not find faith upon the earth* <note>original Latin: "Non inveniet Fidem super terram," a reference to Luke 18:8</note> refers to the general nature of the times and not to every individual person. Let princes, above all, attract such personalities to themselves. 

Besides, counselors are not usually so united that one counselor does not keep watch <note>original: "keepeth Centinell"</note> over another. Therefore, if any give counsel out of faction or for private ends, it commonly reaches the King's ear. But the best *remedy* is for princes to know their counselors as well as their counselors know them:

# Of Counsel.

The qualification of a **counselor** is that they should be skillful in their master's business rather than in his nature; for then he is likely to advise him and not just feed his whims <note>original: "Humour" — referring to a person’s particular temperament, moods, or biases</note>. It is of singular use to **Princes** if they take the opinions of their **Council** <note>original: "Counsell"</note> both separately and together. For private opinion is more free, but opinion given before others is more formal and respectful <note>original: "Reuerend"</note>. In private, men are bolder in following their own inclinations; and in a group <note>original: "in Consort"</note>, men are more subject <note>original: "obnoxious" — from the Latin 'obnoxius', meaning liable or subservient to the influence of others</note> to the whims of others. Therefore, it is good to take both: and from those of lower rank, it is better to seek advice in private to preserve freedom of speech; from those of higher rank, it is better in a group to preserve proper respect. 

It is in vain for **Princes** to take **counsel** concerning **matters** of state if they do not also take **counsel** concerning **people**. For all **matters** are like dead images, and the life of the execution of affairs depends on the good choice of **people**. Nor is it enough to consult concerning **people** "according to their general types" <note>original: "Secundum genera"</note>, as if in an abstract **idea** or a **mathematical description**, regarding what kind and—

<summary>Bacon discusses the practicalities of governance, advising rulers to seek counsel both privately and collectively while emphasizing that the success of any policy depends entirely on choosing the right individuals to execute it.</summary>

# Of Counsel

The character of the *person* should be considered; for the greatest errors are committed, and the most judgment is shown, in the choice of *individuals*. It was truly said, "*Optimi Consiliarii mortui*" <note>Latin: "The best counselors are the dead"</note>; books will speak plainly when counselors flinch <note>original: "blanch," meaning to gloss over the truth or flatter to avoid giving offense</note>. Therefore, it is good to be well-versed in them, especially the books of those who themselves have been actors upon the stage <note>meaning those who have personally participated in the historical or political events they write about</note>.

The *councils* nowadays, in most places, are but informal meetings where matters are talked about rather than debated. And they rush too quickly to the formal order or act of *council*. It would be better if, in matters of weight, the subject were proposed one day and not discussed until the next: "*In Nocte Consilium*" <note>Latin: "In the night is counsel," or "Sleep on it"</note>. This was the practice in the Commission of *Union* between *England* and *Scotland*, which was a solemn and orderly assembly. I recommend set days for petitions; for this both gives the suitors more certainty for their attendance and it frees the meetings for matters of state, so that they may *Hoc agere* <note>Latin: "Do this," meaning to focus exclusively on the business at hand</note>. In the choice of committees for—

For the *Council*, it is better to choose impartial <note>original: "Indifferent"</note> persons, rather than to create an appearance of impartiality by including those who are strongly biased on both sides. I also recommend *standing commissions*; such as for trade, for the treasury <note>original: "Treasure"</note>, for war, for legal petitions <note>original: "Suits"</note>, and for specific provinces. For where there are several specific *councils* but only one *Council of State* (as is the case in *Spain*), they are essentially nothing more than *standing commissions*, except that they possess greater authority. 

Let those who are to inform *councils* based on their specific professions—such as lawyers, sailors, mint officials <note>original: "Mint-men"</note>, and the like—be heard first before *committees*, and then, as the occasion requires, before the *Council*. For that is to harass <note>original: "clamour"</note> councils, not to inform them.

# Of Delays.
## XXI.

Fortune is like the *market*; where many times, if you can wait a little, the price will fall. And again, it is sometimes like *Sibyl's offer*; <note>original: "Sybilla's Offer"; refers to the Cumaean Sibyl who offered nine prophetic books to the King of Rome. When he refused the price, she burned three, then three more, demanding the same price for the remaining three.</note> which at first offers the goods in full, then destroys them piece by piece, yet still keeps the price high. For *Opportunity* <note>original: "Occasion"</note> (as the common verse says) *turns a bald head after she has presented her locks in front and no one has grasped them*; <note>Refers to the personification of Opportunity as having a long forelock to be seized as she approaches, but being bald at the back of the head so she cannot be caught once she has passed by.</note> or at least, she turns the handle of the bottle first to be received, and then the belly, which is hard to grip. There is surely no greater wisdom than to well-time the beginnings and onsets of things. Dangers are no longer minor <note>original: "light"</note> if they once seem minor; and more dangers have deceived men than have overcome them by force. Indeed, it would be better to meet some dangers halfway, even if they have not yet come near,

<sig>R 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: near,</meta>

<summary>In this opening to his essay on delays, Francis Bacon argues that timing is the essence of wisdom, using metaphors to show how opportunities must be seized before they vanish or become harder to grasp.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Delays, Opportunity, Fortune, Sibylline Books, Timing, Wisdom, Strategy</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the timing of actions, specifically addressing the balance between waiting for the right moment and acting with necessary speed.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

# Of Delays

...near, than to keep too long a watch upon their approaches; for if a man watches too long, it is likely he will fall asleep. On the other hand, to be deceived by shadows that appear too long (as some have been when the moon was low and shone on their enemies' backs), and so to shoot before the right time; or to encourage dangers to approach by preparing <note>original: "buckling"</note> for them too early, is another extreme. The ripeness or unripeness of the occasion (as we said) must always be well weighed. Generally, it is good to entrust the beginnings of all great actions to *Argos* <note>In Greek mythology, a giant with a hundred eyes, symbolizing constant vigilance and observation.</note> with his hundred eyes, and the endings to *Briareus* <note>A hundred-handed giant from Greek mythology, representing great power and swift, multifaceted action.</note> with his hundred hands: first to watch, and then to act with speed. For the *Helmet of Pluto* <note>A mythical helmet that rendered the wearer invisible; Bacon uses it as a metaphor for strategic concealment.</note>, which makes the political man go invisible, is *secrecy* in counsel and *celerity* <note>swiftness of movement or action</note> in execution. For once things have reached the stage of execution, no secrecy compares to speed; it is like the motion of a bullet in the air, which flies so fast that it outruns the eye.

<summary>Bacon argues that successful strategy requires a balance between patient vigilance and rapid execution, using the mythological figures of Argos and Briareus to illustrate that one must watch carefully before acting decisively.</summary>

# Of Cunning.

## XXII.

We define *cunning* as a sinister or crooked wisdom. And certainly, there is a great difference between a *cunning* man and a *wise* man, not only in terms of honesty but also in terms of ability. There are those who can "pack the cards" <note>To "pack the cards" meant to shuffle or arrange a deck unfairly in order to cheat.</note> and yet cannot play the game well; similarly, there are some who are skilled in political maneuvering <note>original: "Canvasses"</note> and factions who are otherwise weak men. 

Again, it is one thing to understand people and another thing to understand the matters at hand; for many are experts in men's moods <note>original: "Humours"; the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—once thought to determine a person's temperament and health.</note> who are not particularly capable in the practical side of business. This is the temperament of one who has studied men more than books. Such men are better suited for scheming <note>original: "Practise"</note> than for giving counsel, and they are only effective in their own familiar circles <note>original: "Alley"</note>. Turn them to new [matters...]

<summary>In this opening to his essay, Francis Bacon distinguishes between "cunning" (a dishonest, narrow cleverness) and true "wisdom," noting that some men excel at manipulating people without understanding broader business or policy.</summary>

# Of Cunning.
---

...men, and they have lost their aim. Thus, the old rule to distinguish a fool from a wise man—*Send them both naked to strangers, and you will see* <note>original Latin: *Mitte ambos nudos ad ignotos, & videbis*</note>—hardly applies to them. And because these *cunning men* are like *haberdashers* <note>original: *Haberdashers* of *Small Wares*; retailers of small items like ribbons and thread. Bacon uses this metaphor to describe people who deal in petty, specialized tricks rather than broad wisdom.</note> of *small wares*, it is not out of place to reveal the contents of their shop.

It is a technique of *cunning* to watch the eyes of the person to whom you speak, as the *Jesuits* <note>A Roman Catholic religious order; in Bacon’s time, they were frequently cited for their training in diplomacy, psychology, and persuasion.</note> teach in their rules. This is because there are many wise men who have secret hearts but transparent faces. However, this should be done with a modest lowering of your own eyes at times, as the *Jesuits* also practice.

Another trick is that when you have something that needs to be approved or finished quickly, you should entertain and distract the person with whom you are dealing with some other conversation. This ensures they are not too alert to raise objections. I knew a *counselor* and *secretary* who never approached *Queen Elizabeth I of England* with *bills* <note>Official documents or legal acts requiring a royal signature.</note> to sign without first drawing her into some discussion of *state affairs* <note>original: *Estate*</note>, so that she might...

# Of Cunning

...they pay less attention to the documents. <note>This sentence fragment completes a thought from the previous page regarding distracting an official so they sign papers without reading them closely.</note>

A similar surprise may be achieved by raising matters when the other party is in a hurry and cannot stay to consider what is being proposed carefully.

If a man wants to thwart a piece of business that he suspects someone else will propose skillfully and effectively, let him pretend to favor it and propose it himself, but in such a way that it is likely to fail.

Breaking off in the middle of what one was about to say, as if catching oneself, creates a greater desire in the person you are talking with to know more.

And because it is more effective when information seems to be drawn from you by a question rather than offered freely, you may set a trap for a question by showing a different facial expression and demeanor than usual. This is done to give the other person a reason to ask what is causing the change—just as Nehemiah <note>Original: Nehemias. This refers to the biblical cupbearer to the Persian King Artaxerxes, who used his sad expression to prompt the King to ask what was wrong, leading to the rebuilding of Jerusalem.</note> did: *“I had not been sad in his presence before.”*

# Of Cunning

In matters that are delicate or unpleasant, it is wise to "break the ice" through someone whose words carry less weight. This allows the more influential person to hold back and join the conversation as if by chance, so that he may be asked a question based on what the first person said. *Narcissus* <note>A powerful freedman and advisor to the Roman Emperor Claudius.</note> followed this method when reporting the marriage of *Messalina* <note>The wife of Claudius, who supposedly entered a bigamous marriage with Silius to overthrow the Emperor.</note> and *Silius* to *Claudius*.

In things that a man would not want to be seen in himself, it is a tactic of cunning to use the name of the public. One might say, "*The world says*," or "*There is a rumor going around*."

I knew one man who, when he wrote a letter, would put the most important information in the *postscript*, as if it were merely an incidental matter <note>original: "By-matter"</note>.

I knew another who, when he held a conversation, would skip over the thing he intended most. He would start to leave and then come back again and speak of it as if it were something he had almost forgotten.

Some people arrange for themselves to be "surprised" or caught off guard at times when it is likely the party they are trying to influence will suddenly come **upon** them...

<summary>Bacon describes various deceptive tactics used in social and political life, such as using intermediaries to deliver bad news or framing important requests as casual afterthoughts.</summary>

It is a point of *cunning* to speak certain words in one's own name, which he actually wants another man to learn and repeat, so that he can then take advantage of that person's repetition. I knew two men who were *competitors* for the Secretary’s position <note>The office of Secretary of State was a powerful political role in the Tudor and Stuart courts.</note> in *Queen Elizabeth’s* time, and yet they remained on good terms <note>original: "kept good quarter"</note> with each other and would confer together upon the business. Some have so many tales and stories ready at hand that there is nothing they wish to hint at but they can wrap it into a story. This serves both to keep themselves more guarded and to make others repeat the message with more pleasure.

# Of Wisdom
## for a Man's Self.
### XXIII.

An *ant* is a *wise creature* for *itself*; but it is a *mischievous thing* <note>original: "shrewd," which in this context meant harmful or troublesome rather than clever</note> in an *orchard* or *garden*. And certainly, men who are great *lovers of themselves* waste the *public good*. *Divide* with reason between *self-love* and *society*; and be so true to *yourself* that you are not false to *others*, especially to your *king* and *country*. *Himself* is a *poor center* for a man's actions. It is exactly like the *Earth*. For the Earth alone stands firm upon its own *center*, whereas all *things* that have *affinity* with the *heavens* move upon the *center* of another, which they benefit <note>Bacon employs the Ptolemaic astronomical model as a metaphor: the Earth stays still and self-involved at the center of the universe, while the "heavenly" bodies revolve in orbits that serve the whole</note>. The *referring* of everything to *one's self* is more tolerable in a *sovereign*

# Of Innovations.

## XXIV.

As the births of living creatures are at first ill-shaped, so are all *innovations*, which are the births of time. Yet notwithstanding, just as those who first bring honor into their family are commonly more worthy than most who succeed them, so the first precedent <note>original: "President"; here meaning a legal or historical example to be followed</note> (if it be good) is seldom equaled by imitation. For evil, to man's nature as it stands perverted, has a natural motion that is strongest as it continues; but good is like a forced motion, which is strongest at the very beginning. Surely every *medicine* is an *innovation*; and he that will not apply new remedies must expect new evils. For time is the greatest *innovator*; and if time, in its course, alters things for the worse, and wisdom...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence as the argument carries over to the next page.</note>

<summary>In this opening to his essay on change, Francis Bacon argues that while new ideas may initially seem crude, they are as necessary as medicine because time itself constantly creates new problems that require new solutions.</summary>

and counsel shall not alter them for the better, what shall be the end? It is true that what is settled by custom, even if it is not good, is at least fit <note>In this context, "fit" means well-suited to the current environment or familiar.</note>. Those things which have long existed together are, in a sense, confederate <note>original: "confederate within themselves," meaning they have adapted to work together smoothly over time.</note> with one another. In contrast, new things do not fit <note>original: "peece"</note> together as well; for though they help through their utility, they cause trouble through their lack of conformity. Besides, they are like *strangers*—more admired, but less favored. 

All this would be true if time stood still; but on the contrary, time moves so steadily <note>original: "round"</note> that a stubborn <note>original: "froward"</note> retention of custom is just as turbulent a thing as an *innovation*. Those who reverence old times too much are merely an object of scorn to the new. It would be good, therefore, if men in their *innovations* would follow the example of time itself, which indeed *innovates* greatly, but quietly and by degrees that are scarcely perceived. 

Otherwise, whatever is new is unexpected; and it always improves <note>original: "mends"</note> some and impairs <note>original: "paires"</note> others. He who is helped considers it a stroke of luck and thanks...

# Of Dispatch.
## XXV.

**Affected** *dispatch* <note>original: "Dispatch"; meaning the prompt and efficient completion of a task</note> is one of the most dangerous things for *business* that can be. It is like what *physicians* call *predigestion* or *hasty digestion*, which is sure to fill the body full of *undigested matter* <note>original: "crudities"</note> and the secret seeds of *diseases*. Therefore, do not measure *dispatch* by the amount of time spent sitting <note>referring to time spent in meetings or sessions</note>, but by the *advancement* of the *business*. And as in *races*, it is not the large *stride* or the *high lift* of the feet that creates *speed*, so in *business*, staying focused on the subject and not trying to handle too much at once provides *dispatch*. It is the only concern of some people to finish quickly for the moment, or to devise some false *conclusions* <note>original: "periods"</note> to the *business* so that they may seem like *men of dispatch*. But it is one thing to abbre-

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that true efficiency (dispatch) in business is measured by actual progress rather than speed or long meetings, warning that rushed work leads to future problems.</summary>
<keywords>dispatch, business efficiency, Francis Bacon, productivity, decision making, management</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concludes with the beginning of the word "obviate."</meta>

# Of Dispatch.

...viate <note>The text continues from the previous page, completing the word "obviate," meaning to anticipate or prevent.</note> by contracting, another by cutting off; and business so handled at several sittings or meetings usually goes backward and forward in an unsteady manner. I knew a wise man <note>Likely a reference to Sir Amias Paulet, a diplomat under whom Bacon served.</note> who had a favorite saying when he saw men hasten to a conclusion: *“Stay a little, so that we may finish the sooner.”*

On the other hand, *true dispatch* <note>meaning: promptness and efficiency in conducting business</note> is a valuable thing. For time is the measure of business, just as money is the measure of goods; and business is bought at a high price where there is little *dispatch*. The *Spartans* and *Spaniards* have been noted for their lack of *dispatch*: *May my death come from Spain* <note>original: "Mi venga la muerte de Spagna"</note>; for then it will be sure to be a long time in coming.

Give a good hearing to those who provide the first information in *business*. Rather direct them at the beginning than interrupt them during the course of their speeches; for he who is put out of his own order will go backward and forward, and be more tedious while he waits...

upon his memory, than he could have been if he had gone on in his own course. But sometimes it is seen that the moderator <note>original: "Moderator"; the person presiding over a meeting or debate</note> is more troublesome than the actor <note>original: "Actor"; the person presenting the case or argument</note>.

*Repetitions* <note>original: "Iterations"</note> are commonly a loss of time; but there is no such gain of time as to repeat often the *state of the question* <note>the core issue being debated</note>. For it chases away many a frivolous speech as it is being delivered. Long and overly detailed <note>original: "curious"</note> speeches are as fit for *dispatch* <note>the prompt and efficient completion of business</note> as a robe or mantle with a long train is for a race. Prefaces, transitions <note>original: "passages"</note>, apologies <note>original: "excusations"</note>, and other speeches referring to the speaker's own person are great wastes of time; and though they seem to proceed from modesty, they are actually a form of showing off <note>original: "bravery"</note>. 

Yet beware of being too direct <note>original: "material"</note> when there is any obstacle or resistance in people's wills; for a preoccupied mind always requires an introductory speech, like a warm compress <note>original: "fomentation"</note> applied to make the medicinal ointment <note>original: "unguent"</note> soak in. Above all things, *order*, *logical arrangement* <note>original: "distribution"</note>, and *isolating* <note>original: "singling out"</note> specific parts is the life of *dispatch*; provided that the arrangement is not too...

<summary>Bacon argues that true efficiency in business (dispatch) comes from focusing on the core issue and using logical structure, rather than long-winded introductions or excessive detail.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s essay "Of Dispatch," which discusses the efficient management and completion of business affairs.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>

-> # Of *Dispatch.* <-

...too subtle: For he who does not divide <note>Bacon refers to "dividing" or breaking a subject into its component parts.</note> will never enter well into business; and he who divides too much will never come out of it clearly. To choose the right time is to save time; and an unseasonable motion <note>original: "unseasonable motion," meaning a proposal made at the wrong time</note> is nothing but beating the air. There are three parts of business: the *preparation*, the *debate* or *examination*, and the *perfection* <note>original: "perfection," here meaning the final execution or completion of a task</note>. Regarding these, if you look for *dispatch* <note>original: "dispatch," meaning the quick and efficient completion of a task</note>, let only the middle part be the work of many, and the first and last parts be the work of a few. Proceeding based on something conceived in writing usually facilitates *dispatch*; for even if it should be completely rejected, that negative result provides more direction than a vague <note>original: "indefinite"</note> starting point—just as ashes are more fertile <note>original: "generative"</note> than dust.

# Of Seeming Wise.
## XXVI.

IT has been an opinion that the *French* are wiser than they seem, and the *Spaniards* seem wiser than they are. However it may be between nations, certainly it is so between man and man. For as the *Apostle* <note>Referring to St. Paul in 2 Timothy 3:5.</note> says of *godliness*: "*Having a show of godliness, but denying the power thereof*"; so certainly, there are in matters of *wisdom* and ability those who do *nothing* or *little*, yet do it very solemnly: *Trifles produced with great effort* <note>original Latin: "Magno conatu nugas"</note>. It is a ridiculous thing, and fit for a *satire*, to people of judgment, to see what shifts these *formalists* <term>those who focus on outward forms and etiquette rather than actual substance</term> have, and what *perspectives* <term>optical illusions or tricks of sight used to create a false sense of depth</term> they use to make a *surface* appear as a *solid body* that has *depth* and *bulk*. Some are so *closed* and *reserved* that they will not show

<summary>Francis Bacon begins his essay on the difference between true wisdom and the mere appearance of it, critiquing "formalists" who use theatrical behavior to hide their lack of substance.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Essays, wisdom, reputation, French, Spaniards, formalism, hypocrisy, social performance</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Seeming Wise," in which Francis Bacon describes the various affectations people use to appear more knowledgeable or important than they actually are.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>Of Seeming Wise.</header>

...their wares, but by a dim light <note>original: "darke Light"</note>; and they always seem to keep something back. When they know within themselves that they are speaking about things they do not fully understand, they nevertheless want to seem to others as if they know something they are not at liberty to say. Some help themselves with facial expressions <note>original: "Countenance"</note> and gestures, and act wise through signs. As *Cicero* says of *Piso*, when he answered him, he raised one of his eyebrows up to his forehead and bent the other down to his chin: *You answer, with one eyebrow raised to your forehead and the other lowered to your chin, that cruelty does not please you* <note>original: "Respondes, altero ad Frontem sublato, altero ad Mentum depresso Supercilio; Crudelitatem tibi non placere."</note>. 

Some think to carry it off <note>original: "beare it"</note> by using grand words and being peremptory <note>insisting on immediate attention or obedience</note>. They move forward and assume as accepted <note>original: "take by admittance"</note> things they cannot actually prove. Some, whenever something is beyond their reach, will seem to despise or make light of it, calling it irrelevant <note>original: "Impertinent"</note> or over-refined <note>original: "Curious"</note>; in this way, they want their ignorance to look like sound judgment. Some are never without a distinction <note>original: "difference"</note> to make, and commonly, by distracting people with a subtle point, they gloss over <note>original: "blanch"</note> the main issue.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

--- Page 55 ---
<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>Of Seeming Wise.</header>
<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Seeming Wise," concluding Francis Bacon's critique of people who use superficial behaviors to project an image of wisdom they do not actually possess.</meta>

Of such people, Aulus Gellius <note>A 2nd-century Roman author known for his "Attic Nights"</note> says: "A senseless man, who breaks the importance of matters with trivialities of words" <note>original Latin: *Hominem delirum, qui Verborum Minutijs Rerum frangit Pondera*</note>. Of this kind also, *Plato* in his *Protagoras* introduces *Prodicus* in mockery, and makes him give a speech that consists of nothing but hair-splitting distinctions from beginning to end.

Generally, such men find it easy to take the negative side in all deliberations; they seek to gain a reputation for wisdom by raising objections and predicting difficulties. For when propositions are rejected, that is the end of them; but if they are accepted, it requires more work. This false appearance of wisdom is the ruin of productive business.

To conclude, there is no failing merchant or "inward beggar" <term>a person who is secretly bankrupt or destitute while maintaining a facade of wealth</term> who has as many tricks to uphold the reputation of their wealth as these empty people have to maintain the reputation of their competence. **Seeming wise men** may manage to gain a good reputation, but let no one choose them for employment. Certainly, you would be better off hiring a man who is somewhat blunt or clumsy for business, rather than one who is overly formal.

<summary>Bacon concludes his essay by arguing that those who only "seem" wise are obstacles to progress because they prefer to say "no" rather than do the work required by saying "yes." He advises against employing such formalists, suggesting that even an unpolished person is more useful in business than an empty pretender.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Aulus Gellius, Plato, Prodicus, Seeming Wise, false wisdom, business management, reputation, formality</keywords>

# Of Friendship.

## XXVII.

It would have been difficult for the person who said it to have combined more truth and untruth together in so few words than in that speech: *“Whoever delights in solitude is either a wild beast or a god.”* <note>Bacon is quoting Aristotle’s *Politics*.</note> For it is very true that a natural and secret hatred and aversion <note>original: "Aversation"</note> toward *society* in any man has something of the savage beast about it. But it is completely untrue that such a person should have any of the character of the divine nature—unless it proceeds not from a pleasure in *solitude* itself, but from a love and desire to sequester <note>To isolate or withdraw oneself from others.</note> oneself for a higher conversation <note>In this context, "conversation" refers to a way of life or spiritual/intellectual communion with God or higher truths.</note>. This is the kind of solitude found—though falsely and deceptively <note>original: "fainedly"</note>—among some of the ancient pagans, such as *Epimenides* the *Candian* <note>Epimenides of Crete (Candia), a semi-mythical philosopher and prophet said to have slept for 57 years.</note>, *Numa* <note>Numa Pompilius, the legendary second king of Rome, who claimed to receive divine wisdom from the nymph Egeria in solitude.</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon begins his essay by critiquing Aristotle’s claim that those who seek solitude are either beasts or gods, arguing that while anti-social behavior is beastly, true "divine" solitude is only for spiritual or intellectual purposes.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Friendship, Solitude, Aristotle, Epimenides, Numa Pompilius, Society, Divinity</keywords>

...the Roman <note>Likely referring to Numa Pompilius, the second King of Rome, mentioned on the preceding page</note>, *Empedocles* the *Sicilian*, and *Apollonius* of *Tyana*; and truly and really, in various of the ancient hermits and holy fathers of the church. But little do people perceive what *solitude* is, and how far it extends. For a crowd is not company, and faces are but a gallery of pictures, and talk is but a *tinkling cymbal* <note>original: "Tinckling Cymball"; a biblical reference to 1 Corinthians 13:1, implying noise without meaning</note>, where there is no *love*. The Latin adage addresses this a little: *Magna Civitas, Magna solitudo* <note>original: "Magna Ciuitas, Magna solitudo"; translates to "A great city is a great solitude"</note>. This is because in a great town, friends are scattered, so that there is not that fellowship, for the most part, which is found in smaller neighborhoods. But we may go further and affirm most truly: that it is a pure and miserable *solitude* to lack true *friends*, without which the world is but a wilderness. And even in this sense of solitude, whoever in the frame of their nature and affections is unfit for *friendship* takes after the beast rather than humanity.

But one thing is most remarkable (with which I will conclude this first *fruit of friendship* <note>original: "Fruit of frendship"; here meaning the first primary benefit or result.</note>), which is that this communicating of a man’s self to his *friend* works two contrary effects: for it redoubles *joys* and cuts *griefs* in half. For there is no man who shares his *joys* with his *friend* but that he rejoices the more; and no man who shares his *griefs* with his *friend* but that he grieves the less. 

So it is, in the truth of its operation upon a man's mind, of a similar power to that which *alchemists* <note>original: "Alchymists"</note> attribute to their "stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone, a legendary substance in alchemy thought to be capable of turning base metals into gold and serving as an elixir of life.</note> for a man's body: that it works all contrary effects, but always for the good and benefit of nature. Yet, without calling upon the *alchemists* for help, there is a clear example of this in the ordinary course of nature. For in physical bodies, *union* strengthens and cherishes any natural action, and on the other hand, weakens and dulls any violent impression; and it is exactly the same with minds.

Healthy and sovereign for the *understanding*, just as the first <note>The first benefit mentioned on the previous page was the "ease and discharge of the fullness and swellings of the heart."</note> is for the *emotions* <note>original: "Affections"</note>. For *friendship* indeed makes a fair day in the *emotions*, clearing away storms and tempests; but it brings *daylight* to the *understanding*, leading it out of darkness and the confusion of thoughts. This is not to be understood only as the *faithful counsel* which a person receives from a *friend*; but even before you reach that point, it is certain that whoever has a mind filled with many thoughts finds that his wits and understanding clarify and break through in the process of communicating and talking with *another*. He tosses his thoughts about more easily; he marshals them more orderly; he sees how they look when they are turned into words; finally, he becomes wiser than himself—and more so by one hour’s conversation than by a day’s meditation. It was well said by *Themistocles* to the *King of Persia* that *speech was like cloth of Arras <note>original: "Cloth of Arras" — a rich tapestry woven with complex figures and scenes</note>, opened and spread out, whereby the imagery appears in full detail; whereas in thoughts, they...*

# Of Friendship.

There is as much difference between the *counsel* that a *friend* gives and that which a man gives himself, as there is between the *counsel* of a *friend* and that of a *flatterer*. For there is no such *flatterer* as a man's self; and there is no such remedy against the self-*flattery* of a man's self as the liberty of a *friend*. Counsel is of two sorts: the one concerning *morals* <note>original: "manners"</note>, the other concerning *business*. 

As for the first, the best preservative to keep the mind in health is the faithful admonition of a *friend*. Calling oneself to a strict account is a medicine that is sometimes too piercing and corrosive. Reading good books on *morality* is a little flat and dead. Observing our own faults in others is sometimes improper <note>original: "unproper"</note> for our specific case. But the best prescription <note>original: "receipt"</note>—the best, I say, to work and the best to take—is the admonition of a *friend*. 

It is a strange thing to behold what gross errors and extreme absurdities many people (especially those of the higher classes <note>original: "greater sort"</note>) commit for want of a *friend* to tell them of them, to their great

One is that he shall not be faithfully advised; for it is a rare thing, except it be from a perfect and devoted **friend**, to have counsel given that is not...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dangers of seeking advice from those who do not truly know the person, contrasting it with the holistic understanding provided by a true friend.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
# Of Friendship.

...as will be twisted and distorted toward certain ends held by the person giving it. The other risk is that he will receive advice that is harmful and unsafe (even if well-intended) and mixed partly with mischief and partly with a remedy. It is as if you were to call a physician who is thought to be good for curing the specific disease you complain of, but who is unfamiliar with your body. Therefore, he may put you on the path toward an immediate cure, but ruin your health in some other way—and so cure the disease, but kill the patient.

But a friend, who is fully acquainted with a man's situation <note>original: "estate"</note>, will be careful while advancing any present *business* not to run into other inconveniences. Therefore, do not rely on *scattered advice* <note>original: "scattered counsels"</note>; such advice will distract and mislead rather than settle and direct.

After these two noble *fruits of friendship*—*peace in the emotions* <note>original: "affections"</note> and *support of the judgment*—comes the final fruit. This one is like the *pomegranate*, full of many kernels; I mean *aid* and *sharing a part* in...

Men have their allotted *time* and often die while still desiring certain *things* which they deeply take to *heart*—the *settling* <note>original: "bestowing"; likely referring to arranging a child's marriage or future</note> of a *child*, the *finishing* of a *work*, or the like. A man has a *body*, and that *body* is confined to a *place*; but where *friendship* exists, all the *duties* <note>original: "offices"</note> of *life* are granted both to *him* and his *deputy* <note>term: a person appointed to act on behalf of another</note>. # Of Expense.

## XXVIII.

Riches are for spending, and spending is for honor and good actions. Therefore, *extraordinary expense* must be limited by the worth of the occasion; for *voluntary ruin* <note>original: "undoing"</note> may be as much for one's country as it is for the kingdom of heaven. But *ordinary expense* ought to be limited by a man's estate <term>estate: a person's total net worth, including property and income</term> and governed with such care that it stays within his means <note>original: "compass"</note>. It should not be subject to the deceit and abuse of servants, and it should be ordered for the best outward appearance so that the actual bills are less than the public's estimation of the cost. 

Certainly, if a man desires to simply break even <note>original: "keep but of even hand"</note>, his *ordinary expenses* should be only half of his income <note>original: "receipts"</note>; and if he intends to grow rich, they should be only to 

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that wealth should be used for honorable purposes and advises that ordinary spending should be carefully managed to remain well below one's total income.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, wealth, expense, financial management, estate, income, frugality</keywords>

<header>*Of Expense.*</header>

...to the third part. It is no baseness for the greatest of people to descend and look into their own *estate* <note>original: "Estate"; here meaning one's total financial assets, property, and net worth</note>. Some avoid doing so, not because of negligence alone, but because they fear they will make themselves melancholy if they find their finances are in ruins. But wounds cannot be cured without being examined.

He who cannot look into his own estate at all has a need both to choose those whom he employs well and to change them often; for new employees are more timid and less cunning. For the man who can look into his estate only seldom, it is necessary for him to turn all his affairs into certainties <note>meaning to move from variable or unpredictable costs to fixed, reliable ones</note>. 

A man needs, if he is lavish in one kind of *expense*, to be just as saving in another. For example, if he is lavish in his diet, he should be saving in his clothing. If he is lavish in the hall <note>The "hall" refers to the cost of hosting, entertaining guests, and maintaining a large household staff</note>, he should be saving in the stable. For he who is lavish in *expenses* of all kinds will hardly be preserved from decay <note>original: "Decay"; meaning financial ruin or bankruptcy</note>. 

In clearing a man's estate <note>meaning the process of paying off debts or settling one's financial affairs</note>, he may hurt himself as much by being too sudden as by letting the situation run on too long. For hasty selling is common—

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that personal oversight of one's finances is a necessity rather than a lowly task, advising that high spending in one area must be balanced by frugality in another to avoid ruin.</summary>

# Of Expense

...equally as disadvantageous as interest. Besides, he who clears [his debts] all at once will likely relapse; for finding himself out of difficulties <note>original: "Straights"</note>, he will return to his old habits. But he who clears them by degrees develops a habit of frugality, and improves his mind as well as his estate. Certainly, whoever has an estate to repair <note>meaning to restore or rebuild a depleted fortune</note> must not despise small things. Generally, it is less dishonorable to reduce small expenses than to stoop to small gains <note>original: "pettie Gettings"; referring to seeking profit through trivial or potentially demeaning means</note>. A man ought to be cautious when starting expenses that will continue once begun; but in matters that do not recur, he may be more magnificent <note>original: "Magnificent"; in this context, meaning grand or generous in one-time spending</note>.

# Of the true Greatness of Kingdoms and Estates.

## XXIX.

The speech of *Themistocles* the *Athenian*, which was haughty and arrogant in taking so much credit to himself, would have been a serious and wise observation and judgment <note>original: "censure"</note> if applied generally to others. When he was asked at a feast to play the lute, he said, "*He could not fiddle, but yet he could make a small town into a great city.*" 

These words (helped <note>original: "holpen"</note> a little by a metaphor) may express two different abilities in those who deal in the business of the state <note>original: "Estate"</note>. For if a true survey is taken of counselors and statesmen, there may be found (though rarely) those who can make a *small state great* and yet cannot *fiddle*; just as on the other side, there
