### Of Kingdoms and Estates

When an army of the Romans, numbering no more than 14,000, was marching toward him, he <note>The historical figure is Tigranes the Great of Armenia, speaking of the Roman General Lucullus before the Battle of Tigranocerta.</note> mocked them, saying, "*Those men over there are too many for a diplomatic mission <note>original: "ambassage"</note>, and too few for a fight.*" But before the sun set, he found there were enough of them to chase him down with infinite slaughter. 

There are many examples of the great difference <note>original: "odds"</note> between numbers and courage. Therefore, one may truly judge that the principal point of greatness in any state is to have a population <note>original: "race"</note> of military men. Nor is money the "sinews of war" (as is commonly <note>original: "trivially"</note> said) if the sinews of men's arms are failing because the people are low-born <note>original: "base"</note> or weak. For Solon spoke wisely to Croesus <note>Croesus was a king of Lydia legendary for his wealth; Solon was a wise Athenian lawmaker.</note> (when Croesus boastfully showed him his gold): "*Sir, if anyone else comes who has better iron than you, he will become the master of all this gold.*" 

Therefore, let any prince or state think realistically <note>original: "soberly"</note> of their forces, unless their native militia consists of good and valiant soldiers. On the other hand, let princes who have subjects with a warlike <note>original: "martial"</note> disposition recognize their own strength;

<summary>The author argues that a state's true power resides in the martial courage of its citizens rather than its financial wealth or the size of its population. He illustrates this using historical anecdotes, concluding that "better iron" (valor) will always conquer gold.</summary>

...with **tribute**, is fit for **empire**. Let states that aim for **greatness** take care that their **nobility** and **gentlemen** do not multiply too fast. For that makes the **common subject** grow into a **peasant** and a **lowly laborer** <note>original: "base swain"</note>, discouraged <note>original: "driven out of heart"</note> and in effect nothing more than the **gentleman’s** laborer. Just as you may see in **coppice woods**: if you leave your **seed-trees** <term>staddles: young trees left standing when others are cut down to promote future growth</term> too thick, you shall never have clean **underwood**, but only **shrubs** and **bushes**. 

So in countries, if the **gentlemen** are too many, the **commons** will be base; and you will bring it to such a state that not one person in a hundred <note>original: "hundred poll"</note> will be fit to wear a **helmet**. This is especially true regarding the **infantry**, which is the **nerve** <term>nerve: used here in the classical sense of "sinew" or "strength"</term> of an army. And so, there will be a **great population** but **little strength**. 

This point has been nowhere better seen than by comparing **England** and **France**. Although **England** is far smaller in territory and population, it has nevertheless been a **superior match** <note>original: "overmatch"</note>. This is because the **middle people** of **England** make good **soldiers**, while the **peasants** of **France** do not. And here—

In this regard, the policy of King **Henry VII** (which I have discussed at length in the *History of His Life*) was profound and admirable. He established a standard for farms and houses of husbandry <note>farming</note>; specifically, he ensured they were maintained with enough land to allow a subject to live in comfortable abundance and not in a state of servitude. This kept the plow in the hands of the owners rather than mere hirelings. In this way, you shall achieve the character **Virgil** attributed to **ancient Italy**:

->*A land powerful in arms and in the fertility of its soil.*<- 
<note>original Latin: *Terra potens Armis atque ubere Glebae*</note>

Nor should that class of people be overlooked—which, as far as I know, is almost unique to **England** and hardly found anywhere else, except perhaps in **Poland**. I mean the class of **free servants** and **attendants** to **noblemen** and **gentlemen**. These men are in no way inferior to the **yeomanry** <note>independent farmers who owned their own land</note> when it comes to bearing arms. Therefore, without question, the splendor, magnificence, large retinues, and hospitality of noblemen and gentlemen...

By all means, it must be ensured that the trunk of *Nebuchadnezzar’s* tree of *monarchy* <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Daniel, where King Nebuchadnezzar dreams of a massive tree that provides fruit and shelter for the whole world, symbolizing a great empire.</note> is large enough to support its branches and boughs. That is, the *natural-born subjects* of the Crown or state must exist in a sufficient proportion to the *foreign subjects* <note>original: stranger subjects</note> whom they govern. Therefore, all states that are generous in granting naturalization <note>The legal act of making a foreigner a citizen.</note> toward foreigners are fit for *empire*. To think that a mere handful of people, even with the greatest courage and political skill in the world, can maintain too large an extent of dominion—it may hold for a time, but it will fail suddenly.

The *Spartans* were a picky <note>original: "nice," meaning fastidious or overly cautious</note> people regarding naturalization. Because of this, as long as they stayed within their own narrow boundaries, they stood firm. But when they expanded and their "branches" became too large for their "trunk," they suddenly collapsed like a tree blown over by the wind <note>original: windfall upon the sudden</note>.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the demographics and expansion of states, specifically focusing on how Rome integrated outsiders to build its empire.</meta>

...sudden. Never any state was, in this point, so open to receiving *strangers* into their body as were the *Romans*. Therefore, it worked out for them accordingly; for they grew into the greatest *monarchy*. Their custom was to grant *naturalization* (which they called the *right of citizenship* <note>original: *Ius Civitatis*</note>) and to grant it in the highest degree. That is, not only the *right of commerce* <note>original: *Ius Commercii*</note>, the *right of marriage* <note>original: *Ius Connubii*</note>, and the *right of inheritance* <note>original: *Ius Hereditatis*</note>, but also the *right of voting* <note>original: *Ius Suffragii*</note> and the *right of holding office* <note>original: *Ius Honorum*</note>. 

And this was granted not to single individuals alone, but likewise to whole families—even to cities and sometimes to entire nations. Add to this their custom of the *planting* <term>original: "Plantation"; here meaning the establishment of settlements or colonies</term> of *colonies*, by which the Roman "plant" was transplanted into the soil of other nations. And considering both these practices together, you might say that it was not the *Romans* who spread over the *world*, but rather it was the *world* that spread over the *Romans*; and that was the surest way to *greatness*. I have wondered sometimes at *Spain*, how they hold together and contain such large dominions with so few native-born *Spaniards*; but certainly, the whole extent...

That which comes nearest to it is to leave those trades <note>original: "Arts"</note> chiefly to foreigners (who for that purpose are the more easily to be admitted) and to keep the principal body of the common natives within those three kinds: *farmers* <note>original: "Tillers of the Ground"</note>; *free servants*; and *craftsmen* of *strong* and *manly trades*, such as *smiths*, *masons*, *carpenters*, and so on; not counting *professional soldiers*.

But above all, for *empire* and *greatness*, it is of the utmost importance that a nation profess *military service* <note>original: "Armes"</note> as their principal honor, study, and occupation. For the things which we have formerly spoken of are but preparations <term>original: "Habilitations"; the state of being made fit or qualified</term> for war. And what is preparation without *intention* and *action*? *Romulus* <note>The legendary founder of Rome</note>, after his death (as they report, or pretend), sent a message to the *Romans*: that, above all, they should focus on *military skill*; and then, they would prove to be the greatest *empire* of the world. # Of the True Greatness

...state, a just and honorable war is the true *exercise*. A civil war, indeed, is like the heat of a *fever*; but a foreign war is like the heat of *exercise*, and serves to keep the body [of the state] in health. For in a slothful peace, both courage will grow effeminate <note>original: "effeminate" — to become weak, soft, or unmanly</note> and morals <note>original: "Manners"</note> will corrupt.

But however it may be regarding happiness, it is beyond all question that for the sake of greatness, it contributes to be constantly, for the most part, in arms. The strength of a veteran army—though it is a costly <note>original: "chargeable"</note> business—that is always in the field <note>original: "on Foot"</note> is what commonly dictates the law, or at least maintains a reputation among all neighboring states. This may clearly be seen in *Spain*, which has had a veteran army in one region or another almost continually for the last 120 <note>original: "Six-score"</note> years.

To be master of the sea is an epitome <note>original: "Abridgement" — a smaller version that contains the essence of the whole</note> of a monarchy. *Cicero*, writing to *Atticus* about *Pompey’s* preparations against *Caesar*, says: "Pompey’s plan is clearly that of Themistocles; for he thinks that he who gains mastery of the sea..." <note>original: "Consilium Pompeij plane Themistocleum est; Putat enim, qui Mari potitur..." — Pompey’s strategy is plainly that of Themistocles; for he thinks that he who commands the sea [commands all].</note>

The *wars of latter ages* seem to be made in the dark, in respect of the glory and honor which reflected upon men from the *wars in ancient times*. ## of Health.
### XXX.

There is a wisdom in this beyond the rules of **medicine** <note>original: "Physicke"</note>: a person's own observation of what he finds benefit from and what he finds harm from is the best medicine to preserve health. But it is a safer conclusion to say, "This does not agree well with me, therefore I will not continue it," than to say, "I find no ill-effect from this, therefore I may use it." For the strength of nature in youth overlooks many excesses, for which a man is held in debt until he reaches his old age. Be aware of the advancement of years, and do not expect to continue doing the same things; for age will not be defied. Beware of sudden changes in any major aspect of **diet** <term>in this period, "diet" referred generally to one's entire way of living, including food, exercise, and sleep</term>, and if necessity...

### Of the Regimen of Health

Examine your customs of diet, sleep, exercise, clothing, and the like; and try to discontinue, little by little, anything you judge to be harmful. ## Of the Regime of Health.

# Of Suspicion.
## XXXI.

**S**uspicions among thoughts are like bats among birds: they always fly at twilight. Certainly, they are to be repressed, or at the least, well guarded; for they cloud the mind, they lose <note>original: "leese"; an archaic form of lose.</note> friends, and they interfere <note>original: "checke with"; to clash or conflict with.</note> with business, so that business cannot proceed smoothly <note>original: "currantly"</note> and constantly. They dispose kings to tyranny, husbands to jealousy, and wise men to irresolution and melancholy. They are defects not in the heart, but in the brain; for they take root <note>original: "take Place"</note> even in the strongest <note>original: "stoutest"; here meaning brave or resolute rather than physically large.</note> natures, as in the example of Henry VII <note>Henry VII (reigned 1485–1509), the first Tudor king, was famous for his cautious and suspicious nature, which he used to maintain political stability.</note> of England: there was not a more suspicious man, nor a more resolute <note>original: "stout"</note> one. And in such a character <note>original: "composition"</note>, they do little harm. For commonly they are not admitted except with an examination of whether they

<summary>Francis Bacon introduces the nature of suspicion, comparing it to a bat that thrives in darkness and warning of its tendency to paralyze action and damage relationships. He notes that while suspicion is a mental defect, it is less harmful in resolute individuals who examine their doubts rationally.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, suspicion, psychology, Henry VII, leadership, jealousy, irresolution, character</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>192</page-num>
<header># Of Suspicion</header>

<meta>image quality: good; significant bleed-through from the verso page; catchword: "municate." This page continues a discussion on the psychology of suspicion and how to manage it.</meta>

...whether they are likely or not? But in fearful natures, they gain ground too fast. Nothing makes a person suspect as much as knowing little. Therefore, people should remedy *suspicion* by seeking to know more, rather than keeping their *suspicions* suppressed <note>original: "in Smother"</note>. 

What would people have? Do they think those they employ and deal with are saints? Do they not think that others will seek their own ends and be truer to themselves than to those they serve? Therefore, there is no better way to moderate *suspicions* than to account for such *suspicions* as if they were true, and yet to restrain them as if they were false. A person ought to make use of *suspicions* only so far as to provide a safeguard; he should prepare so that even if what he suspects proves to be true, it may do him no harm. 

*Suspicions* that the mind gathers on its own are merely *buzzes* <note>original: "Buzzes"; meaning idle noises or minor, groundless distractions</note>; but *suspicions* that are artificially nourished and put into people's heads by the stories and whisperings of others have *stings*. Certainly, the best means to clear the way through this "wood of *suspicions*" is frankly to communicate...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that suspicion arises from ignorance and fear, advising that one should prepare for the worst possibilities of human self-interest while remaining skeptical of rumors planted by others.</summary>

...communicate them with the party that he *suspects*. By doing so, he will be sure to know more of the truth about them than he did before. Moreover, it will make that party more cautious <term>circumspect</term> not to give further cause for *suspicion*. 

But this should not be done with men of low character <note>original: "base Natures"</note>. For such men, if they find themselves once suspected, will never be loyal. The *Italian* proverb says: "Suspicion releases faith" <note>original: *Sospetto licentia fede*</note>. It is as if *suspicion* gave a *passport* <note>original: *Pasport*; here meaning a license or permission to depart</note> to *faith* <note>original: *Faith*; meaning loyalty or trust</note>. But suspicion ought instead to encourage a person to clear their name and fulfill their duty.

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's essay "Of Studies," specifically concluding the discussion on the habits of reading, conversation, and writing, before transitioning into how different fields of study shape a person's character and remedy mental weaknesses.</meta>

<page-num>294</page-num>

-># Of Studies.<-

...ready man; and writing makes an exact man. 

And therefore, if a man writes little, he needs a great memory; if he converses <note>original: "conferre"</note> little, he needs a quick wit; and if he reads little, he needs much cunning to seem to know what he does not. *Histories* make men wise; *poets* make them witty; *mathematics* makes them subtle; *natural philosophy* <note>the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note> makes them deep; *moral philosophy* makes them grave; and *logic* and *rhetoric* make them able to contend in argument. *Studies pass into character.* <note>Latin original: "Abeunt studia in mores." From Ovid’s Heroides.</note> 

Indeed, there is no obstruction <note>original: "stond"</note> or impediment in the mind <note>original: "wit"</note> that cannot be worked out by the right *studies*, just as diseases of the body may be treated with appropriate exercises. Bowling is good for the stone <note>kidney stones</note> and reins <note>the kidneys or the small of the back</note>; archery <note>original: "shooting"</note> is good for the lungs and chest; gentle walking is good for the stomach; riding is good for the head; and the like. 

So, if a man’s mind wanders, let him *study mathematics*; for in mathematical *demonstrations*, if his mind wanders even a little, he must begin again. If his mind is not suited <note>original: "apt"</note> to distinguish or find differences, let him *study the Schoolmen* <note>medieval theologians and philosophers known for their rigorous, minute logical distinctions</note>; for they are "hair-splitters" <note>Latin original: "cymini sectores," literally "splitters of cumin seeds."</note>. If he is not...

<summary>Bacon argues that different academic disciplines shape the human character and that specific studies can serve as "remedies" for various mental deficiencies, much like physical exercise cures bodily ailments.</summary>

<page-num>295</page-num>

# Of Studies

---

If a man is inclined to examine matters from every angle, and to call upon one thing to prove and illustrate another, let him study <term>lawyers' cases</term> <note>original: "Lawyers Cases"; the study of legal precedents and case law, which requires comparing complex details to find applicable rules</note>. In this way, every flaw of the mind may have a specific <term>remedy</term> <note>original: "Receit"; used here in the medicinal sense of a prescription or recipe for a cure</note>.

---

# Of Faction.
## 51.

Many hold an unwise opinion: that for a prince to govern his state <note>original: "Estate"; here meaning the government or the body politic</note>, or for a great person to govern his proceedings according to the interests of factions, is a principal part of policy <note>political strategy or statecraft</note>. On the contrary, the greatest wisdom lies either in managing those things which are general, and in which men of several factions nevertheless agree; or in dealing with correspondence to particular persons, one by one. 

However, I do not say that the consideration of factions is to be neglected. Men of lower rank <note>original: "Meane Men"</note>, in their rising, must adhere to a faction; but great men, who have strength in themselves, are better off maintaining themselves as indifferent <note>impartial</note> and

<meta>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword: Neutral.</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues against the idea that governing through factions is a sign of good statesmanship, suggesting instead that true wisdom lies in seeking common ground or dealing with individuals directly.</summary>
<keywords>faction, policy, statecraft, leadership, impartiality, Francis Bacon, political strategy</keywords>

### Of Faction.

Neutral. Yet even in beginners, to adhere so moderately that he be a man of the one *Faction* who is most acceptable <note>original: "Passable"</note> to the other, commonly gives the best results <note>original: "best Way"</note>. The lower and weaker *Faction* is the firmer in its alliance <note>original: "Coniunction"</note>; and it is often seen that a few who are stubborn <note>original: "Stiffe"</note> tire out a greater number who are more moderate. When one of the *Factions* is extinguished, the remaining one subdivides. As the *Faction* between *Lucullus* and the rest of the nobles of the Senate (whom they called the *Optimates* <note>Latin: "the best men"; the traditionalist senatorial faction in the late Roman Republic</note>) held out for a while against the *Faction* of *Pompey* and *Caesar*; but when the Senate's authority was pulled down, *Caesar* and *Pompey* soon after broke apart. The *Faction* or party of *Antony* <note>original: "Antonius"</note> and *Octavian Caesar* against *Brutus* and *Cassius* likewise held out for a time; but when *Brutus* and *Cassius* were overthrown, then soon after *Antony* and *Octavian* broke and subdivided. These examples are from wars, but the same holds true in private *Factions*. And therefore, those who are seconds in *Factions*—

<summary>This page explores the dynamics of political groups, explaining that weaker factions are often more united and that once a common enemy is defeated, the remaining alliance invariably splits into new rivalries.</summary>

<header>*Of Faction.*</header>

## *Of Faction.*

---

**Factions** <note>OCR error: "Etions"</note> often, when the faction subdivides, prove to be leaders <note>original: "Principals"</note>. But many times also, they prove to be non-entities <note>original: "Ciphars"</note> and are discarded <note>original: "Casheer'd"</note>. For many a man's strength lies in being in opposition; and when that opposition fails, he becomes useless. It is commonly seen that men, once they are appointed to office <note>original: "Placed"</note>, join the faction opposite to the one that helped them enter. They likely think that they have already secured their first favor <note>original: "First Sute"</note> and are now ready for a new acquisition <note>original: "New Purchase"</note>. 

The traitor in a faction easily gets away with his schemes; for when matters have long been stuck in a balance, winning over even one man tips the scales <note>original: "casteth them"</note>, and he receives all the credit. Maintaining an even balance <note>original: "Euen Carriage"</note> between two factions does not always result from moderation, but from a person being true only to themselves, with the goal of making use of both sides. 

Certainly in Italy, they find it a little suspicious in Popes when they often use the phrase "Common Father" <note>original: *Padre commune*</note>. They take it to be a sign of one who intends to refer everything to the greatness of his own family <note>original: "House"</note>. Kings must be careful how they take sides—

# Of Faction.

---

...and make themselves part of a *Faction* or *Party*. For alliances within the state are always harmful to monarchies, because they create an obligation that outweighs the obligation to the sovereign. They make the King appear "as one of us" <note>original Latin: *tanquàm unus ex nobis*</note>, as was seen in the *League of France* <note>The Catholic League (1576–1595), a powerful faction that challenged the French monarchy during the Wars of Religion</note>. When *factions* are carried too high and too violently, it is a sign of weakness in princes, and it greatly prejudices both their authority and their business. The movements of *factions* under kings ought to be like the movements (as the *astronomers* describe them) of the lower planetary spheres; these may have their own specific motions, but they are still quietly guided by the higher motion of the "First Mover" <note>original Latin: *Primum Mobile*. In the ancient geocentric model of the universe, this was the outermost sphere that moved all the inner spheres in their proper courses</note>.

# Of Ceremonies
## and Respects.
### LII.

A person who is only sincere and plain <note>original: "Real"; here meaning someone who focuses on substance over social graces</note> needs to have exceptionally great virtues, just as a gemstone needs to be of high quality if it is set without a foil <note>A thin leaf of metal placed under a gem to enhance its brilliance</note>. But if a man observes it well, gaining praise and commendation is much like earning money: the proverb is true that *light gains make heavy purses*. Small profits come frequently, whereas great ones come only occasionally. 

In the same way, it is true that small matters win great praise because they are continually in use and noticed; whereas the opportunity to display a great virtue arises only on rare occasions <note>original: "Festivals"</note>. Therefore, having "good forms" <note>Good manners, etiquette, and social graces</note> adds much to a person's reputation. As *Queen Isabella* <note>Isabella I of Castile (1451–1504)</note> said, they are *like perpetual letters of recommendation*.

<summary>In this opening to his essay on social etiquette, Francis Bacon argues that while inner virtue is essential, outward manners ("ceremonies") are vital because they provide constant, small opportunities to earn a good reputation.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, etiquette, manners, Queen Isabella, social graces, reputation, virtue</keywords>

# Of Ceremonies and Respects.

---

To achieve them <note>original: "Attaine them," referring to social graces and proper etiquette</note>, it is almost enough not to despise them; for by doing so, a man will observe them in others, and he can trust himself with the rest. For if he labors too much to express them, he shall lose their grace, which consists in being natural and unaffected. Some men's behavior is like a verse where every syllable is measured. How can a man comprehend great matters if he troubles his mind too much with such small observations? 

Not to use *ceremonies* at all is to teach others not to use them in return, and so it diminishes the *respect* shown to himself; they especially should not be omitted when dealing with strangers and formal natures. But dwelling upon them and exalting them "above the moon" <note>an expression meaning to praise excessively or hyperbolically</note> is not only tedious but also diminishes the credibility and trust of the speaker. And certainly, there is a way of conveying effective and memorable points amidst compliments, which is of singular use if a man can master it. Among a man's peers, a man shall be sure of familiarity; and therefore, it is good a

# Of Ceremonies and Manners <note>original: "Respects"</note>
---

...a little to maintain dignity <note>original: "keep State"</note>. Among a man's inferiors, one will be sure to receive reverence; and therefore it is good to be a little informal <note>original: "Familiar"</note>. He who overdoes anything, so that he gives others cause for boredom or over-satiety <note>original: "Sacietie"</note>, makes himself seem cheap. To adapt oneself to others is good, provided it is demonstrated that a man does it out of respect <note>original: "Regard"</note> and not out of mere compliance <note>original: "Facilitie"</note>. 

It is a good rule, when generally agreeing with <note>original: "Seconding"</note> another, to still add something of one's own: if you grant his opinion, let it be with some distinction; if you follow his proposal <note>original: "Motion"</note>, let it be with a condition; if you approve of his advice, let it be by citing further reasons. Men should be careful not to be too perfect in social etiquette <note>original: "Complements"</note>; for however capable <note>original: "Sufficient"</note> they may be otherwise, those who envy them will be sure to label them with that attribute, to the disadvantage of their greater virtues. It is also a hindrance <note>original: "loſſe"</note> in business to be too full of formalities <note>original: "Reſpects"</note>, or to be too fussy <note>original: "Curious"</note> in waiting for the exact right times and opportunities. Solomon says: *He who considers the wind shall not* <meta>Catchword: sow</meta> <note>Ecclesiastes 11:4: "He that observeth the wind shall not sow; and he that regardeth the clouds shall not reap."</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that while some ceremony is necessary for dignity, excessive formality makes a person seem "cheap" or overly compliant, and can even hinder business success.</summary>

# Of Praise.
## 53 <note>original: LIII</note>

Praise is the reflection of virtue. But it depends on the glass or body that provides the reflection. If it comes from the common people, it is usually false and worthless <note>original: "naught"</note>, and follows vain people rather than virtuous ones. For the common people do not understand many excellent virtues. The lowest virtues draw *praise* from them; the middle virtues produce astonishment or admiration in them; but for the highest virtues, they have no sense or perception at all. Instead, *appearances* and *appearances that resemble virtues* <note>original: *species virtutibus similes*</note> serve best with them. <summary>In this essay, Bacon argues that public praise is often a distorted reflection of true virtue because the masses lack the discernment to appreciate the highest human excellences. He famously compares fame to a river that preserves the superficial while losing the profound.</summary>

# Of Praise.

Scripture says, "A good name is like a fragrant ointment" <note>original: *Nomen bonum instar unguenti fragrantis* (Ecclesiastes 7:1)</note>. It fills all the space around it and will not easily fade away. For the scents of ointments are more durable than those of flowers. There are so many false aspects of **praise** that a person may rightly view it with suspicion. 

Some **praises** proceed merely from flattery. If the speaker is an ordinary flatterer, he will have certain common attributes which may serve any man. If he is a cunning flatterer, he will follow the "arch-flatterer," which is a man’s own self <note>The "arch-flatterer" refers to one's own ego or self-love</note>; whatever a man thinks best of himself, in that area the flatterer will support him most. 

But if he is an impudent <note>shameless or bold</note> flatterer, he will look at whatever a man is conscious of as his greatest defect—the thing that makes him most embarrassed in himself—and the flatterer will insist on attributing that quality to him, "despising his own conscience" <note>original: *Spretâ Conscientiâ*</note>. 

Some **praises** come from good wishes and respect, which is a form of civility due to kings and great persons: "to instruct by praising" <note>original: *Laudando præcipere*</note>. In this way, by telling men what they are, they represent to them what they <meta>catchword: they</meta>

<summary>In this opening to his essay on praise, Francis Bacon compares a good reputation to a lasting ointment and distinguishes between various types of flattery, ranging from generic compliments to manipulative ego-stroking.</summary>
<keywords>praise, flattery, reputation, ego, social etiquette, Francis Bacon, morality</keywords>

Some men are praised maliciously to their harm, thereby to stir up envy and jealousy toward them; *The worst kind of enemies are those who praise you* <note>original Latin: "Pessimum genus Inimicorum laudantium"</note>. It was even a proverb among the Greeks that he who was praised to his harm would have a pimple <note>original: "push"</note> rise upon his nose; just as we say that a blister will rise upon the tongue of someone who tells a lie. Certainly, moderate praise—used at the right opportunity and not in a common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> way—is what does good.

Solomon says, *“He who praises his friend loudly, rising early in the morning, it shall be counted a curse to him”* <note>original: "He that praiseth his Frend aloud, Rising Early, it shall be to him, no better then a Curse." To praise oneself cannot be decent except in rare cases; but a man may praise his office or profession with good grace and with a kind of magnanimity <note>greatness of spirit or dignity</note>.

# Of Vain-Glory.
## 54.

It was a clever story told by **Aesop**: A fly sat upon the axle-tree of a chariot wheel and said, "*What a dust I raise!*" So there are some **vain persons** who, whenever something moves forward on its own or is driven by more powerful forces, if they have even the smallest hand in it, they think it is they who are carrying the whole thing. Those who are **boastful** <note>original: "Glorious" – in this context, meaning full of vain-glory or ostentatious</note> must necessarily be **divisive** <note>original: "Factious"</note>, for all outward display <note>original: "Bravery"</note> depends upon making comparisons with others. They must also be **forceful** to back up their own boasts. Nor can they be **secret**, and therefore they are not effective; but they fit the **French proverb**: *Beaucoup de bruit, peu de fruit*—*Much noise, little fruit* <note>original: "Bruit" – noise or rumor</note>. Yet there is certainly a use for this quality in **public affairs** <note>original: "Civil Affairs"</note>. In situations where public opinion...

For example, when a man negotiates between two princes to draw them to join in a war against a third, and exaggerates the forces of each prince to the other beyond measure. And sometimes, he that deals between man and man raises his own credit with both by pretending to have a greater interest in each than he actually has. In these and similar cases, it often happens that *something* is produced out of *nothing*; for lies are sufficient to breed *opinion* <note>In this context, "opinion" means reputation or public belief.</note>, and *opinion* brings on *substance* <note>Meaning real, tangible results or power.</note>.

In cases of great enterprise, involving high cost <note>original: "Charge"</note> and risk, a combination of *glorious natures* <note>People who are bold, boastful, and eager for fame.</note> puts life into the business; while those who are of *solid* and *sober na-*

# <page-num>350</page-num>
## <header>Of Vain-Glory.</header>

...natures have more of the ballast than of the sail. In the reputation for learning, progress will be slow without some feathers of *ostentation*. "Those who write books about despising glory still put their names on the cover" <note>original Latin: *Qui de contemnendâ Gloriâ Libros ſcribunt, Nomen ſuum inſcribunt*</note>. *Socrates*, *Aristotle*, and *Galen* were men full of *ostentation*. Certainly, *vain-glory* helps to perpetuate a person's memory; virtue has never been so indebted to human nature that it received its due credit at second hand without some help. Neither would the fame of *Cicero*, *Seneca*, or *Pliny the Younger* <note>original: *Plinius Secundus*</note> have endured so well through the ages if it had not been joined with some *vanity* in themselves—much like *varnish*, which makes ceilings not only shine but also last. 

But all this while, when I speak of *vain-glory*, I do not mean that quality which *Tacitus* attributes to *Mucianus*: "He had a certain art of showing off everything he said and did" <note>original Latin: *Omnium, quæ dixerat, feceratq́; Arte quadam Oſtentator*</note>. For that proceeds not from *vanity*, but from natural magnanimity <term>magnanimity: loftiness of spirit; nobility of soul</term> and discretion. In some persons, this is not only attractive but even graceful. For excuses, concessions <term>cessions: the act of giving way or yielding to another</term>, and even modesty itself, when well-managed, are merely different arts of *ostentation*.

<summary>Bacon argues that a degree of self-promotion or "ostentation" is necessary for a person's reputation and the longevity of their fame, acting as a protective varnish for their virtues. He distinguishes between shallow vanity and a sophisticated, graceful art of displaying one's talents and character.</summary>

<keywords>vain-glory, ostentation, reputation, fame, learning, virtue, vanity, Cicero, Seneca, Tacitus, social strategy</keywords>

<page-num>311</page-num>
<header>Of Vain-Glory</header>

**Ostentation.** And among those arts, there is none better than the one that *Plinius Secundus* <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, known as Pliny the Younger (c. 61–113 AD), a Roman lawyer and author.</note> speaks of, which is to be liberal with praise and commendation toward others in those areas where a man has some perfection himself. For, as *Pliny* says very wittily: *“In commending another, you do yourself justice; for he whom you commend is either superior to you in what you praise, or inferior. If he is inferior, and yet worthy of praise, you are much more so. If he is superior, and yet not worthy of praise, then you are even less worthy.”* *Glorious* <note>original: "Glorious"; in this context, it means boastful or full of empty pride (from the Latin 'gloriosus').</note> men are the scorn of wise men, the admiration of fools, the idols of parasites, and the slaves of their own boasts <note>original: "vaunts"</note>.

<summary>The author concludes that the most effective way to display one's own excellence is to praise that same quality in others, while cautioning that boastful individuals are ultimately mocked by the wise and enslaved by their own vanity.</summary>

# Of Honour and Reputation.
## LV.

The winning of <term>*Honour*</term> is simply the revealing of a man's <term>Virtue</term> <note>original: "Vertue"</note> and worth, without disadvantage. For some in their actions do woo and seek after <note>original: "affect"</note> <term>*Honour*</term> and <term>*Reputation*</term>; these types of men are commonly much talked about, but inwardly little admired. And some, on the contrary, darken their <term>Virtue</term> in the showing of it, so that they are undervalued in public opinion. If a man performs that which has not been attempted before; or was attempted and given up <note>original: "giuen ouer"</note>; or has been achieved, but not with such good circumstances <note>meaning with less skill or less favorable results</note>; he shall acquire <note>original: "purchase"</note> more <term>*Honour*</term> than by accomplishing a matter of greater difficulty or <term>Virtue</term> wherein he is merely a follower. ### Of Honour and Reputation.

his actions, so that in at least one of them, he satisfies every faction or group of people, the music will be the fuller <note>The "music" refers to the harmony and completeness of public praise.</note>. A man is a poor manager <note>original: "ill Husband"</note> of his *honor* if he enters into any action where failing might disgrace him more than succeeding could *honor* him. *Honor* that is gained and sharpened <note>original: "broken," likely referring to the cutting of gemstones</note> by comparison to another has the brightest reflection, like diamonds cut with facets. Therefore, let a man strive to excel any of his competitors in *honor* by "out-shooting" them, if he can, with their own bow <note>Beating them at their own game or specialty.</note>. Discreet followers and servants contribute much to *reputation*. "All reputation originates from those within the household" <note>original: *Omnis Fama à Domesticis emanat*.</note>. Envy, which is the canker <note>A destructive disease or spreading sore; something that corrodes.</note> of *honor*, is best extinguished by a man declaring that his goals are to seek merit rather than fame, and by attributing his successes to divine providence and good fortune <note>original: "felicity"</note> rather than to his own virtue or cunning <note>original: "policy"</note>. The true ranking <note>original: "marshalling"</note> of the degrees of sovereign *honor* is as follows: In the first place are the **Founders of Empires** <note>original: *Conditores Imperiorum*</note>; founders of

States and commonwealths: such as were Romulus, Cyrus, Caesar, Ottoman <note>Osman I, founder of the Ottoman Empire</note>, and Ismail <note>Shah Ismail I, founder of the Safavid dynasty of Persia</note>.

In the second place are lawgivers <note>original: *legis-latores*</note>, who are also called "second founders" or "perpetual rulers" <note>original: *perpetui principes*</note> because they govern through their ordinances long after they are gone. Such were Lycurgus, Solon, Justinian, Edgar <note>King Edgar the Peaceful of England</note>, and Alfonso the Wise of Castile, who created the *Siete Partidas* <note>A famous seven-part statutory code in 13th-century Spain</note>.

These include Augustus Caesar, Vespasian, Aurelian, Theodoric, King Henry VII of England, and King Henry IV of France.

In the fourth place are those who extend or defend the empire <note>original: *propagatores* or *propugnatores imperii*</note>: those who enlarge their territories through honorable wars or make a noble defense against invaders.

And in the last place are the "fathers of their country" <note>original: *patres patriae*</note>, who reign justly and make the times in which they live good. These last two kinds need no examples, as they are so numerous.

# Of Judicature.
## 56.

**J**udges ought to remember that their office is *to state the law* <note>original Latin: *Ius dicere*</note>, and not *to give the law* <note>original Latin: *Ius dare*</note>; to interpret law, and not to make law or give law. Otherwise, it will be like the authority claimed by the *Church of Rome*, which under the pretext of the *exposition of Scripture* <note>the interpretation of religious texts</note>, does not hesitate to add and alter, and to pronounce that which they do not find—and by a show of *antiquity*, to introduce *novelty*. Judges ought to be more learned than witty, more reverend <note>worthy of respect</note> than popular <note>original: "plausible," in the sense of seeking public applause</note>, and more cautious <note>original: "advised"</note> than confident. Above all things, integrity is their portion and proper virtue. *Cursed (says the Law) is he that removes the landmark.* <note>A reference to Deuteronomy 27:17, regarding the theft of land by moving boundary markers.</note> The *mislayer* of a <term>mere-stone</term> <note>a stone used to mark a boundary</note>

<summary>In this opening to his essay on the judiciary, Bacon argues that the role of a judge is to interpret existing laws rather than create new ones, emphasizing integrity and caution over intellectual vanity.</summary>
<keywords>judicature, judges, legal interpretation, integrity, common law, boundary markers, ethics</keywords>

For these examples merely corrupt the stream; the other corrupts the fountain. So says Solomon: *A troubled fountain and a tainted spring is a righteous man who falters in his cause before his adversary.* <note>original Latin: *Fons turbatus, & Vena corrupta, est Iustus cadens in causa suâ coram Aduersario.*</note> The office of judges may relate to four areas: to the parties that sue; to the advocates that plead; to the clerks and ministers of justice underneath them; and to the sovereign or state above them.

The principal duty of a judge is to suppress force and fraud; of which force is the more pernicious when it is open, and fraud when it is hidden and disguised. <page-num>322</page-num>
<header>Of Judicature.</header>

## Of Judicature.

There are four types of people who hinder the work of the courts. First, certain persons who are sowers of lawsuits, who make the court swell with cases while the country pines <note>original: "pine"; meaning to suffer or waste away</note>. The second sort are those who engage courts in quarrels over jurisdiction; these are not truly *Friends of the Court* <note>original: "Amici Curiæ"</note> but rather *Parasites of the Court* <note>original: "Parasiti Curiæ"</note>, inflating a court’s authority beyond its proper bounds for their own profit and advantage. The third sort are those who may be considered the "left hands" of courts—persons full of clever and dishonest tricks and maneuvers, by which they pervert the plain and direct courses of the *courts* and lead *justice* into indirect paths and labyrinths. And the fourth is the extortionist <note>original: "Poler"; one who "fleeces" or strips others of their money</note> and exacter of fees. This justifies the common comparison of the *courts of justice* to a *bramble bush*: while the *sheep* flies to it for protection from the weather, he is sure to lose part of his fleece. On the other hand, a senior clerk <note>original: "Ancient Clerke"; a long-serving or high-ranking court official</note>, skillful in precedents <note>original: "Presidents"</note>, cautious in proceedings, and knowledgeable in the *business of the court*, is an excellent "finger" of a *court* and often points the way to the *judge himself*.

<meta>catchword: Fourthly,</meta>

Fourthly, regarding that which may concern the *Sovereign* and the *State*. *Judges* ought above all to remember the conclusion of the *Roman Twelve Tables* <note>The earliest code of Roman law, dating to the 5th century BCE.</note>: *The safety of the people is the supreme law* <note>original Latin: Salus Populi Suprema Lex</note>. And they should know that *laws*, unless they are directed toward that end, are but deceptive things <note>original: captious</note> and oracles not well-inspired. Therefore, it is a *fortunate thing* in a *state* when *kings* and *states* often consult with *judges*, and again, when *judges* often consult with the *king* and *state*: the former, when there is a *matter of law* intervening in *business of state*; the latter, when there is some *consideration of state* intervening in a *matter of law*. For many times, the *things* brought to *judgment* may be matters of "*mine* and *thine*" <note>original Latin: Meum and Tuum; a legal term for private property disputes between individuals</note>, when the reasoning and consequences of the case may reach toward a *point of state*. # Of Anger.
## LVII.

To seek to extinguish *anger* utterly is but a boast <note>original: "bravery"</note> of the *Stoics* <note>A school of philosophy that taught the suppression of all emotions</note>. We have better teachings <note>original: "oracles"</note>: *Be angry, but sin not.* *Let not the sun go down upon your anger.* *Anger* must be limited and confined, both in its course <note>original: "race"</note> and in its duration. We will first speak of how the natural inclination and habit of being *angry* may be tempered and calmed. Secondly, how the particular impulses of *anger* may be repressed, or at least restrained from doing mischief. Thirdly, how to rouse *anger* or appease *anger* in another.

Regarding the first point: there is no other way but to meditate and ruminate well upon the effects of *anger*, and how it troubles a person's life. And the best time to do 

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that anger cannot be entirely extinguished as the Stoics claimed, but must instead be managed, limited in duration, and understood through its negative effects on life.</summary>
<keywords>Anger, Stoicism, Self-Control, Ethics, Moral Philosophy, Psychology</keywords>

# Of Anger.

...this is to look back upon *Anger* when the fit is thoroughly over. *Seneca* <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher who wrote an influential treatise titled "De Ira" (On Anger).</note> says well: that *Anger* is like a *ruin*, which breaks itself upon that which it falls. The Scripture exhorts us to *possess* our souls in *patience*. Whoever is out of *patience* is out of possession of his own soul. Men must not turn into *bees*, who:

---

->*And leave their lives in the wound.* <note>original: "Animasque in vulnere ponunt" — a quote from Virgil’s *Georgics* describing how bees, in their anger, sting others but die in the process.</note><-

*Anger* is certainly a kind of baseness, as is clearly seen in the weakness of those subjects in whom it reigns: children, women, old folks, and sick folks. Men must only take care that they carry their *anger* with scorn rather than with fear, so that they may seem to be above the injury rather than beneath it—which is a thing easily done if a man will discipline himself in it.

For the second point: the *causes* and *motives* of *anger* are chiefly three. First, to be too *sensitive* to *hurt* <note>original: "Sensible of Hurt"</note>. For no man is *angry* who does not *feel* himself hurt. Therefore, tender and delicate persons must necessarily be often *angry*; they...

<summary>Bacon argues that anger is a sign of weakness and baseness, comparing it to a falling building that breaks itself or a bee that dies while stinging. He identifies the first major cause of anger as being overly sensitive to personal injury.</summary>
<keywords>Anger, Seneca, Stoicism, patience, self-control, vulnerability, causes of anger, sensitivity to hurt</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the types of people most prone to anger, specifically those with "delicate" or "weak" dispositions mentioned on the preceding page.</meta>

<page-num>327</page-num>
# Of Anger.

---

...have so many things to trouble them, which more robust natures have little sense of. The next cause is the perception and interpretation of the injury offered, finding it to be, in its circumstances, full of *contempt*. For *contempt* is what puts an edge upon *anger*, as much as—or even more than—the *harm* itself. Therefore, when men are clever at picking out circumstances of *contempt*, they kindle their *anger* greatly. Lastly, the belief that one's *reputation* has been touched <note>In this context, "touched" means slighted, insulted, or damaged.</note> multiplies and sharpens *anger*. In this case, the remedy is that a man should have—as *Consalvo* <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba (1453–1515), a celebrated Spanish general known as "The Great Captain."</note> used to say—a thicker web of honor <note>original Latin: *telam honoris crassiorem*; meaning one's sense of honor should not be so thin or fragile that every small slight breaks through it.</note>.

But in all efforts to restrain *anger*, the best remedy is to gain time and to make oneself believe that the opportunity for revenge has not yet arrived, but that he foresees a time for it; thus, he stills himself in the meantime and reserves his reaction.

To keep *anger* from causing harm <note>original: "mischief"; in the 17th century, this referred to serious injury, damage, or evil consequences rather than mere playfulness.</note>, even though it takes hold of a man, there are two things of which you must have spe- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next word is "special."</note>

<summary>This page explores the triggers of anger—specifically contempt and perceived hits to one's reputation—and suggests that the best way to restrain anger is to delay action and develop a less sensitive sense of honor.</summary>
<keywords>Anger, Contempt, Reputation, Consalvo, Self-restraint, Honor, Stoicism, Francis Bacon</keywords>

<meta>The text on this page continues from the previous page, likely completing the word "special."</meta>

# Of Anger.

...special caution. The first is to avoid *extreme bitterness of words*, especially if they are *pointed and personal* <note>original: "aculeate and proper"; "aculeate" means stinging like a needle</note>. For *common insults* <note>Latin: *communia maledicta*</note> do not sting nearly as much. Furthermore, in *anger*, a man must reveal no secrets; for doing so makes him unfit for society. The second caution is that you do not *abruptly break off* <note>original: "peremptorily break off"</note> any business during a *fit of anger*. However much bitterness you may *show*, do not *act* in any way that is not revocable <note>meaning: something that cannot be undone or taken back</note>.

Regarding *raising* or *appeasing anger* in another person, it is done chiefly by the *choosing of times*. To provoke men, incense them when they are most irritable <note>original: "frowardest"</note> and in their worst moods. Again, one can provoke anger by gathering (as was mentioned before) everything you can find to emphasize the sense of contempt. 

The two *remedies* for this are the exact opposites. The first is to choose a good time to first relate to a man an *unpleasant matter* <note>original: "angry business"</note>; for the first impression matters a great deal. The other is to separate, as much as possible, the interpretation of the injury from the point of *contempt*. Attribute the offense instead to misunderstanding, fear, passion, or whatever you wish.

<summary>Francis Bacon concludes his essay on anger by advising readers to avoid revealing secrets or taking irreversible actions during an outburst. He also explains how to manage the anger of others by timing difficult news and reframing perceived insults as mere misunderstandings.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Of Anger, conflict resolution, rhetoric, social conduct, psychological strategy</keywords>

# Of the Vicissitude
## of Things.
### LVIII.

**S**OLOMON says, "*There is no new thing upon the earth*." So, just as *Plato* had a notion <note>original: "Imagination"</note> that all knowledge was but remembrance, *Solomon* gives his judgment <note>original: "Sentence"</note> that all novelty is but oblivion. By this, you may see that the River of Lethe <term>Lethe: In Greek mythology, a river in the underworld whose water caused those who drank it to forget their past lives.</term> runs as much above ground as below. 

There is an obscure <note>original: "abstruse"</note> astrologer who says that if it were not for two things that are constant, no individual thing would last for a single moment. The first is that the fixed stars <term>fixed stars: Stars that appear to maintain a relative position to each other, unlike the "wandering stars" or planets.</term> always stand at the same distance from one another, never coming closer together nor moving further apart. The second is that the diurnal motion <term>diurnal motion: The apparent daily rotation of the stars and planets around the Earth.</term> perpetually keeps time. It is certain that the mat-

<summary>Francis Bacon introduces the cyclical nature of history and physical reality, arguing that "novelty" is simply the result of human forgetfulness. He cites Solomon and Plato to support the idea that change is a constant governed by the stability of the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>Vicissitude, Solomon, Plato, Lethe, oblivion, astrology, fixed stars, diurnal motion, cyclical history</keywords>

# Of the Vicissitude of Things

<warning>Heavy ink bleed and show-through from the reverse side of the page create dark patches that partially obscure several words throughout the text.</warning>

...matter is in a perpetual flux and never remains the same. The great shrouds <note>original: "winding-sheets"</note> that bury all things in oblivion <note>total forgetfulness or state of being forgotten</note> are two: deluges <note>massive floods</note> and earthquakes. As for conflagrations <note>great fires</note> and great droughts, they do not merely depopulate and destroy. Phaeton’s chariot <note>original: "Phaetons Carre"; a reference to the Greek myth where Phaeton lost control of the sun's chariot, nearly burning the Earth</note> went for only a day. And the three years' drought in the time of Elijah <note>original: "Elias"</note> was only localized <note>original: "Particular"</note> and left people alive. As for the great fires caused by lightning, which are frequent in the <term>West Indies</term> <note>At the time of writing, this often referred to the Americas in general</note>, they are only narrow in scope. 

But in the other two destructions, by deluge and earthquake, it is further to be noted that the remnant of people who happen to be spared are commonly ignorant mountain people who can give no account of the past. So, the oblivion is just the same as if no one had been left alive. If you consider the people of the <term>West Indies</term> carefully, it is very probable that they are a newer or a younger people than the people of the <term>Old World</term> <note>Eurasia and Africa</note>. And it is much more likely that the destruction that has occurred there in the past was not caused by earthquakes...

# Of Vicissitude of Things

(As the Egyptian priest told Solon concerning the island of Atlantis: that it was swallowed by an earthquake;) but rather, that it was devastated by a localized flood <note>original: "Particular Deluge"</note>. For earthquakes are rare in those parts. On the other hand, they have such torrential rivers <note>original: "Pouring Rivers"</note> that the rivers of Asia, Africa, and Europe are but brooks in comparison. Their Andes mountains are likewise much higher than ours; therefore, it seems that the survivors of the human race <note>original: "Remnants of Generation of Men"</note> were saved from such a localized flood by being in the highlands. 

Regarding the observation made by Machiavelli <note>Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Italian political philosopher</note>—that the rivalry <note>original: "Jealousy"</note> between religious sects greatly erases the memory of past events—he criticized Pope Gregory the Great for doing everything in his power to wipe out all pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note> antiquities. However, I do not find that such religious fervor has any great effect or lasts very long; this was seen in the succession of Pope Sabinian, who revived the previously suppressed antiquities.

The cycles or changes <note>original: "Vicissitude or Mutations"</note> in the celestial bodies <note>original: "Superiour Globe," referring to the heavens and planets</note> are not a suitable subject for this current discussion. It may be that Plato's "Great Year" <note>The "Great Year" refers to the astronomical cycle in which the sun, moon, and planets all return to the same relative positions.</note>...

# Of the Vicissitude <note>Vicissitude: The natural change or mutation of things; the successive ups and downs of life or nature.</note> of Things.

Years, if the world should last so long, would have some effect—not in renewing the state of similar individuals (for that is the vanity <note>original: "fume"; here meaning a vain fancy or empty vapor</note> of those who imagine that celestial bodies have more precise influences upon things below than they actually do), but in the general mass. *Comets*, without question, also have power and effect over the bulk and mass of things. But they are gazed upon and followed in their journey rather than wisely observed for their effects—especially in their specific <note>original: "respective"</note> effects. That is: what kind of *comet*—based on its size, color, the direction <note>original: "version"</note> of its rays, its position in the heavens, or its duration—produces what kind of effects.

There is a trifling observation <note>original: "toy"; a light matter or curiosity</note> which I have heard of, and I would not have it dismissed but rather considered for a while. They say it is observed in the *Low Countries* <note>The coastal regions of northwestern Europe, including the Netherlands and Belgium.</note> (though I do not know exactly where) that every thirty-five years, the same pattern and sequence <note>original: "sute" (suit)</note> of years and weather returns: such as great frosts, great rains, and great droughts.

<summary>Bacon argues that while celestial bodies like comets influence the world in broad strokes, their specific effects are poorly understood. He also introduces the idea of a thirty-five-year weather cycle observed in the Low Countries.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Vicissitude, Comets, Astronomy, Weather Cycles, Low Countries, Astrology, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

Warm winters, summers with little heat, and the like: and they call it the *Prime* <note>original: "Prime"; refers to a supposed thirty-five-year cycle in which weather patterns were thought to repeat</note>. It is a thing I mention more readily because, by calculating backward, I have found some concurrence <note>agreement or correspondence</note> with this theory.

But to leave these points of *Nature* and to come to *Men*: the greatest *vicissitude* <note>change or succession of one thing to another</note> of things among *men* is the *vicissitude* of *sects* and *religions*. For those orbs <note>astronomical metaphor for spheres of influence</note> rule most strongly in men's minds. The *True Religion* is built upon the rock; the rest are tossed upon the waves of time. I will speak, therefore, of the causes of *new sects*, and give some counsel concerning them—as far as the weakness of human judgment can provide a stay <note>support or restraint</note> to such great revolutions.

When the *religion* formerly received is torn apart by discords, and when the holiness of the professors <note>those who practice or lead the religion</note> of *religion* has decayed and is full of scandal—and if, at the same time, the age is stupid, ignorant, and barbarous—you may suspect <note>original: "doubt"</note> the springing up of a *new sect*. This is especially likely if there should also arise any extravagant <note>eccentric or wandering from the norm</note> and strange spirit...

If a *new sect* does not have two properties, do not fear it, for it will not spread. The one is the supplanting <term>supplanting: the act of replacing or overthrowing</term> or the opposing of established authority, for nothing is more popular than that. The other is the giving of permission for pleasures and a voluptuous life.

As for *speculative heresies* (such as the *Arians* <note>original: Arrians; followers of Arius in the 4th century who challenged the nature of the Trinity</note> in *ancient times*, and now the *Arminians* <note>followers of Jacobus Arminius, whose theological views on grace and free will were a major contemporary controversy in Bacon's time</note>), though they work mightily upon men’s wits, they do not produce any great alterations in states, except it be by the help of *civil occasions* <note>original: Civill Occasions; meaning political opportunities or social unrest</note>. 

There are three ways for the planting of *new sects*: by the power of *signs and miracles*; by the eloquence and *wisdom of speech and persuasion*; and by the *sword*. As for *martyrdoms*, I count them among *miracles* because they seem to exceed the strength of human nature; and I may say the same of *exceptional and admirable holiness of life*. # Of Vicissitude of Things.

...the far Southern people have [rarely] invaded the Northern, but rather the opposite. By this it is clear that the *northern regions* of the world are naturally more martial <note>warlike</note> regions. This may be due to the stars of that hemisphere; or because of the great continents that exist in the *North*—whereas the *Southern part*, as far as is known, is almost entirely sea; or (as is most apparent) because of the cold of the *northern parts*. It is this cold which, even without the aid of military discipline, makes bodies hardier and courage warmer.

Upon the *breaking and shattering* of a great *state or empire*, you can be certain that *wars* will follow. For great empires, while they stand, enervate <note>weaken or drain of strength</note> and destroy the military power of the native peoples they have subdued, who then rely entirely on the empire's own protective forces. When those central forces also fail, everything goes to ruin, and they become prey for others. This is what happened during the decay of the *Roman Empire*, and likewise in the empire of *Germany* <note>original: "Almaigne," referring here to the Holy Roman Empire</note> after *Charlemagne* <note>original: "Charles the Great"</note>, when "every bird took a feather" <note>an idiom suggesting that local lords and smaller states each seized a piece of the collapsing empire's power and territory</note>. The great *acquisitions* and *unions* of *kingdoms* also stir up *wars*. For when a state grows to an *overwhelming power*, it is like a great flood that is sure to overflow. This has been seen in the states of *Rome, Turkey, Spain*, and others. Observe that when the world has the fewest *barbarian peoples*—or only those who generally will not marry or reproduce unless they know they have the means to live (as is the case almost everywhere today, except for *Tartary* <note>original: "Tartary"; a historical name for Northern and Central Asia inhabited by Turkic and Mongol peoples</note>)—there is no danger of "inundations" <term>mass migrations or invasions</term> of people.

But when there are *great shoals of people* <note>original: "great Shoales of People"; meaning vast, crowded swarms of populations</note> who continue to multiply without foreseeing the means of life and sustenance, it is necessary that once every century or two, they discharge a portion of their people onto other nations. The ancient *northern people* used to do this by drawing lots, casting lots to decide which group should stay at home and which should seek their fortunes elsewhere. When a *warlike* state grows soft and *effeminate* <note>In this historical context, "effeminate" refers to a loss of martial discipline, vigor, or "manly" readiness for war</note>, they can be certain that a *war* is coming. ## Of Vicissitude of Things.

As for *weapons*, they hardly fall under fixed rules or observation; yet we see that even they have their own *cycles* <note>original: "returnes"</note> and *vicissitudes* <note>vicissitude: the natural change or mutation from one thing to another</note>. For it is certain that <term>ordnance</term> <note>artillery or heavy guns</note> was known in the city of the *Oxidrakes* in *India*; it was this which the *Macedonians* called thunder, lightning, and magic. And it is well known that the use of <term>ordnance</term> has existed in *China* for over 2,000 years. 

The qualities of *weapons* and their improvement consist of: 

First, the ability to strike from a distance <note>original: "fetching a far off"</note>, for that outruns the danger, as is seen with <term>ordnance</term> and <term>muskets</term> <note>early handheld firearms</note>. 

Secondly, the strength of the impact <note>original: "percussion"</note>, in which <term>ordnance</term> likewise exceeds all <term>arietations</term> <note>the use of battering rams (from the Latin "aries" for ram)</note> and other ancient inventions. 

The third is their convenience of use: such as being functional in all weather, having a carriage that is light and manageable, and similar advantages.

## Of Vicissitude of Things.

First, *men* relied extremely upon *numbers*. They also based their *wars* upon *main force* <note>original: "Maine Force"; raw physical strength or brute force</note> and *valor*, appointing specific days for *pitched fields* <note>set battles on pre-arranged ground</note> and so testing the outcome on an even match. At that time, they were more ignorant in *ranging* and *arraying* their *battles* <note>original: "Battailes"; here referring to the tactical formations and divisions of an army</note>. After a while, they grew to rely upon *numbers* that were competent rather than vast. They began to seek *advantages* of *place*, *cunning diversions*, and the like; and they became more skillful in the *ordering* of their *battles*.

In the *youth* of a state, *arms* flourish. In the *middle age* of a state, *learning* thrives, and then both of them flourish together for a time. In the *declining age* of a state, *mechanical arts* <note>original: "Mechanicall Arts"; manual crafts, engineering, and manufacturing</note> and *merchandise* <note>trade and commerce</note> prevail. 

*Learning* also has its infancy, when it is just beginning and almost childish. Then comes its youth, when it is luxuriant and juvenile. Then its strength of years, when it is solid and *reduced* <note>original: "Reduced"; meaning consolidated, refined, or brought into a systematic form</note>. And lastly, its old age, when it grows dry and exhausted. But it is not good to look too long upon these turning wheels of *vi-* <meta>catchword: cissitude,</meta>

<term>Reproduction</term> <note>original: "reproduction"</note> — A copy of an original work; in this case, a digital version of a physical book.
<term>Library book</term> — A book originally held in a physical library collection.
<term>Digitization</term> — The process of converting physical text and images into a digital format for computer use.
<term>Preservation</term> — The act of protecting and maintaining historical documents to prevent the loss of information over time.
<term>Universal access</term> — The goal of making information available to everyone, everywhere in the world.

<meta>This page begins the dedication of the work to George Villiers, 1st Duke of Buckingham (1592–1628), who was a favorite of both King James I and King Charles I.</meta>

<header>THE EPISTLE DEDICATORY</header>

### TO
# THE RIGHT
# HONORABLE MY
## VERY GOOD LORD THE DUKE
### of *Buckingham* his Grace, Lord
### High Admiral of England.

->EXCELLENT LORD.<-

Solomon says; *A good name is like a precious ointment* <note>Ecclesiastes 7:1. In the early modern period, expensive ointments and perfumes were symbols of status and lasting legacy.</note>; And I assure myself, such will your Grace's name be, with

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Posterity</meta>

<summary>This page is the opening of a formal dedication to the Duke of Buckingham, using a biblical proverb from Solomon to praise the Duke's reputation.</summary>
<keywords>Duke of Buckingham, George Villiers, Solomon, dedication, High Admiral of England, reputation</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Francis Bacon's *Essays*. The previous page (implied) likely began the address to the patron, the Duke of Buckingham. The catchword "last" on this page indicates the transition to the next page's first word.</meta>

<header>THE EPISTLE</header>

Posterity. For your fortune and merit both have been eminent. And you have planted things that are likely to last. I do now publish my *Essays*; which, of all my other works, have been most current <note>original: "currant"; meaning widely circulated or popular</note>. For, as it seems, they come home to men’s business and bosoms <note>This famous phrase suggests the essays deal with practical daily affairs and deeply felt personal matters.</note>. I have enlarged them, both in number and weight <note>meaning depth or importance</note>; so that they are indeed a new work. I thought it therefore appropriate, given my affection and obligation to Your Grace <note>Bacon dedicated this 1625 edition to George Villiers, the Duke of Buckingham.</note>, to place your name before them, both in English and in Latin. For I believe that the Latin volume of them (being in the universal language <note>Latin was the international language of scholarship and diplomacy in Early Modern Europe.</note>) may

<summary>Francis Bacon dedicates the final, enlarged edition of his Essays to the Duke of Buckingham, noting that these writings are his most popular because they address practical and personal human experiences.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Essays, Duke of Buckingham, Latin translation, universal language, 17th-century literature</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication of Francis Bacon's "Essays" (1625 edition) to George Villiers, the Duke of Buckingham. It follows a discussion on the enduring nature of written works.</meta>

# DEDICATION

...last as long as books last. I dedicated my *Instauration* <note>original: *Instauratio Magna*, Bacon's ambitious project to reorganize and renew all human knowledge</note> to the King; my *History of Henry VII* (which I have now also translated into Latin) and my sections on *Natural History* to the Prince <note>the future King Charles I</note>; and these <note>referring to this collection of Essays</note> I dedicate to your Grace, being the best fruits that I could yield from the good harvest which God gives to my pen and my labors. May God lead your Grace by the hand.

->*Your Grace's most obliged and*<-
->*faithful servant,*<-

->FRANCIS ST. ALBAN <note>Francis Bacon, who held the title Viscount St. Alban</note>.<-

<summary>Francis Bacon concludes the dedication of his Essays to the Duke of Buckingham, situating the work alongside his other major publications dedicated to King James I and Prince Charles.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Duke of Buckingham, Henry VII, Instauration, Natural History, book dedication, 17th-century literature</keywords>

# Of Truth.
## I.

"What is truth?" said jesting Pilate, and would not stay for an answer. Certainly there are those who delight in giddiness <note>original: "Giddinesse"; here meaning a restless instability of mind or a refusal to settle on one opinion</note>, and count it a form of bondage to fix a belief, desiring free will in thinking as well as in acting. And though the sects of philosophers of that kind <note>Bacon refers to the ancient Skeptics, such as the Pyrrhonists, who argued that certain knowledge is impossible.</note> are gone, there still remain certain rambling wits <note>original: "discoursing Wits"; intellectuals who enjoy aimless talk and debate for its own sake</note> who are of the same temperament, though there is not as much vigor <note>original: "Bloud" (blood)</note> in them as there was in those of the ancients. But it is not

<summary>Francis Bacon begins his essay by discussing the nature of truth, noting that many people—like Pontius Pilate—lack the patience or desire to find it, preferring instead the freedom of intellectual inconsistency.</summary>

# Of Truth.

It is not only the difficulty and labor that men take in finding out the **Truth**, nor again that once it is found, it imposes itself <note>original: "imposeth"; meaning it restricts or dictates one's thoughts</note> upon men’s thoughts, that brings **Lies** into favor; rather, it is a natural, though corrupt, love of the **Lie** itself. 

One of the later school of the **Grecians** <note>Likely referring to the skeptic philosopher Lucian of Samosata</note> examines the matter and is at a loss to think what there could be in it that makes men love **Lies**, when they bring neither pleasure, as with **Poets**, nor advantage, as with the **Merchant**, but are told for the **Lie's** sake. 

But I cannot say why: this same **Truth** is a naked and open daylight that does not show the masks, mummeries <note>term: "mummeries" — ridiculous ceremonies, masked performances, or hypocritical shows</note>, and triumphs of the world half so stately or elegantly as candle-lights do. **Truth** may perhaps reach the price of a pearl, which shows best by day, but it will not rise to the price of a diamond or carbuncle <note>term: "carbuncle" — a deep red gemstone, such as a garnet, known for its fiery reflection</note> that shows best in varied lights. 

A mixture of a **Lie** always adds pleasure. Does any man doubt that if vain opinions, flattering hopes, 

<summary>Bacon argues that people often prefer lies to truth not just because truth is hard to find, but because of a natural human inclination toward the "varied lights" of falsehood over the "naked daylight" of truth.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Truth, Lies, Philosophy, Human Nature, Grecians, Poetry, Perception</keywords>

# Of Truth.

[Vain] hopes, false valuations, imaginations as one would [wish], and the like; but [if these were removed] it would leave the minds of many men as poor, shrunken things—full of melancholy and indisposition, and unpleasing to themselves. One of the Church Fathers, with great severity, called poetry "the wine of demons" <note>original: *Vinum Daemonum*; this phrase is often attributed to St. Augustine or St. Jerome, reflecting the early Church's suspicion of secular fiction</note>, because it fills the imagination, yet it does so only with the shadow of a *lie*. 

But it is not the *lie* that merely passes through the mind, but the *lie* that sinks in and settles there, that does the harm we spoke of before. However, even if these things are true in men's depraved judgments and affections, *Truth*—which alone can judge itself—teaches us that the inquiry after *truth* (which is the wooing of it), the knowledge of *truth* (which is the presence of it), and the belief in *truth* (which is the enjoying of it) is the sovereign good of human nature. 

The first thing God created in the works of the Days was the light of the senses; the last was the light of reason; and His Sabbath work, ever since, is the <meta>The catchword "Illumi-" indicates the next page begins with "Illumination."</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that while human minds often prefer the comfort of illusions, the pursuit, knowledge, and belief in Truth constitute the highest form of human existence, mirroring God's own creative process from physical light to intellectual reason.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Truth, poetry, Vinum Daemonum, reason, sovereignty of truth, creation allegory</keywords>

...illumination of His Spirit. First, He breathed light upon the face of the matter, or chaos; then He breathed light into the face of man; and He still breathes and inspires light into the faces of His chosen. The poet <note>original: "The Poet"; referring to the Roman poet Lucretius</note> who brought beauty to that sect <note>original: "the Sect"; referring to the Epicureans</note> which was otherwise inferior to the rest, yet says excellently well:

> *It is a pleasure to stand upon the shore and to see ships tossed upon the sea; a pleasure to stand in the window of a castle and to see a battle, and the fortunes <note>original: "Aduentures"</note> thereof, below. But no pleasure is comparable to standing upon the vantage ground of truth—a hill that cannot be commanded <term>commanded: in a military sense, a height that cannot be overlooked or dominated by higher ground</term>, and where the air is always clear and serene—and to see the errors, and wanderings, and mists, and tempests in the valley below.*

However, this must always be done so that this prospect is viewed with pity, and not with swelling <note>original: "Swelling"; meaning arrogance or vanity</note> or pride. Certainly, it is heaven upon earth to have a man’s mind move in charity, rest in providence, and turn upon the poles of truth.

There is no vice that covers a man with so much shame as to be found false and perfidious. And therefore Montaigne <note>original: "Mountaigny"</note> says aptly when he inquired why the accusation of a lie should be such a disgrace and such an odious charge. He says, "If it is well weighed, to say that a man lies is as much as to say that he is brave toward God and a coward toward men." <note>Bacon refers to Michel de Montaigne’s Essays (Book 2, Chapter 18), where Montaigne quotes Plutarch.</note> For a lie faces God and shrinks from man.
