# Of Death.
## II.

**M**en fear *Death*, as children fear going into the dark; and just as that natural fear in children is increased by tales, so is the other. Certainly, the contemplation of *Death* as the *wages of sin* <note>original: "wages of sinne"; a reference to Romans 6:23, "For the wages of sin is death."</note> and the passage to another world is holy and religious; but the fear of it as a tribute due to nature is weak. Yet in religious meditations, there is sometimes a mixture of vanity and superstition. You shall read in some of the friars' books of *Mortification* <term>mortification: the practice of self-denial or physical discipline to overcome sin and spiritualize the mind</term> that a man should think to himself what the pain is if he has even the tip of his finger pressed or tortured;

<summary>In this opening to his essay on death, Francis Bacon compares the fear of dying to a child's fear of the dark and argues that while death is a serious spiritual matter, fearing it as a natural process is a weakness.</summary>
<keywords>death, fear, religion, mortification, superstition, Francis Bacon, wages of sin</keywords>

<page-num>7</page-num>
# Of Death.

...tured; and by that, imagine what the pains of *Death* are, when the whole body is corrupted and dissolved; when many times, *Death* passes with less pain than the torture of a limb; for the most vital parts are not the most sensitive. And by him who spoke only as a philosopher and natural man <note>i.e., someone speaking from reason and observation rather than religious revelation</note>, it was well said: *The trappings of death terrify more than death itself* <note>original Latin: "Pompa Mortis magis terret, quàm Mors ipsa"</note>. Groans and convulsions, a discolored face, friends weeping, mourning clothes <note>original: "Blackes"</note>, funeral rites <note>original: "Obsequies"</note>, and the like, make *Death* appear terrible. It is worth observing that there is no passion in the mind of man so weak that it does not overcome <note>original: "Mates"</note> and master the fear of *Death*. And therefore *Death* is not such a terrible enemy when a man has so many attendants around him that can win the combat against it. *Revenge* triumphs over *Death*; *Love* slights it; *Honor* aspires to it; *Grief* flies to it; *Fear* anticipates <note>original: "pre-occupateth"</note> it. Nay, we read that after *Otho* the Emperor had slain himself, pity (which is the tenderest of affections) provoked...

<summary>The author argues that the fear of death is often greater than the physical pain of dying, as the "trappings" and rituals of mourning are more frightening than the event itself. He notes that various human passions, such as love and honor, are strong enough to overcome the natural fear of death.</summary>
<keywords>Death, stoicism, Seneca, mourning rituals, human passions, Emperor Otho, philosophy of mortality</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>

-> # Of Death. <-

...many to die out of mere compassion for their sovereign, and as the truest sort of followers. Indeed, *Seneca* adds fastidiousness and satiety <note>original: "Niceneſſe & Saciety"; here meaning boredom or being fed up with life's repetitive nature</note>: "Think how long you have been doing the same things; a man may wish to die, not only because he is brave or miserable, but even because he is bored" <note>original: *Cogita quam diù eadem feceris ; Mori velle, non tantùm Fortis, aut Miſer, ſed etiam Faſtidioſus poteſt.*</note>. A person might choose to die even if they were neither brave nor miserable, simply from the weariness of doing the same thing over and over again.

It is no less worthy to observe how little change the approach of *Death* makes in noble spirits; for they appear to be the same men until the very last moment. *Augustus Caesar* died with a compliment: "Livia, mindful of our marriage, live on and farewell" <note>original: *Liuia, Coniugij noſtri memor, viue & vale.*</note>. *Tiberius* died in dissimulation <note>original: "dissimulation"; meaning deception or masking his true feelings</note>; as *Tacitus* says of him: "Now his strength and body were failing Tiberius, but not his power to deceive" <note>original: *Iam Tiberium Vires, & Corpus, non Diſſimulatio, deferebant.*</note>. *Vespasian* died with a joke; sitting upon the stool <note>A reference to the close-stool or toilet; Vespasian reportedly died of diarrhea and joked about the Roman practice of deifying emperors after death.</note>, he said, "I think I am becoming a god" <note>original: *Vt puto Deus fio.*</note>. *Galba* died with a pronouncement: "Strike, if it be for the good of the Roman people" <note>original: *Feri, ſi ex re ſit populi Romani*</note>, while offering up his neck. *Septimius Severus* died in a hurry to get things done: "Come here, if there is anything left for me to do" <note>original: *Adeſte, ſi quid mihi reſtat agendum.*</note>. And there are many similar examples.

Certainly, the *Stoics* <note>An ancient school of philosophy that taught the endurance of pain or hardship without display of feelings and without complaint.</note> spent too much effort on *Death*, and by their great preparations...

<summary>This page explores how boredom and loyalty can motivate death and highlights the composure of famous Roman emperors who maintained their distinct personalities and habits even in their final moments.</summary>

# Of Death.

...preparations, made it appear more fearful. Better, he says, is he "who accounts the close of life as one of the benefits of Nature" <note>original Latin: *Qui Finem Vitæ extremum inter Munera ponat Naturæ*—from the Roman poet Juvenal's *Satires*.</note>. It is as natural to die as it is to be born; and to a little infant, perhaps, the one is as painful as the other. 

He who dies in the midst of an earnest pursuit is like one who is wounded in the heat of battle <note>original: "hot blood"</note>; who, for the time, scarcely feels the hurt. Therefore, a mind fixed and bent upon something that is good averts the <term>pains</term> <note>original: "dolors"</note> of death. But above all, believe it: the sweetest song is the *Nunc dimittis* <note>Latin: "Now let [your servant] depart." A reference to the Song of Simeon in the Gospel of Luke, signifying the peace of a person who has seen their life's purpose fulfilled.</note>, when a man has attained worthy goals and expectations. Death also has this effect: it opens the gate to good fame and extinguishes envy.

# Of Unity in Religion.
## III.

**R** *eligion* being the chief bond <note>original: "Band"</note> of human society, it is a fortunate thing when religion itself is well contained within the true bond of *unity*. Quarrels and divisions about *religion* were evils unknown to the *pagans* <note>original: "Heathen"</note>. The reason was that the *religion* of the *pagans* consisted more in *rites* and *ceremonies* than in any constant *belief*. For you can imagine what kind of *faith* theirs was when the chief *teachers* <note>original: "Doctors," referring to learned scholars or theologians</note> and *authorities* <note>original: "Fathers"</note> of their church were the *poets*. But the true God has this attribute: that he is a *jealous God*. Therefore, his worship and *religion* will tolerate no *mixture* or *partner*. We shall therefore say a few words concerning the *uni-*

<summary>Francis Bacon begins his essay by arguing that while pagan religions were based on ritual and lacked doctrinal conflict, the Judeo-Christian tradition demands exclusive devotion and internal unity.</summary>
<keywords>religion, unity, paganism, theology, poetry, "jealous God", social stability</keywords>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Of Unity in Religion</header>
<sig>C 2</sig>

# Of Unity in Religion

...unity of the Church: *What are its fruits; what are its boundaries; and what are the means to achieve it?*

*The fruits of unity* (after the pleasing of God, which is the most important thing of all) are twofold: one directed toward those *outside the Church*, and the other toward those *within it*. Regarding the former: it is certain that heresies and schisms <note>schisms: formal divisions or splits within a religious group</note> are the greatest scandals of all, even more so than the corruption of morals. For just as in the physical body, a wound or a <term>solution of continuity</term> <note>original: "Solution of Continuity"; a medical term for a break in the physical connection of tissues, like a deep cut</note> is worse than a corrupt "humor" <note>humors: in ancient medicine, the bodily fluids believed to control health and temperament</note>, the same is true in spiritual matters. Thus, nothing keeps people out of the Church, or drives people away from it, as much as a breach of *unity*. Therefore, whenever things reach the point where one says, *Ecce in Deserto* <note>original Latin: "Behold, he is in the desert" (Matthew 24:26)</note>, and another says, *Ecce in penetralibus* <note>original Latin: "Behold, he is in the inner rooms" (Matthew 24:26)</note>—that is, when some seek Christ in the <term>conventicles</term> <note>conventicles: secret or unauthorized religious meetings</note> of heretics, and others in a purely outward appearance of a church—that voice needs to sound continually in people's ears: *Nolite exire*, *do not go out*.

<summary>The author argues that religious unity is essential because divisions and heresies are more damaging to the Church's reputation than moral corruption, ultimately driving people away from the faith.</summary>
<keywords>Unity, Schism, Heresy, Church Scandal, Catholicity, Religious Division, Francis Bacon</keywords>

# Of Unity in Religion

The Apostle to the Gentiles <note>St. Paul (original: "Doctor of the Gentiles")</note>—the nature of whose calling drew him to have a special concern for those *outside* the faith—says: "*If a non-believer <note>original: "Heathen"</note> comes in and hears you speaking with different tongues, will he not say that you are mad?*" And certainly, it is little better when atheists and profane people hear of so many conflicting and contrary opinions in religion; it turns them away from the Church and makes them "sit down in the seat of the scornful" <note>A reference to Psalm 1:1, meaning those who mock or cynicaly deride religion</note>. It is a trivial thing to cite in such a serious matter, yet it expresses the distorted nature of the situation well: there is a master of satire <note>original: "Master of Scoffing," likely referring to François Rabelais (c. 1494–1553)</note> who, in his catalog of books for a fictional library, includes this book title: *The Morris Dance of Heretics.* <note>The Morris dance was a traditional English folk dance involving choreographed, often frantic movements; the title mocks the various sects as if they were performing a chaotic dance.</note> For indeed, every sect has its own different posture or strange gesture <note>original: "Cringe"</note>, which cannot help but provoke mockery in worldly people and cynical politicians <note>original: "Depraved Politickes"</note>, who are naturally inclined to despise holy things.

As for the benefits <note>original: "Fruit"</note> for those who are inside the faith, the result is peace, which contains...

# Of Unity in Religion.

...infinite blessings: it establishes faith; it kindles charity. The outward peace of the church distills into peace of conscience. And it turns the labors of writing and reading controversies into treatises on mortification <term>spiritual self-discipline or the subduing of one's passions</term> and devotion.

Concerning <unclear>the</unclear> bounds of unity, where they are truly placed is exceedingly important. There appear to be two extremes. To certain *Zealots* <term>original: "Zelants"; meaning those with excessive or fanatical religious fervor</term>, all talk of pacification <term>making peace or reconciliation</term> is hateful. *“Is it peace, Jehu? What have you to do with peace? Turn behind me.”* <note>A reference to 2 Kings 9:18. In this context, it suggests that partisan believers care more about loyalty to their "party" than about actual peace.</note> To them, peace is not the issue, but rather following a leader and a party. On the other hand, certain *Laodiceans* <term>referring to the "lukewarm" church in Revelation 3:14-16; people who are indifferent to religious principles</term> and lukewarm persons think they may accommodate points of <unclear>religion</unclear> by middle ways, by taking parts of both sides, and by clever reconciliations—as if they would make an arbitration <term>original: "Arbitrement"; a legal settlement or judgment</term> between God and man. Both these extremes are to be avoided. This will be achieved if the "League of Christians," written by our Savior himself, were understood soundly and plainly in its two seemingly contradictory clauses:

<header>Of Unity in Religion</header>

...plainly expounded: *He who is not with us is against us;* and again, *He who is not against us is with us.* <note>original: "He that is not with vs, is against vs : And againe ; He that is not against vs, is with vs :" - These are two seemingly contradictory sayings of Jesus found in the Gospels (Matthew 12:30 and Mark 9:40). Bacon uses them to show that while some truths are absolute, there is also room for neutrality or diversity in non-essentials.</note> That is, if the fundamental points and matters of substance in religion were truly discerned and distinguished from points that are not strictly matters of faith, but rather of opinion, order, or good intention. This is a thing that may seem to many a trivial matter that has been done already; but if it were done less partially, it would be embraced more generally.

Regarding this, I can offer only this advice, according to my own limited perspective <note>original: "small Modell"</note>. Men should take care not to tear God’s Church apart with two kinds of controversies. The first is when the matter being debated is too small and light—not worth the heat and strife it causes—ignited only by a spirit of contradiction. For, as it is noted by one of the Church Fathers: *Christ’s coat, indeed, had no seam; but the Church’s garment had many colors.* <note>original: "Christs Coat... no seame... Churches Vesture... diuers colours" - Bacon references the seamless robe of Jesus (John 19:23) as a symbol of spiritual unity, while the "garment of many colors" (referencing Psalm 45:14) represents the diversity of the Church's various traditions.</note> On this point he says, *In veste varietas sit, Scissura non sit* [Let there be variety in the garment, but let there be no tear]. <note>original: "In veste varietas sit, Scissura non sit" - A Latin phrase often attributed to St. Augustine or St. Bernard of Clairvaux, arguing that the Church can have diverse customs without breaking its essential unity.</note> Unity and uniformity are two different things. The other kind of controversy is when the matter of...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that religious conflict can be avoided by distinguishing between essential "points of faith" and minor "points of opinion." He cautions against causing schisms over trivial matters, famously noting that while the spirit of the Church is one, its outward practices may be diverse.</summary>

# Of Unity in Religion

The point under controversy is great, but it is driven to an over-great subtlety and obscurity, so that it becomes a thing rather clever <note>original: "ingenious"</note> than substantial. A person of judgment and understanding shall sometimes hear ignorant men differ, and know well within himself that those who so differ mean one thing, and yet they themselves would never agree. And if it happens so in that distance of judgment which is between man and man, shall we not think that God above, who knows the heart, discerns that frail men, in some of their contradictions, intend the same thing and accepts both? 

The nature of such controversies is excellently expressed by *St. Paul* in the warning and precept that he gives concerning the same: "Avoid profane novelties of words, and oppositions of knowledge falsely so called" <note>original Latin: "Devita profanas vocum Novitates, & Oppositiones falsi Nominis Scientiæ." From 1 Timothy 6:20.</note>. Men create oppositions which do not exist, and put them into new terms so fixed that, whereas the meaning ought to govern the term, the term in effect

<summary>Bacon argues that many religious disagreements are merely verbal disputes caused by rigid terminology, suggesting that God recognizes the shared intent behind human contradictions.</summary>

<page-num>16</page-num>

# Of Unity in Religion.

...governs the meaning. There are also two false *peaces*, or *unities*: the one, when the *peace* is grounded only upon an implicit ignorance, for all colors will look the same in the *dark*; the other, when it is patched together through a direct acceptance of contradictions in fundamental points. For truth and falsehood, in such things, are like the *iron and clay in the toes of Nebuchadnezzar’s image* <note>original: "Nabucadnezars Image"; a reference to the Book of Daniel in the Bible, symbolizing a structure made of incompatible materials that can stay together but cannot truly fuse.</note>. They may cling together, but they will not incorporate.

Concerning the *means of procuring unity*: men must beware that in the procuring, or <term>fortifying</term> <note>original: "Muniting"</note> of *religious unity*, they do not dissolve and deface the laws of charity and of human society. There are two "swords" among Christians: the spiritual and the temporal <note>The "spiritual sword" refers to the authority of the Church, while the "temporal sword" refers to the power of the state.</note>. Both have their proper office and place in the maintenance of *religion*. But we may not take up the "third sword," which is Muhammad's sword <note>original: "Mahomets Sword"; Bacon uses this as a metaphor for spreading faith through military conquest or violence.</note>, or anything like it—that is, to propagate *religion* by wars or by bloody <note>original: "Sanguinary"</note> persecutions to force consciences, except it be in cases of <term>public scandal</term> <note>original: "Ouert Scandall"; this refers to outward actions that openly disrupt social order or moral standards, as opposed to private beliefs.</note>,

<summary>Bacon identifies two types of false religious unity—those based on ignorance or the mixing of contradictory fundamental truths—and warns against using violence or "the third sword" to enforce religious conformity.</summary>

# Of Revenge.

## IV.

Revenge is a kind of wild justice, which, the more man's nature runs toward it, the more the law ought to weed it out. For as for the first wrong, it only offends the law; but the revenge of that wrong puts the law out of its office <note>meaning revenge usurps the role and authority of the legal system</note>. Certainly, in taking *revenge*, a man is only even with his enemy; but in passing it over, he is superior, for it is a prince's role to pardon. And *Solomon*, I am sure, says, "*It is the glory of a man to pass by an offense*" <note>Proverbs 19:11</note>. That which

<header>Of Revenge.</header>

## Of Revenge.

...is past, is gone, and irrevocable. And wise men have enough to do with things present and to come; therefore, those who labor over past matters do but trifle with themselves. No man does a wrong merely for the sake of doing wrong, but rather to gain for himself profit, pleasure, honor, or something similar. Therefore, why should I be angry with a man for loving himself better than me? And if any man should do wrong merely out of an ill nature, why, it is just like the thorn or the briar, which prick and scratch because they can do nothing else. 

The most tolerable sort of *revenge* is for those wrongs for which there is no law to provide a remedy. But even then, let a man take care that the *revenge* is of a kind that the law cannot punish; otherwise, a man's enemy is still one step ahead, and it is "two for one" <note>Meaning the person seeking revenge suffers twice: first from the original injury, and second from the legal punishment for their retaliation.</note>. 

Some, when they take *revenge*, desire that the other party should know where the blow comes from. This is the more noble <note>original: "Generous," meaning honorable or characteristic of noble spirit.</note> approach. For the pleasure seems to lie not so much in causing the injury as in making the party repent. But base and...

## Of Revenge.

Public revenges are, for the most part, successful <note>original: "fortunate," meaning bringing a favorable outcome for the state</note>; such as the revenge for the death of *Caesar*; for the death of *Pertinax* <note>Roman Emperor assassinated in 193 AD, whose death was avenged by Septimius Severus</note>; for the death of *Henry III* of *France*; and many more. # Of Adversity.

## V.

**I**t was a lofty saying of Seneca (after the manner of the Stoics): "The good things which belong to prosperity are to be wished for, but the good things that belong to adversity are to be admired" <note>original Latin: *Bona Rerum Secundarum, Optabilia; Adversarum, Mirabilia*</note>. But to speak more moderately <note>original: "in a Meane"</note>: the virtue of *prosperity* is *temperance*; the virtue of *adversity* is *fortitude*, which in moral philosophy is the more heroic virtue. *Prosperity* is the blessing of the Old Testament; *adversity* is the blessing of the New, which carries the greater benediction and the clearer revelation of God's favor. # Of Adversity

# Of Simulation
## And
# Dissimulation.
### VI.

**D**issimulation is but a weak kind of strategy <note>original: "Policy"</note> or wisdom; for it requires a strong mind <note>original: "Wit"</note> and a strong heart to know when to tell the truth and to actually do it. Therefore, it is the weaker sort of politicians <note>original: "Politicks"</note> who are the greatest dissemblers.

Tacitus <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a Roman historian known for his analysis of power.</note> says that <term>Livia</term> <note>Livia Drusilla, wife of Augustus and mother of Tiberius.</note> matched well with the skills of her husband and the dissimulation of her son—attributing skills or strategy to <term>Augustus</term> and dissimulation to <term>Tiberius</term>. And again, when <term>Mucianus</term> <note>A general and advisor who helped Vespasian come to power.</note> encourages <term>Vespasian</term> to take up arms against <term>Vitellius</term>, he says: *We are not rising up against the piercing judgment of Augustus, nor the extreme caution or secrecy <note>original: "Closenesse"</note> of Tiberius.* These properties

<summary>In this opening to his sixth essay, Francis Bacon argues that dissimulation (hiding the truth) is a lesser form of wisdom used by those who lack the strength for genuine transparency. He cites the Roman Emperors Augustus and Tiberius as examples of the distinction between strategic skill and mere secrecy.</summary>

The knowledge of <term>Arts</term> <note>original: "Arts" – here meaning strategic skills or technical expertise.</note> or <term>Policy</term> <note>original: "Policy" – political strategy or prudence.</note>, and **Dissimulation** or **Secrecy**, are indeed separate habits and abilities, and they should be distinguished from one another. For if a man has such sharp judgment that he can discern which things should be revealed, which should be kept secret, and which should be shown in "half-lights" (partially revealed), as well as to whom and when—which are indeed the "arts of state" and "arts of life," as **Tacitus** <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), a Roman historian and politician known for his analysis of power.</note> well calls them—then a habit of constant **dissimulation** is actually a hindrance and a sign of weakness. 

But if a man cannot achieve that level of judgment, then his only option is to be generally secretive and a **dissembler**. For when a man cannot choose or adjust his approach in specific situations, it is best for him to take the safest and most cautious path in general. It is like someone who cannot see well walking slowly and carefully. 

Certainly, the most capable men who ever lived have all possessed an openness and frankness in their dealings, along with a reputation for reliability and truthfulness. However, they were like horses that are well-managed; for they knew very well when to stop or turn. And at those times, when they

<summary>Bacon argues that while the most capable leaders are generally open and honest, they possess the strategic judgment to know exactly when to be secretive, whereas less capable men must rely on constant secrecy for safety.</summary>

If they used it then, it so happened that their previous reputation for good faith and clear dealing made them almost invisible <note>In this context, "invisible" means their deception was so well-hidden by their reputation that it was impossible to detect</note>.

There are three degrees of this hiding and veiling of oneself:

1.  The first is **closeness, reservation, and secrecy**, when a man leaves himself unobserved, or gives no opening for others to discover what he truly is.
2.  The second is **dissimulation** in the negative sense, when a man gives signs and indications that he is not what he actually is.
3.  The third is **simulation** <note>original: "Simulation"; an active form of deception where one pretends to be or have something they do not</note> in the affirmative sense, when a man industriously and explicitly feigns and pretends to be what he is not.

As for the first of these, **secrecy**: it is indeed the virtue of a confessor <note>original: "Confessour"; here referring to a priest who hears confessions and is sworn to silence</note>. And assuredly, the secret man hears many confessions; for who will open themselves up to a blabber or a babbler? But if a man is thought to be secret, it invites discovery; just as a denser <note>original: "Close Aire"</note> air sucks in the more open air. And as in religious confession, the revealing of secrets is not for worldly use, but for the

### Of Simulation

Just as people find relief for their hearts by talking, so *secret men* come to the knowledge of many things in that way; for men tend to unburden their minds to them rather than intentionally sharing information. In a few words, *mysteries* are rightfully kept by *secrecy*. Besides (to tell the truth), *nakedness* is unappealing in the mind as well as in the body; it adds a significant amount of respect to a person's manners and actions if they are not completely transparent. As for big talkers and <term>futile: from the Latin 'futilis', meaning 'leaky' or 'pouring out easily'</term> persons, they are commonly vain and gullible as well. For he who talks about what he knows will also talk about what he does not know. Therefore, let it be established: *that a habit of secrecy is both prudent and moral.*

In this regard, it is good for a man's face to give his tongue leave to speak. For revealing one's self through the movements of one's facial expressions is a great weakness and a betrayal, especially since these expressions are often more noticed and believed than a person's words.

As for the second point, which is *dissimulation* <note>original: "Dissimulation" — here meaning the act of disguising or hiding what one truly is or thinks.</note>: it often follows from secrecy out of necessity. So much so, that he who wishes to be secret...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that a habit of secrecy is both a practical and moral advantage, noting that a "transparent" mind is as unseemly as a naked body and that facial expressions can betray one's thoughts more than words.</summary>

For men are too cunning to allow a man to maintain a neutral bearing <note>original: "indifferent carriage"</note> between the two, and to remain *secret* without tipping the scales <note>original: "Swaying the Ballance"</note> on either side. They will so beset a man with questions, draw him out, and pick information out of him, that unless he maintains an absurd silence, he must show an inclination one way; or, if he does not, they will learn as much from his silence as from his speech. As for *equivocations* <note>original: "Equiuocations"; using ambiguous language to hide the truth</note> or oracular <note>original: "Oraculous"; ambiguous or cryptic</note> speeches, they cannot last long. So it is that no man can be *secret* unless he gives himself a little room for *dissimulation*, which is, as it were, merely the hem or train <note>original: "Skirts or Traine"; the outer edges or following parts of a garment</note> of *secrecy*.

<header>Of Simulation</header>

The three great advantages of *simulation and dissimulation* are: First, to lull opposition to sleep and to take others by surprise. For when a man's intentions are published, it is an alarm to call up all those who are against them. The second is to reserve for oneself a fair retreat; for if a man commits himself by a clear declaration, he must either follow through or suffer defeat <note>original: "take a fall"</note>. The third is to better discover the mind of another. For men will hardly show themselves to be against someone who opens himself up; instead, they will politely <note>original: "faire"</note> let him go on, and turn their freedom of speech into freedom of thought. And therefore, it is a shrewd proverb of the Spaniard: *Tell a lie, and find a truth*. As if there were no way of discovery except by *simulation*. There are also three disadvantages to balance the scale. The first is that *simulation* and *dissimulation* commonly carry with them an appearance of fearfulness <note>meaning: timidity or cowardice</note>, which in a—

<summary>Bacon outlines the three strategic benefits of using simulation and dissimulation: catching opponents off-guard, maintaining an exit strategy, and gaining insight into others' thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>simulation, dissimulation, strategy, secrecy, Spanish proverb, diplomacy, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the disadvantages of simulation and dissimulation, concluding the essay "Of Simulation and Dissimulation."</meta>

<header>and Dissimulation.</header>

[A]ny business, does spoil the feathers <note>Bacon uses the metaphor of an arrow's feathers being ruffled, which prevents a straight flight.</note> for flying straight <note>original: "round flying"; meaning to fly swiftly and directly to a target</note> up to the mark. The second disadvantage is that it puzzles and perplexes the thoughts <note>original: "conceits"</note> of many who perhaps would otherwise cooperate with him, and it makes a man walk almost alone toward his own ends. The third and greatest is that it deprives a man of one of the most principal instruments for action, which is ***Trust*** and ***Belief***. The best composition and temperament <note>original: "temperature"; referring to the balanced mixture of a person's qualities</note> is: to have ***Openness*** in reputation and opinion; ***Secrecy*** in habit; ***Dissimulation*** in seasonable use; and a power to feign, if there is no other remedy.

# Of Parents and
## Children.
### VII.

The joys of *parents* are secret, and so are their griefs and fears. They cannot express <note>original: "utter"</note> the one, nor will they express the other. *Children* sweeten labors, but they make misfortunes more bitter; they increase the cares of life, but they mitigate the remembrance of death. Perpetuity <note>the state of lasting forever</note> through reproduction <note>original: "Generation"</note> is common to beasts, but memory, merit, and noble works are proper <note>unique or specific</note> to men. And surely a man shall see that the noblest works and foundations <note>Refers to the establishment of institutions, charities, or great public legacy projects.</note> have proceeded from *childless men*, who have sought to express the images of their minds where those of their bodies have failed. So, the care of posterity <note>future generations</note> is greatest in those who have no

<summary>Francis Bacon explores the emotional complexities of parenthood, arguing that while children provide comfort, the most significant intellectual and civic legacies often come from childless individuals.</summary>

# Of Parents and Children.

Posterity. Those who are the first founders <note>original: "Raisers"</note> of their families <note>original: "Houses"</note> are most indulgent toward their *children*. They view them not only as the continuation of their species, but as the continuation of their own work; and so they see them as both *children* and *creations* <note>original: "Creatures," meaning something created by their own efforts</note>.

The difference in affection that *parents* show toward their various *children* is often unequal and sometimes unjustified <note>original: "unworthy"</note>, especially on the part of the *mother*. As *Solomon* says: "*A wise son gladdens the father, but a foolish <note>original: "ungracious"</note> son shames the mother.*" One may see, in a house full of *children*, that one or two of the eldest are respected, while the youngest are spoiled <note>original: "made wantons"</note>. Meanwhile, those in the middle are often somewhat forgotten, yet they frequently turn out to be the best. 

Stinginess <note>original: "Illiberality"</note> on the part of *parents* regarding the allowance given to their *children* is a harmful error. It makes the children dishonorable <note>original: "base"</note> and acquaints them with trickery <note>original: "shifts"</note>. It causes them to associate with low <note>original: "mean"</note> company and makes them overindulge <note>original: "surfeit"</note> even more when they eventually come into wealth. Therefore, the result <note>original: "proof"</note> is best when parents keep...

The *Italians* make little distinction between *children* and *nephews* or near *kinsfolk*; so long as they are of the same stock <note>original: "of the Lumpe"; meaning part of the same family lineage or genetic group</note>, they do not care if they did not descend directly from their own bodies. And, to tell the truth, in nature it is much the same; so much so that we sometimes see a nephew resemble an *uncle* or a *kinsman* more than his own *parent*, depending on how the bloodlines mix. 

Let *parents* choose early <note>original: "betimes"</note> the vocations and careers they intend their children to take, for that is when they are most flexible. # Of Marriage
## And Single Life.
### VIII.

---

**H**E who has a *Wife* and *Children* has given hostages to *Fortune*; for they are obstacles to great undertakings, whether of *Virtue* or *Mischief*. Certainly, the best works, and those of greatest merit for the public, have proceeded from *unmarried* or *childless men*, who have, in both their affection and their means, married and endowed the public. Yet it is only reasonable that those who have *children* should have the greatest care for future times, to which they know they must transmit their dearest pledges <note>original: "pledges" — referring to children as a person's stake in the future</note>. Nay more, there are some foolish, rich, covetous men who take a pride in having no *children*, so that they may be thought to be so much the richer. For perhaps they have heard some talk: "*Such a one is a very rich man*"; and another objects to it: "*Yes, but he has a great charge of children*"—as if children were a reduction of his riches. 

But the most ordinary cause of a *single life* is *liberty*, especially in certain self-pleasing and humorous <note>original: "humorous"; in the 17th century, this meant someone driven by their own whims, moods, or specific temperament rather than someone who is funny</note> minds, which are so sensitive to every restraint that they will go so far as to think their girdles and garters are bonds and shackles. 

*Unmarried men* are the best friends, the best masters, and the best servants, but not always the best subjects; for they are light to run away, and almost all fugitives are of that condition. # Of Envy.

## IX.

There are none of the *affections* <note>original: "affections"; here meaning intense emotions, passions, or states of mind</note> that have been noted to fascinate or bewitch, except *love* and *envy*. They both involve vehement wishes; they readily form themselves into imaginations and suggestions; and they are easily expressed through the eye, especially in the presence of their objects. These are the factors that contribute to "fascination" <note>original: "fascination"; referring to the ancient belief in the power of a look to cast a spell or "fasten" onto someone</note>, if such a thing exists. We see likewise that the Scripture calls *envy* "an evil eye" <note>See Matthew 20:15 or Mark 7:22</note>. Astrologers also call the harmful influences of the stars "evil aspects" <note>original: "affects"; in astrology, the specific angular relationship between planets</note>. Thus, there still seems to be an acknowledgment that the act of *envy* involves an "ejaculation" <note>original: "ejaculation"; in this context, a rapid darting out or emission of energy</note> or an irradiation from the eye. Indeed, some have been so inquisitive <note>original: "curious"</note> as to note that the times when the "stroke" or "percussion" <note>original: "percussion"; meaning a physical impact or striking, referring here to the perceived physical effect of an envious glance</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon begins his essay on envy by comparing it to love, noting that both are powerful passions capable of "fascinating" or bewitching others through the medium of the eye.</summary>

...the envious eye does the most harm when the person envied is seen in glory or triumph. This sharpens envy; and besides, at such times, the spirits <note>In the medical and philosophical theories of Bacon's time, "spirits" were thin, vaporous substances believed to connect the body and soul; they were thought to flow to the surface of the skin during moments of intense emotion or pride.</note> of the envied person come forth mostly into the outer parts of the body, as if to meet the blow.

But leaving these subtleties <note>original: "Curiosities"</note> (though they are not unworthy of consideration in the proper place), we will discuss what people are inclined to envy others, what people are *most subject to being envied themselves*, and *what the difference is between public and private envy.*

A man who has no virtue in himself always envies virtue in others. For men's minds will either feed upon their own good or upon the evil of others; and whoever lacks the one will prey upon the other. Anyone who is without hope of attaining another's virtue will seek to get on even footing <note>original: "come at even hand"</note> by depressing another's fortune.

## Of Envy.

...much of other men's matters, it cannot be because all that fuss <note>original: "Adoe"</note> concerns his own estate. Therefore, it must be that he takes a kind of playful pleasure in looking upon the fortunes of others. Neither can he who minds only his own business find much matter for *envy*. For *envy* is a wandering <note>original: "Gadding"</note> passion that walks the streets and does not stay at home; *no one is inquisitive without also being malevolent* <note>original Latin: "Non est curiosus, quin idem sit maleuolus"</note>.

Men of noble birth are noted to be *envious* toward "new men" <note>the newly wealthy or recently ennobled</note> when they rise. This is because the distance between them has changed; it is like an optical illusion <note>original: "deceipt of the Eye"</note>—when others move forward, they think they themselves are going backward.

Deformed persons, eunuchs, old men, and bastards are *envious*. For he who cannot possibly improve his own situation will do what he can to impair another's—unless these defects happen to fall upon a very brave and heroic nature, which intends to make his natural deficiencies <note>original: "Wants"</note> part of his honor. In that

The same is true of men who rise after calamities and misfortunes; for they are like men who have fallen out of favor with the times, and they think other men’s harms are a redemption for their own sufferings.

Those who desire to excel in too many matters, out of levity <note>frivolity or lack of serious intent</note> and vainglory, are always *envious*. They can never lack for reasons to be so, as it is impossible that they should not be surpassed by many people in at least one of those many areas. This was the character of *Hadrian* the Emperor, who mortally *envied* poets, painters, and artisans <note>original: "Artificers"</note> in works where he himself had a talent <note>original: "veine"</note> to excel.

People of noble blood are less envied as they *rise* in status, for it seems only right, given their birth. Furthermore, it does not seem as though much has been added to their fortunes; and *envy* is like sunbeams, which beat hotter upon a bank or steep rising ground than upon a flat plain. For the same reason, those who are promoted by degrees are less envied than those who are promoted suddenly and *all at once* <note>original: *per saltum*, literally "by a leap"</note>.

Those who have combined their honors with great labors <note>original: "Trauels," meaning travails or hard work</note>, cares, or perils are less subject to *envy*. For nothing increases envy more than an unnecessary and ambitious monopolizing <note>original: "ingrossing"</note> of business. And nothing extinguishes envy more than for a person of high rank to preserve all other subordinate officers in the full rights and privileges <note>original: "preheminences"</note> of their positions. For by that means, there are many screens between him and envy.

Above all, those most subject to *envy* are those who carry the greatness of their fortunes in an insolent and proud manner: being never content except when they are showing how great they are, either by outward pomp or by triumphing over all opposition or competition. # Of Envy.

---

A plain and open manner (provided it is without arrogance and vainglory) draws less *envy* than if it is done in a more crafty and cunning fashion. For in that course, a man does nothing but disavow fortune; he seems to be conscious of his own lack of worth, and only teaches others to *envy* him.

Finally, to conclude this part: as we said in the beginning, the act of *envy* had something in it of *witchcraft*; so there is no other cure for *envy* but the cure for *witchcraft*. And that is to remove the *lot* <note>original: "Lot"; in the context of folk magic or witchcraft, this refers to the spell, charm, or the specific object of the curse</note> (as they call it) and to lay it upon another. For this purpose, the wiser among great persons always bring onto the *stage* <note>Bacon frequently uses theatrical metaphors to describe public and political life</note> someone upon whom to divert <note>original: "derive"; used here in the sense of channeling or siphoning off a flow, like water</note> the *envy* that would otherwise fall upon themselves. Sometimes they divert it onto *ministers* and *servants*; sometimes upon *colleagues* and *associates*, and the like. For that role, there are never a lack of people of bold and enterprising <note>original: "Vndertaking"; meaning those who are eager to take on tasks, often with a sense of daring or ambition</note> natures, who—as long as they may have power and business—will take it at any cost.

# Of Envy

Now to speak of *Public Envy*. There is yet some good in *Public Envy*, whereas in *Private Envy*, there is none. For *Public Envy* is like an *Ostracism* <note>original: "Ostracisme"; a reference to the Ancient Athenian practice of voting to temporarily banish a citizen who was becoming too powerful</note>, which eclipses men when they grow too great. Therefore, it also serves as a bridle to great men, to keep them within bounds.

This *Envy*, derived from the Latin word *Invidia* <note>Latin: *invidia*, meaning jealousy or ill-will</note>, is known in modern languages by the name of *Discontentment*. We shall speak of this when handling the subject of *Sedition*. It is a disease in a state, similar to an *Infection*. For just as an *Infection* spreads upon that which is healthy and taints it, so when *Envy* has once entered a state, it slanders <note>original: "traduceth"</note> even the best actions and turns them into a bad odor. 

Therefore, little is gained by mixing in popular <note>original: "plausible," in the sense of actions deserving of applause or intended to please the public</note> actions. For doing so only suggests a weakness and a fear of *Envy*. This hurts the state all the more, just as is common with *Infections*: if you fear them, you bring them upon yourself.

This public *Envy* seems to strike chiefly upon principal officers or ministers,

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that "Public Envy" can serve as a political check on overly powerful individuals, though it acts like a contagious disease that can ruin a government's reputation regardless of its good deeds.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Public Envy, Ostracism, Invidia, Discontentment, Sedition, political stability, reputation</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "public envy" from the previous page, beginning mid-word with "ministers."</meta>

# **Of Envy**

...ministers, rather than upon kings and the estates <term>estates: refers here to the political state or body politic</term> themselves. But this is a certain rule: if the *envy* toward a minister is great when the cause of it within him is small, or if the *envy* is general—directed, in a sense, toward all the ministers of a state—then that *envy* (though hidden) is truly directed at the state itself.

And so much for *public envy* or *discontent*, and how it differs from *private envy*, which was discussed first.

We will add this, in general, concerning the affection <term>affection: a persistent emotion or state of mind</term> of *envy*: that of all other emotions, it is the most obsessive and continual. For other *emotions* are only triggered now and then. Therefore, it was well said, "Envy takes no holidays" <note>original Latin: *Invidia festos dies non agit*</note>. For it is always working against someone or another. 

It is also noted that *love* and *envy* make a person pine away, which other emotions do not, because they are not so constant. It is also the vilest and most depraved emotion; for this reason, it is the defining attribute of the Devil, who is called "*the envious man who sows tares* <term>tares: harmful weeds, likely darnel, that look like wheat when young</term> *amongst the wheat.*" <note>A reference to the biblical Parable of the Tares in Matthew 13:24–30.</note>

<sig>H</sig>
wheat <note>catchword</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon concludes his analysis of public envy, noting that widespread resentment toward government ministers often signals a hidden hatred for the state itself. He characterizes envy as a uniquely constant and depraved emotion, even identifying it as the defining trait of the Devil.</summary>

# Of Love.
## X.

The stage is more beholden <note>original: "beholding"</note> to *Love* than the life of man. For on the stage, *Love* is always the subject of comedies, and occasionally of tragedies. But in life, it does much mischief: sometimes like a *Siren* <note>original: "Syren"; a reference to the mythical creatures who lured sailors to their doom with beautiful songs.</note>; sometimes like a *Fury* <note>divine spirits of vengeance in Greek and Roman mythology.</note>. You may observe that among all the great and worthy persons (of whom the memory remains, whether from ancient or recent times), there is not one who has been transported to the mad degree of *Love*:

<summary>Bacon begins his essay on love by arguing that while it is a staple of theatrical entertainment, it is often destructive in real life. He notes that historically significant figures rarely allowed themselves to be overcome by romantic passion.</summary>

Therefore, it seems (though rarely) that *love* can find entrance not only into an open heart, but also into a heart well-fortified, if a close watch is not kept.

It is a poor saying of Epicurus <note>Greek philosopher (341–270 BC) who founded a school of philosophy based on seeking modest pleasures</note>: "We are a large enough theater for one another" <note>original Latin: *Satis magnum alter alteri theatrum sumus*</note>. It is as if man, made for the contemplation of heaven and all noble objects, should do nothing but kneel before a little idol and make himself a subject—if not of the mouth (as beasts are), then of the eye, which was given to him for higher purposes. 

It is a strange thing to note the excess of this passion, and how it defies <note>original: "braves"</note> the nature and value of things. This is shown by the fact that speaking in a perpetual *hyperbole*...

...hyperbole <note>original: "bole"</note> is graceful in nothing but in **Love**. For no **proud man** ever thought so absurdly well of himself as the **lover** does of the **person loved**. And therefore it was well said, *"That it is impossible to love and to be wise."* <note>Bacon is paraphrasing a maxim often attributed to the Roman dramatist Publilius Syrus: "Amare et sapere vix deo conceditur" (To love and be wise is hardly granted even to a god).</note>

Neither does this weakness appear only to others and not to the **person loved**; rather, it is most obvious to the **loved** party, unless the **love** is reciprocal <term>original: "reci-proque"</term>. For it is a true **rule** that **love** is always rewarded either with **reciprocity** or with an inward and secret **contempt**. 

Therefore, men ought to be all the more wary of this **passion**, which causes the loss of not only other things but of one's self. As for those other losses, the **poets' stories** illustrate them well: he who preferred **Helen** gave up the gifts of **Juno** and **Pallas**. <note>A reference to the "Judgment of Paris" from Greek mythology. There is in human nature a secret inclination and movement toward the love of others; if this is not spent upon one or a few, it naturally spreads itself toward many and makes men become humane and charitable, as is seen...

# Of Great Place.

->XI.<-

Men in high office <note>original: "Great Place"</note> are triple *servants*: *servants* of the *sovereign* or *state*; *servants* of *fame*; and *servants* of *business*. Thus, they have no *freedom*: neither in their *persons*, nor in their *actions*, nor in their *time*. It is a strange desire to seek *power* and lose *liberty*, or to seek *power* over others and lose *power* over *oneself*. # Of Great Place.

Men come to <term>Dignities</term><note>original: "Dignities"; high positions of honor or rank</note>. The standing is slippery, and the <term>Regress</term><note>original: "Regresse"; the act of stepping down or retiring from office</note> is either a downfall or at least an <term>Eclipse</term><note>original: "Eclipse"; a fading from public view or loss of influence</note>, which is a melancholy thing. *Since you are no longer what you were, there is no reason why you should wish to live* <note>original Latin: "Cum non sis, qui fueris, non esse, cur velis vivere." From Cicero.</note>. Indeed, men cannot retire when they would like to; neither will they retire when it would be reasonable to do so. Instead, they are impatient with <term>privateness</term><note>original: "privatenesse"; life away from public office</note>, even in old age and sickness, which require the shadow <note>meaning: the peace and seclusion of retirement</note>. They are like old townspeople who insist on sitting at their street doors, even though by doing so they offer their old age to public scorn. 

Certainly, great persons have need to borrow other men's opinions to think themselves happy; for if they judge by their own feeling, they cannot find it. But if they think to themselves about what other men think of them, and that other men would gladly be as they are, then they are happy, as it were, by report—when perhaps they find the contrary within. For they are the first to find their own griefs, though they are the last to find their own faults. Certainly, men in great fortunes are strangers to them-

<summary>Bacon discusses the precarious nature of high office, arguing that powerful men often struggle to retire and must rely on public perception rather than personal feelings to convince themselves they are happy.</summary>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Great Place," following a discussion on how those in high office are "thrice servants" (to the sovereign, the state, and their own reputation) and how they often lose their personal freedom in the pursuit of status.</meta>

# Of Great Place

...themselves; and while they are in the puzzle <note>original: "pusle"; meaning a state of confusion, entanglement, or overwhelming preoccupation</note> of business, they have no time to attend to their health, either of body or mind. *Death weighs heavily on the man who, while far too well known to everyone else, dies a stranger to himself.* <note>original Latin: "Illi Mors grauis incubat, qui notus nimis omnibus, ignotus moritur sibi." From Seneca’s tragedy *Thyestes*.</note>

In high office <note>original: "Place"</note>, there is the license to do both good and evil; of which the latter is a curse. For regarding evil, the best condition is not to want to do it, and the second best is not to be able to do it. But the power to do good is the true and lawful purpose of ambition. For good thoughts, though God accepts them, are little better than good dreams to other men unless they are put into action. And that action cannot be achieved without power and high office, which serve as the vantage point and commanding ground.

Merit and good works are the goal of human activity, and the conscious awareness <note>original: "Conscience"</note> of having performed them is the fulfillment of human rest. For if a man can participate in God’s theater <note>meaning the active world of creation</note>, he shall likewise participate in God’s rest. *And God turned to look upon the works which His hands had made, and saw that they were all very good* <note>original Latin: "Et conuersus Deus, vt aspiceret Opera, quæ fecerunt manus suæ, vidit quod omnia essent bona nimis." A reference to Genesis 1:31.</note>; and then followed the Sabbath. In the performance of your duties <note>original: "Discharge of thy Place"</note>, set before yourself...

<summary>Bacon argues that the true purpose of seeking high office is the power to do good, as thoughts without action are merely dreams. He links the satisfaction of a job well done to the divine rest of God after Creation.</summary>
<keywords>Great Place, power, ambition, merit, Seneca, conscience, divine rest, ethics of office</keywords>

## Of Great Place.

---

the best examples; for imitation is a globe of precepts <note>original: "Globe of Precepts"; meaning a complete or comprehensive collection of rules</note>. And after a time, set before yourself your own example; and examine yourself strictly, whether you did not do best at first. Do not neglect also the examples of those who have conducted themselves poorly in the same *place* <note>meaning a high office or position of authority</note>: not to make yourself look better by criticizing their memory, but to guide yourself in what to avoid. Reform, therefore, without ostentation <note>original: "Brauerie"</note> or scandal toward former times and persons; but yet set it down as your goal to create good precedents <note>original: "Presidents"</note> as well as to follow them. Return things to their original institution <note>the way they were first established</note> and observe in what way and how they have degenerated; yet ask counsel of both times: of ancient times, what is best, and of the latter time, what is most suitable. Seek to make your course regular <note>consistent and predictable</note>, so that men may know beforehand what they may expect; but do not be too dogmatic <note>original: "positiue"</note> and peremptory. Explain yourself well when you depart from your rule. Preserve the rights of your *place*; but stir...

<summary>Bacon advises those in high office to use both historical precedents and their own past successes as guides, emphasizing that reform should be balanced and consistent.</summary>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper conduct of those in high office, following from a point about avoiding jurisdictional disputes.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
->### Of Great Place.<-

Not questions of jurisdiction; and rather assume your right in silence and *de facto* <note>original: "de facto"; Latin for "in practice" or "as a matter of fact."</note> than voice it with claims and challenges. Preserve, likewise, the rights of inferior *places*; and think it more honor to direct as a chief than to be busy in all things. Embrace and invite help and advice regarding the execution of your *place*; and do not drive away those who bring you information as "meddlers," but accept them in good faith. 

The vices of *authority* are chiefly four: *delays*, *corruption*, *roughness*, and *facility* <term>original: "facilitie"; here meaning being too easily influenced or lacking the firmness to say no.</term>. 

For *delays*: give easy access; keep appointed times; finish the business currently in hand; and do not interweave different tasks unless it is out of necessity. 

For *corruption*: do not only bind your own hands, or your servants' hands, from taking bribes, but bind the hands of suitors <note>Those seeking a favor or legal judgment from an official.</note> also from offering them. For practicing integrity accomplishes the first; but openly declaring your integrity, with a manifest detestation of bribery, accomplishes the other. And avoid not only the fault, but even the suspicion of it. Whoever is

<summary>Francis Bacon outlines the four primary vices of authority—delays, corruption, roughness, and facility—and provides practical advice on how a high official can maintain integrity and efficiency.</summary>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the vices of authority in "great place" (high office), specifically transitioning from the dangers of inconsistency to the problems of corruption, roughness, and being too easily influenced.</meta>

# Of Great Place.

If you are found to be inconsistent <note>original: "variable"</note> and you change your mind clearly without an obvious cause, it gives rise to the suspicion of ***corruption***. Therefore, always, when you change your opinion or course of action, profess it plainly and declare it along with the reasons that move you to change. Do not think to sneak it past others <note>original: "to steale it"; to change one's position stealthily or deceptively</note>. A servant or a favorite, if he is an intimate <note>original: "inward"</note> and there is no other apparent cause for your high regard for him, is commonly thought to be only a side-path to secret ***corruption***. 

Regarding ***roughness***: it is a needless cause of ***discontent***. ***Severity*** breeds ***fear***, but ***roughness*** breeds ***hate***. Even ***reproofs*** from ***authority*** ought to be grave and not ***taunting***. 

As for ***facility*** <note>original: "Facilitie"; the quality of being too easy-going, yielding too readily to pressure, or being overly eager to please</note>, it is worse than ***bribery***. For ***bribes*** come only now and then; but if ***importunity*** <note>persistent or urgent requests</note> or ***idle respect*** <note>meaning personal favoritism or social bias</note> lead a man, he shall never be without them. As ***Solomon*** says: "*To show partiality is not good, because for a piece of bread a man will transgress.*" <note>Proverbs 28:21</note> It is most true, what was said in ancient times: "*Place shows the man*." <note>A translation of the Greek proverb "archē andra deixei," suggesting that holding power reveals a person's true character.</note> And it shows some for the better and some for the worse:

<meta>Catchword: Omnium</meta>

<summary>Bacon warns that inconsistent behavior in office suggests corruption and advises leaders to be transparent about changes in policy while avoiding roughness and the weakness of being too easily influenced by others.</summary>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's reflections on the behavior and responsibilities of those in high office ("Great Place"), focusing on how authority reveals and shapes character.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Of Great Place.</header>

# Of Great Place.

"By everyone's consent, he would have been thought capable of ruling, if only he had not ruled" <note>original Latin: *Omnium consensu, capax Imperii, nisi imperasset*</note>; so says **Tacitus** of the Emperor **Galba**. But of **Vespasian** he says: "Of all the emperors, Vespasian was the only one who changed for the better" <note>original Latin: *Solus Imperantium Vespasianus mutatus in melius*</note> after coming to power. Though the first comment referred to his competence <note>original: "Sufficiencie"</note>, the other referred to his character and disposition <note>original: "Manners, and Affection"</note>. It is a sure sign of a worthy and noble spirit when being given honor makes a person better. 

For honor is, or should be, the natural home of virtue. Just as in the natural world, objects move violently toward their destination but rest calmly once they arrive, so virtue is restless and aggressive during the pursuit of ambition, but becomes settled and calm once it attains authority. 

Every ascent to a position of great power is like climbing a winding staircase. If there are political factions, it is wise to align yourself with a side while you are rising, but to remain balanced and neutral once you have reached your position. Treat the memory of your predecessor with fairness and kindness; for if you do not, it is a debt that will surely be paid back to you by others once you are gone. If you have colleagues, show them respect; it is better to invite them to consult with you when they do not expect it than to exclude them when they have every reason to expect to be called upon.

<summary>Bacon discusses how high office reveals a person's true character, arguing that while the path to power requires political maneuvering, the exercise of power should be marked by stability, fairness toward predecessors, and respect for colleagues.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Tacitus, Galba, Vespasian, leadership, ambition, political office, ethics, power dynamics</keywords>

# Of Boldness.
## XII.

It is a trivial Grammar School text, yet it is worthy of a wise man’s consideration. A question was asked of *Demosthenes*: *What was the chief part of an orator?* He answered, *Action*; what next? *Action*; what next again? *Action*. He said it, who knew it best; and he had, by nature, no advantage in the very thing he praised <note>original: "commended"</note>. It is a strange thing that this part of an orator, which is merely superficial and more of an actor's skill <note>original: "vertue of a Player"</note>, should be placed so high—above those other noble parts of invention <note>original: "Inuention"; the process of discovering and organizing arguments</note>, elocution <note>original: "Elocution"; the style and phrasing of a speech</note>, and the rest. Indeed, it is treated almost alone, as if it were everything. But the reason is plain. In human nature, there is generally more of the fool than of the wise man;

# Of Boldness.

Wise; and therefore those faculties by which the foolish part of men's minds is captured are most potent. The case of *boldness* in civil business <note>original: "Ciuill Buſineſſe"; meaning public affairs or politics</note> is wonderfully similar. What is first? *Boldness*. What is second and third? *Boldness* <note>Bacon here alludes to the famous anecdote of Demosthenes, who said the three most important parts of oratory were "action" or "delivery." Bacon substitutes "boldness" for "action."</note>. And yet *boldness* is a child of ignorance and baseness, far inferior to other qualities. 

Nevertheless, it fascinates and binds hand and foot those who are either shallow in judgment or weak in courage, which includes the greatest part of people. Indeed, it even prevails with wise men in their weaker moments. Therefore, we see it has done wonders in popular states <note>original: "Popular States"; referring to democracies or republics where public opinion is dominant</note>, but less so with senates and princes. It is always more effective upon the first entrance of *bold persons* into action than it is soon after; for *boldness* is an ill keeper of promises. 

Surely, just as there are *mountebanks* <note>original: "Mountebanques"; traveling quack doctors who sold dubious medicines</note> for the physical body, so there are *mountebanks* for the political body: men who undertake great cures and perhaps have been lucky in two or three experiments, but lack the grounds of science <note>original: "Science"; here meaning systematic or foundational knowledge</note>. And therefore...

<summary>In this opening to his essay on boldness, Francis Bacon argues that while boldness is an inferior quality born of ignorance, it is highly effective in politics because it exploits the weaknesses of the majority. He compares bold political actors to medical quacks who rely on luck rather than scientific knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>boldness, politics, psychology, Demosthenes, mountebanks, public affairs, human nature</keywords>

The people assembled; *Muhammad* called the hill to come to him, again and again; and when the hill stood still, he was not a bit abashed <note>original: "never a whit abashed"</note>, but said: *If the hill will not come to Muhammad, Muhammad will go to the hill.* So these men, when they have promised great matters and failed most shamefully (yet if they possess the perfection of *boldness*), they will simply brush it off <note>original: "slight it over"</note>, pivot <note>original: "make a turn"</note>, and make no more ado about it. Certainly, to men of great judgment, *bold* persons are a source of amusement <note>original: "a Sport"</note> to behold; indeed, even to the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>, *boldness* has something of the ridiculous about it. For if absurdity is the subject of laughter, do not doubt that great *boldness* is seldom without some absurdity. It is especially amusing to see when a *bold person* is embarrassed <note>original: "out of Countenance"</note>; for that puts his face into a

<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Boldness," specifically discussing the physical behavior of bold men compared to bashful ones and the strategic placement of bold individuals in leadership roles.</meta>

# Of Boldness

...most shrunken and wooden posture—as it needs must. For in bashfulness, the spirits <note>In the medical theory of the time, "spirits" were vital fluids or vapors thought to control movement and emotion.</note> do come and go a little; but with *Bold Men*, upon similar occasion, they stand at a stay <note>they remain fixed or frozen</note>. It is like a stalemate <note>original: "Stale"</note> at chess, where it is not a checkmate, and yet the game cannot move. But this last point would be more fitting for a satire than for a serious observation.

This is well worth weighing: that *Boldness* is always blind, for it does not see dangers and inconveniences. Therefore, it is bad in counsel <note>deliberation and planning</note> but good in execution <note>the actual carrying out of a task</note>. So, the right use of *Bold* persons is that they should never command in chief, but instead be subordinates <note>original: "Seconds"</note> under the direction of others. For in counsel, it is good to see dangers; but in execution, it is best not to see them, unless they are very great.

->Of Goodness and<-

# Of Goodness
## And
## Goodness of Nature.

## XIII

Take goodness in this sense: the desiring of the well-being <note>original: "affecting of the Weale"</note> of men, which is what the Greeks call *Philanthropia* <note>original: Philanthropia; Greek for "love of mankind"</note>. The word "humanity" (as it is currently used) is a little too light to express it. I call the habit "goodness," and the natural inclination "goodness of nature." 

Of all virtues and dignities of the mind, this is the greatest, being the very character of the Deity <note>God</note>. Without it, man is a busy, mischievous, wretched thing—no better than a kind of vermin. Goodness corresponds to the theological virtue of charity <note>original: Charitie; referring to "agape" or selfless, brotherly love</note> and admits no excess, only error.

<summary>Francis Bacon defines goodness as the active desire for the welfare of others, identifying it as the highest human virtue and the defining characteristic of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Philanthropia, Goodness, Charity, Virtue, Theology, Ethics, Humanity</keywords>

Error. The desire for power in excess caused the angels to fall; the desire for knowledge in excess caused man to fall; but in ***Charity***, there is no ***excess***; neither can angel or man come into danger by it. The inclination toward ***goodness*** is imprinted deeply in the ***nature of man***—so much so that if it does not flow toward men, it will turn toward other living creatures. This is seen among the Turks, a cruel people, who nevertheless are kind to beasts and give alms to dogs and birds. Indeed, ***Busbechius*** <note>Ogier Ghiselin de Busbecq (1522–1592), a Flemish diplomat who wrote famous letters about his time in the Ottoman Empire.</note> reports that a Christian boy in ***Constantinople*** was nearly stoned for gagging a long-billed bird <note>likely a pelican or a crane</note> out of a sense of mischief <note>original: "waggishness"</note>. Errors, indeed, in this virtue of ***goodness*** or ***charity*** may be committed. The Italians have an ungracious proverb: *Tanto buon che val niente* <note>Italian: "So good that he is good for nothing."</note>. Seek the good of other men, but do not be in bondage to their faces or fancies; for that is merely facility <note>original: "facilitie"; an over-eagerness to please or a lack of firmness</note> or softness, which takes an honest mind prisoner. Common benefits are to be shared with all, but special benefits should be given with choice. # Of Nobility.
## XIV.

**W**e will speak of *Nobility*, first as a *part* of a *state*; then as a *condition* of *individual persons*. A *monarchy* where there is no *nobility* at all is always a pure and absolute *tyranny*, such as that of the *Turks* <note>original: "Turkes"; referring to the Ottoman Empire</note>. For *nobility* tempers <note>original: "attempers"; to moderate or balance the power of a ruler</note> *sovereignty* and draws the eyes of the people somewhat away from the *royal lineage* <note>original: "Line Royall"</note>. But for *democracies*, they do not need it; and they are commonly more quiet and less subject to *sedition* than where there are *lineages* <term>Stirps: a Latin term for roots, families, or ancestral stocks</term> of *nobles*. For men's eyes are upon the business and not upon the persons; or if they look upon the persons, it is for the sake of the business—as those most fit for it—and not for banners <note>original: "Flags"; heraldic symbols of rank and ancestry</note> and pedigree. We see the *Swiss* <note>original: "Switzers"</note> last well, notwithstanding their diversity

<summary>In this opening to his essay on nobility, Francis Bacon argues that while a nobility is essential to temper the power of a monarch, it is unnecessary and often a source of instability in democracies.</summary>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>Of Nobility.</header>

# Of Nobility.

...unity <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "necessity" or "unity," referring to the shared interests of a republic</note> of religion and of cantons. For utility is their bond, and not personal prestige <note>original: "Respects"</note>. The United Provinces of the Low Countries <note>The Netherlands</note> excel in their government; for where there is an equality, the consultations are more impartial <note>original: "indifferent"</note>, and the taxes and tributes are paid more cheerfully. A great and powerful *nobility* adds majesty to a monarch but diminishes their actual power; it puts life and spirit into the common people, but weighs down <note>original: "presseth"</note> their financial success. It is well when *nobles* are not too great for sovereignty nor for justice, and yet are maintained at such a height that the insolence of inferiors may be broken upon them before it reaches the majesty of kings. A numerous *nobility* causes poverty and inconvenience in a state, for it creates an excessive burden of expense. Besides, since it is inevitable that many of the nobility will in time lose their wealth <note>original: "fall in time to be weake in Fortune"</note>, it creates a kind of disproportion between honor and means.

As for *nobility* in individuals:

<page-num>74</page-num>

# Of Nobility.

It is a reverent <note>original: "Reuerend"</note> thing to see an ancient castle or building that is not in decay, or to see a fine <note>original: "faire"</note> timber tree, sound and perfect. How much more so to behold an ancient *noble family* which has stood against the waves and weathers of time. For new *nobility* is but the act of power, but *ancient nobility* is the act of time. 

Those who are first raised to *nobility* are commonly more virtuous but less innocent than their descendants; for there is rarely any rising except by a mixture <note>original: "Commixture"</note> of good and evil arts. But it is right <note>original: "it is Reason"</note> that the memory of their virtues should remain with their posterity, and their faults die with themselves. 

*Nobility of birth* commonly diminishes <note>original: "abateth"</note> industry, and he who is not industrious envies him who is. Besides, *noble persons* cannot go much higher; and he who stands still <note>original: "standeth at a stay"</note> while others rise can hardly avoid feelings <note>original: "Motions"</note> of envy. On the other hand, *nobility* extinguishes the passive envy from others toward them, because they are

Disregarding rumors and false alarms often stops them best; attempting to suppress them frequently only makes the sensation last longer. This is especially true if, during those disputes, those who support the mandate speak hesitantly and cautiously, while those against it speak boldly.

For the movements of the most powerful persons in a government ought to be like the movements of the planets under the <term>Primum Mobile</term> <note>original: "First Mover." # Of Seditions

But let us pass from this part concerning predictions (about which, nevertheless, more light may be gained from what follows), and let us speak first of the *materials of seditions* <term>seditions: actions or speech inciting people to rebel against the authority of a state or monarch</term>; then of the *motives* for them; and thirdly of the *remedies*.

Concerning the *materials of seditions*: it is a thing well worth considering. For the surest way to prevent seditions (if the times permit it) is to take away the *matter* <note>the underlying "fuel" or physical conditions</note> of them. For if there is fuel prepared, it is hard to tell from where the spark shall come that will set it on fire. The *matter* of *seditions* is of two kinds: *great poverty* and *great discontentment*. It is certain that for every *overthrown estate* <note>original: "overthrown estates"; referring to ruined personal fortunes or bankruptcies</note>, there are just as many *votes for trouble*. *Lucan* <note>a Roman poet (39–65 AD) who wrote about the Roman Civil War</note> noted well the *state of Rome* before the *Civil War*:

> *Hence devouring usury, and interest greedy for time...*
> <note>original Latin: "Hinc usura vorax, rapidumque in tempore foenus." This refers to the predatory lending and debt that fueled social instability.</note>

<summary>Bacon identifies the fundamental causes of rebellion as poverty and social discontent, arguing that the best way to prevent unrest is to remove the "fuel" of economic hardship.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the causes and symptoms of civil unrest (seditions). The catchword "uoke" from the previous page likely completed the word "provoke," though here it appears at the end of the page looking forward to the next.</meta>

*From this came shaken credit, and war profitable to many.* <note>original Latin: "Hinc concussa fides, et multis utile bellum." This is a quote from the Roman poet Lucan, describing the social decay that led to the Roman Civil War.</note>

This same "war profitable to many" is a certain and infallible sign of a state disposed toward **seditions** and **troubles**. And if this **poverty** and **financial ruin** among the upper classes is joined with a **want** and **necessity** among the common people, the danger is imminent and great. For the **rebellions of the belly** <note>This refers to uprisings caused by famine, hunger, or extreme economic hardship.</note> are the worst. As for **discontentments**, they are in the **body politic** <note>The state or nation viewed as a collective body.</note> like **humors** <note>original: "Humours." In early medicine, these were the bodily fluids thought to determine health and temperament; an imbalance or "heat" in the humors caused illness.</note> in the physical body, which are prone to gather an **unnatural heat** and to **ignite**. 

And let no prince measure the danger of these discontents by whether they are **just** or **unjust**. To do so would be to imagine people are more reasonable than they are; for people often reject what is actually for their own good. Nor should the danger be measured by whether the **grievances** from which they rise are, in fact, great or small. For those are the most dangerous **discontentments** where the **fear** is greater than the **feeling**. *There is a limit to suffering, but no limit to fearing.* <note>original Latin: "Dolendi modus, timendi non item." A quote from Pliny the Younger, suggesting that while actual pain has boundaries, the imagination can expand fear indefinitely.</note> Besides, in cases of great oppression, the very things that provoke...
