# Of Seditions

...provoke patience, they also subdue <note>original: "mate"; to overcome or checkmate</note> courage. But with fears, it is not so. Neither let any Prince or State feel secure regarding *discontent*, simply because it has occurred often or lasted long without any peril following. For while it is true that every vapor or fume does not turn into a storm, it is nevertheless true that storms, though they may blow over many times, may eventually strike. And as the *Spanish proverb* well notes: *The cord breaks at last with the weakest pull.*

The *causes* and *motives* of *seditions* <note>original: "seditions"; organized rebellions or uprisings against authority</note> are: *innovation in religion*; *taxes*; *alteration of laws and customs*; *violation of privileges*; *general oppression*; *advancement of unworthy persons*; *foreigners* <note>original: "strangers"</note>; *famines* <note>original: "dearths"</note>; *disbanded soldiers*; *factions grown desperate*; and whatever else offends the people and joins them together in a common cause.

Regarding the *remedies*: there are some general *preventative measures* <note>original: "preservatives"</note> of which we will speak. As for the exact cure, it must an—

<summary>This page outlines the unpredictable nature of political discontent and lists the primary social and economic triggers that lead to organized rebellion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the causes of sedition and moves into the practical remedies for preventing social unrest.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>and Troubles.</header>

<unclear>answer</unclear> to the particular disease, and so be left to counsel rather than rule.

The first *remedy* or prevention is to remove by all means possible that *material cause of sedition* of which we spoke, which is *want* and *poverty* in the *state* <note>original: "Estate." Here referring to the general condition and wealth of the nation.</note>. To this purpose, several strategies are useful: the opening and well-balancing of trade; the encouraging of manufactures; the banishing of idleness; the restraining of waste and excess by <term>sumptuary laws</term> <note>Laws designed to regulate consumption and limit extravagance in food, drink, and clothing.</note>; the improvement and management of the soil; the regulating of prices for goods for sale; the moderating of taxes and tributes; and the like.

Generally, it must be ensured that the population of a kingdom (especially if it is not thinned by wars) does not exceed the resources of the kingdom which should maintain them. Neither is the population to be counted by number alone; for a smaller number of people who spend more and earn less will exhaust a state's resources sooner than a greater number who live more modestly and accumulate more wealth. Therefore, the

<header>Of Seditions</header>

## Of Seditions

The multiplication of the nobility, and other ranks of quality, in a greater proportion than the common people, quickly brings a state to poverty <note>original: "necessity"</note>. An oversized clergy does the same, for they contribute nothing to the national wealth <note>original: "stock"</note>. It is the same when more scholars are educated than there are career appointments <note>original: "preferments"</note> to employ them.

It should also be remembered that, because the growth of any state's wealth must come at the expense of the foreigner (for whatever is gained in one place is lost in another), there are only three things which one nation sells to another: the raw *Commodity* as nature provides it; the *Manufacture*; and the *Vecture* <note>transportation of goods</note> or carriage. So, if these three wheels turn, wealth will flow as in a spring tide. And it often happens that the workmanship will surpass the material <note>original Latin: *materiam superabit opus*</note>; that is, the work and the transportation are worth more than the raw material and enrich a state more. This is notably seen in the people of the Netherlands <note>original: "Low-Country-men"</note>, who have the best mines above ground in the world <note>Bacon suggests that Dutch trade and industry are more profitable than actual gold or silver mines</note>.

<summary>Bacon argues that an overabundance of non-productive social classes, such as the nobility, clergy, and over-educated scholars, drains a nation's resources. He posits that national wealth is built on foreign trade through three pillars: raw goods, manufacturing, and shipping.</summary>
<keywords>nobility, clergy, education, economic theory, trade, manufacturing, transportation, Netherlands, mercantilism, social balance</keywords>

Above all things, good policy should be used to ensure that the wealth and money in a state are not gathered into a few hands. Otherwise, a state may have a great total stock of capital and yet still starve. Money is like manure <note>original: "Muck"</note>; it is no good unless it is spread around. This is achieved mainly by suppressing, or at least keeping a tight rein <note>original: "strait Hand"</note> on, the exploitative <note>original: "Devouring"</note> trades of *usury* <note>lending money at high interest</note>, *monopolizing* <note>original: "Ingrossing"; the practice of buying up goods to corner the market and raise prices</note>, large-scale *pasture farming* <note>referring to the enclosure movement where arable land was converted to sheep pasture, often displacing rural populations</note>, and the like.

To remove *discontentment*, or at least the danger of it, there are in every state (as we know) two classes of *subjects*: the *nobility* and the *common people*. When only one of these is *discontented*, the danger is not great. For the common people are slow to act if they are not stirred up by the upper classes <note>original: "the Greater Sort"</note>; and the upper classes have little strength unless the masses <note>original: "the Multitude"</note> are already inclined and ready to move on their own. The real danger arises when the upper classes are merely waiting for the commoners <note>original: "the Meaner"</note> to stir the waters, so that they may then declare their own opposition. The poets imagine <note>original: "feign"</note> that the rest of

<summary>Bacon argues that wealth must be distributed widely to maintain a healthy state and explains that rebellion only becomes a critical threat when the elite and the commoners are both ready to revolt.</summary>

And it is a certain sign of a wise government and its proceedings when it can hold people’s hearts by *hopes* when it cannot do so by satisfaction. It is wise when it can manage affairs in such a way that no evil appears so final <note>original: "peremptory"</note> that it does not have some outlet of *hope*. This is less difficult to achieve because both individuals and factions are quite prone to flattering themselves, or at least to putting on a brave face <note>original: "to brave"</note> against what they do not truly believe.

Also, the foresight and prevention to ensure there is no likely or suitable <term>leader</term> <note>original: "Head"</note> to whom <term>dissatisfied</term> persons may resort, and under whom they may join together, is a well-known but excellent point of caution. Generally, dividing and breaking up all factions and alliances that are hostile to the state—and putting distance or at least distrust between them—is a highly effective *Remedy*. For it is a desperate case if those who support the actions of the state are full of discord and faction, while those who are against it are entirely united.

I have noted that certain clever and sharp remarks made by *Princes* have sparked *Seditions*. *Caesar* did himself infinite harm with the remark: "Sulla did not know his letters; he could not dictate." <note>original Latin: *Sylla nesciuit Literas, non potuit dictare*. *“The Roman Empire will have no further need for soldiers.”* <note>original Latin: *opus erit ampliùs Romano Imperio militibus.* This tactless remark by Emperor Galba alienated his troops and led to his downfall.</note>
A speech of great despair for the soldiers, and there are many others like it. Surely, **Princes** (rulers) need to be careful about what they say in sensitive matters and precarious times, especially in these short speeches which fly abroad like darts and are thought to be shot out of their secret intentions. As for long discourses, they are dull things and not nearly as noticed.

Lastly, let **Princes**, to prepare for all eventualities, not be without some great person—one or even more—of military valor near them to repress **seditions** in their early stages. For without that, there tends to be more panic in the **Court** upon the first breaking out of **troubles** than is appropriate. And the **State** runs the danger of what **Tacitus** says: *“And such was the state of their minds, that a few dared to commit the worst of crimes, more desired it, and all allowed it.”* <note>original Latin: *Atque is Habitus animorum fuit, ut pessimum facinus auderent Pauci, Plures vellent, Omnes paterentur.*</note> But let such military persons be reliable and well-regarded, rather than factious and <term>popular</term> <note>In this context, "popular" refers to a leader who actively courts the favor of the common people, which was often viewed by monarchs as a sign of dangerous ambition.</note>; they should also maintain a good correspondence with the o-

# Of Atheism.
## XVI.

I would rather believe all the fables in the *Legend* <note>original: "Legend"; likely referring to the *Legenda Aurea* or "Golden Legend," a famous medieval collection of stories about the lives of saints</note>, the *Talmud*, and the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran"</note>, than believe that this universal frame <note>the structure of the universe</note> is without a mind. And therefore, God never performed a miracle to convince an atheist, because His ordinary works <note>the natural laws and beauty of the physical world</note> provide enough evidence to do so. It is true that a little philosophy inclines a man's mind toward atheism, but depth in philosophy brings men's minds back to religion. For while the mind of man looks upon "second causes" <term>Second causes: The natural or physical explanations for how things happen, as opposed to the "First Cause" or Divine will.</term> in isolation, it may sometimes stop there and go no further;

Atheists are always talking about their opinion, as if they feel weak <note>original: "fainted"</note> in their belief within themselves and would be glad to be strengthened by the agreement of others. # Of Discourse.

## XXXII.

S<note>Decorative initial</note>ome in their *discourse* <note>original: "Discourse"; here meaning conversation or speech</note> desire praise for their wit <note>original: "Wit"; meaning mental sharpness or intelligence</note>—being able to maintain all arguments—rather than for their judgment in discerning what is true. It is as if it were a merit to know what *might* be said, rather than what *should* be thought. 

Some have certain commonplaces <note>original: "Common Places"; pre-prepared topics, clichés, or stock arguments used to fill conversation</note> and themes in which they are good, but they lack variety. This kind of poverty is for the most part tedious and, once it is perceived, ridiculous. 

The most honorable part of talk is to provide the occasion <note>original: "giue the Occasion"; to start the conversation or introduce a topic</note>, and then to moderate and pass to something else; for then a man leads the dance. It is good, in *discourse* and conversational speech, to vary and intermingle talk of the present occasion with arguments, and stories with rea- <note>The text likely continues with "reasons" on the following page.</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that effective conversation requires judgment and variety rather than just cleverness or rehearsed topics. He suggests that the most skillful speaker is one who can initiate a topic and then guide the flow of the "dance" of conversation.</summary>

# Of Discourse

For he will give them the opportunity to please themselves by speaking, and he himself will continually gather knowledge. But let his questions not be bothersome, for that is more suited for a <term>poser</term> <note>original: "poser"; meaning an official examiner or someone who interrogates others as a test</note>. And he should be sure to allow other men their turns to speak. Indeed, if there is anyone who tries to dominate and take up all the time, he should find ways to interrupt them and bring others into the conversation, just as musicians do with those who dance <term>galliards</term> <note>original: "galliards"; a lively, athletic Renaissance dance that could be exhausting if performed for too long</note> for too long. 

If you sometimes hide your knowledge of things you are expected to know, you will be believed at other times when you claim to know things that you actually do not. Speaking about oneself should be rare and well-chosen. I knew someone who used to say in mockery, "*He must certainly be a wise man, since he speaks so much about himself.*" 

There is only one case where a man may praise himself with good grace, and that is by praising a virtue in another person—especially if it is a virtue that he himself aspires to <note>original: "pretendeth"; in this context, it means to lay claim to or aim for a quality</note>. Personal or critical remarks <note>original: "Speech of Touch"; refers to comments that "touch" a person's character, often used for witty jibes or pointed critiques</note> regarding others should be spa- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the word "ringly" (sparingly).</note>

<summary>In this excerpt from "Of Discourse," Francis Bacon advises on the etiquette of conversation, suggesting that a good listener gains knowledge while a polite speaker ensures everyone has a chance to contribute without being interrogated.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's essay "Of Discourse," specifically discussing the use of wit and the balance between long-form speaking and quick conversation.</meta>
<page-num>197</page-num>
->**Of Discourse**<-

...sparingly used: for *discourse* <note>original: "Discourse"; meaning conversation or verbal exchange</note> ought to be like an open field, without striking home at any specific man <note>meaning conversation should be a broad space for everyone, not a weapon used to attack individuals</note>. I knew two noblemen from the west of England; one of them was given to mocking <note>original: "scoff"</note>, but he always kept royal hospitality in his house. The other would ask those who had been at the first man's table, "Tell me truly, was there never a taunt <note>original: "flout"</note> or a cutting remark <note>original: "dry blow"</note> given?" To which the guest would answer, "Such and such a thing happened." The lord would then say, "I thought he would ruin <note>original: "marre"</note> a good dinner." 

Discretion in speech is more important than **eloquence**; and to speak in a way that is agreeable to the person with whom we are dealing is more important than to speak with fine words or in a logical order. A good sustained speech <note>original: "continued speech"</note> without the ability to engage in dialogue <note>original: "interlocution"</note> shows a slowness of mind. Conversely, a good reply or a quick second speech without the ability to provide a solid, settled speech shows shallowness and weakness. 

We see this among animals: those that are weakest in a long race <note>original: "the course"</note> are often the nimblest at the turn—as it is between the greyhound and the hare. To use too many introductory details <note>original: "circumstances"</note> before one comes to the matter at hand is wearisome; to use none at all is blunt.

->Of<-

<summary>In this excerpt from his essay "Of Discourse," Francis Bacon argues that conversation should be inclusive rather than personal and that social discretion is more valuable than mere eloquence or rhetorical skill.</summary>

# Of Plantations.

## XXXIII.

**P**lantations <note>In this context, Bacon uses "plantations" to mean the establishment of new colonies.</note> are among ancient, primitive, and heroic works. When the world was young, it begot more children; but now that it is old, it begets fewer. For I may justly consider new *plantations* to be the children of former *kingdoms*. I prefer a *plantation* in a pure soil—that is, where people are not *displaced* <note>original: "displanted"</note> in order to *plant* others. Otherwise, it is an extirpation <term>the complete removal or destruction of a population or people from a territory</term> rather than a *plantation*.

*Planting* countries is like *planting* woods; for you must expect to lose <note>original: "leese"</note> almost twenty years of profit and wait for your reward in the end. For the principal thing that has been the destruction of most *plantations* has been the base and hasty pursuit of profit in the first years. It is true, speedy...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that establishing colonies is a noble and "heroic" endeavor, likening them to the children of established kingdoms, while warning that they require long-term investment rather than a greedy search for immediate profit.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Plantations, Colonization, Political Philosophy, Economic Theory, Extirpation, Colonial Profit</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's advice on establishing new colonies (plantations), focusing on the types of people who should settle them and the natural resources to look for.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>

# Of Plantations.

Profit is not to be neglected, as far as it is consistent with the good of the **plantation**, but no further. It is a shameful and unblessed thing to take the "scum" of the people—wicked, condemned men—to be the people with whom you **plant**. And not only so, but it ruins the **plantation**; for they will always live like rogues and not settle down to work, but will be lazy, do mischief, consume provisions <note>original: "Victuals"</note>, and quickly grow weary. Then, they will send reports <note>original: "Certifie ouer"</note> back to their home country to the discredit of the **plantation**. 

The people with whom you **plant** ought to be gardeners, plowmen, laborers, smiths, carpenters, joiners <note>skilled woodworkers who join pieces of timber</note>, fishermen, and fowlers <note>hunters of wild birds</note>, along with some few apothecaries <note>pharmacists</note>, surgeons, cooks, and bakers. In a country being colonized, first look around to see what kind of food the land yields on its own, ready to hand: such as chestnuts, walnuts, pine-nuts <note>original: "Pine-Apples" – in the 17th century, this referred to the cones of pine trees and their edible seeds</note>, olives, dates, plums, cherries, wild honey, and the like; and make use of them. Then consider what food or edible <note>original: "Esculent"</note> things there are which grow quickly, and

<summary>Bacon argues that colonies should be settled by skilled laborers and tradesmen rather than criminals, as the latter lead to the colony's failure through laziness and bad reports.</summary>

# *Of Plantations.*

Rice also yields a great increase and is a good kind of food. 

Above all, there should be brought a store of biscuit, oatmeal, flour, meal, and the like in the beginning, until bread can be produced locally. For beasts or birds, take chiefly those that are least subject to diseases and multiply fastest: such as swine, goats, cocks, hens, turkeys, geese, house-doves <note>pigeons kept for food</note>, and the like. 

The victuals <note>food supplies</note> in *plantations* <note>colonies</note> ought to be used almost as in a besieged town; that is, with fixed rations. # Of Riches.

## XXXIV.

I cannot call *riches* anything better than the baggage of *virtue*. The *Roman word* is better: *impedimenta* <note>Latin: "hindrances" or "baggage"</note>. For as baggage is to an army, so are *riches* to *virtue*. It cannot be spared, nor left behind, but it hinders the march; indeed, the care of it sometimes loses or disturbs the victory. 

There is no real use for great *riches*, except in their distribution; the rest is merely an idea <note>original: "conceit," meaning an imaginary fancy or mental concept</note>. As *Solomon* says: *Where there is much, there are many to consume it; and what does the owner have, except the sight of it with his eyes?* <note>A reference to the biblical book of Ecclesiastes 5:11</note>. 

The personal enjoyment <note>original: "fruition"</note> of any man cannot truly reach the point of feeling great *riches*. One may have the custody of them, or the power of charity <term>dole</term> and giving gifts <term>donative</term> from them, or a

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that wealth is the "baggage" of virtue—necessary but often a hindrance—and asserts that great riches have no real utility beyond their distribution to others.</summary>

# Of Riches.

They bring fame, but no solid use to the owner. Do you not see what artificial prices are set upon gemstones and rarities? And what works of ostentation <note>original: "Ostentation"; meaning boastful displays of vanity or wealth</note> are undertaken just so there might seem to be some use for great **riches**? 

But then you will say they may be of use to buy men out of dangers or troubles. As Solomon says: ***Wealth is a fortified city in the imagination of the rich man.*** <note>Proverbs 18:11</note> But this is excellently expressed: it is in his *imagination*, and not always in *fact*. For certainly great **riches** have "sold" <note>betrayed or made a target of</note> more men than they have "bought out" <note>rescued</note>. 

Seek not ostentatious **riches**, but only such as you may get justly, use soberly, distribute cheerfully, and leave behind contentedly. Yet do not have a theoretical or monk-like <note>original: "Friarly"</note> contempt for them. But make a distinction, as Cicero said well of Rabirius Posthumus: *In his efforts to increase his wealth, it was clear that he did not seek a prize for his greed, but an instrument for his goodness.* <note>original: "In studio rei amplificandæ, apparebat, non Auaritiæ Prædam, sed Instrumentum Bonitati, quæri."</note> 

Listen also to Solomon, and beware of gathering **riches** too hastily: *He who hastens to get rich will not be innocent.* <note>original: "Qui festinat ad Diuitias, non erit insons." From Proverbs 28:20.</note> The poets imagine that when

# Of Riches.

When **Plutus** (who represents **Riches**) is sent from **Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods in Roman mythology, representing divine providence or natural order.</note>, he limps and moves slowly. But when he is sent from **Pluto** <note>The god of the underworld, associated here with death and the devil.</note>, he runs and is swift of foot. This means that **riches** gained by good means and honest labor advance slowly. However, when they come through the death of others—such as through inheritance, wills, and the like—they come tumbling upon a person. 

This might also be applied to **Pluto** if we take him to represent the Devil. For when **riches** come from the Devil—such as through fraud, oppression, and unjust means—they arrive at great speed. The ways to become wealthy are many, and most of them are foul. <term>Parsimony</term> <note>original: "Parsimony"; extreme frugality or stinginess.</note> is one of the best ways, and yet it is not innocent, for it withholds men from performing works of liberality and charity. 

The <term>improvement of the ground</term> <note>original: "Improvement of the Ground"; agricultural development and land management.</note> is the most natural way of obtaining **riches**, for it is the blessing of our "Great Mother," the Earth; but it is a slow process. And yet, where men of great wealth stoop to <term>husbandry</term> <note>original: "husbandry"; the cultivation of crops and breeding of animals.</note>, it multiplies **riches** exceedingly. ## Of Riches:

[A] nobleman in **England** who managed the largest audits <note>original: "Audits"; here meaning financial accounts or records of revenue</note> of any man in my time: he was a great grazier, a great sheep-master, a great timber-man, a great coal-master <note>original: "Colliar"</note>, a great grain-master <note>original: "Corne-Maister"</note>, a great lead-master, and likewise for iron, and a number of similar points of husbandry <term>husbandry: the careful management of land, livestock, and resources</term>. It was as if the earth were a sea to him, because of the constant importation of wealth. 

It was truly observed by one person that he himself reached a little **riches** with great difficulty, but reached great **riches** very easily. For when a man’s capital <note>original: "Stocke"</note> has reached the point where he can wait for the best market prices <note>original: "expect the Prime of Markets"</note>, and secure <note>original: "ouercome"</note> those bargains which are too large for most men to afford, and be a partner in the industries of younger men, he cannot help but increase his wealth greatly. 

The gains of **ordinary trades** and **vocations** <note>professions or jobs</note> are honest, and are furthered chiefly by two things: by diligence, and by a good name for good and fair dealing. But the gains from **bargains** are of a more doubtful nature; such as when men wait upon the necessity of others...

# Of Riches.

As for the *trading of bargains* <note>original: "chopping of bargains"</note>, when a man buys not to hold but to resell, that commonly "grinds double"—exploiting both the seller and the buyer. *Partnerships* <note>original: "sharings"</note> greatly enrich a person if the partners who are trusted are well-chosen. *Usury* <term>the practice of lending money at interest</term> is the most certain means of gain, though one of the worst; it is a means by which a man eats his bread "by the sweat of another's brow" <note>original: *in sudore vultûs alieni*</note>. Good *fortune*, such as being the *first* to come up with an *invention* or to receive a *privilege* <term>a monopoly or patent</term>, sometimes causes a wonderful overgrowth in *riches*. # Of Riches

Judgment, as well as *creativity* <note>original: "Invention"</note>, allows a man to do great *things* <note>original: "Matters"</note>; especially if the *times* are favorable. He who relies upon *guaranteed profits* <note>original: "Gains Certain"</note> shall hardly grow to great *riches*; and he who puts everything into *speculative ventures* <note>original: "Adventures"</note> often *goes bankrupt* <note>original: "break"</note> and falls into *poverty*. It is wise, therefore, to protect *speculative ventures* with *certainties* that may support any losses.

*Monopolies* and the *bulk-buying* <term>Coemption</term> <note>Coemption: The act of purchasing the entire supply of a commodity to control the market price.</note> of *goods* for *resale*, where they are not legally restricted, are great *means* to enrich oneself; especially if the *person* has information <note>original: "intelligence"</note> about what *things* are likely to come into *demand* <note>original: "Request"</note>, and so stores *himself* in advance.

*Riches* obtained through *service* to others, though it may be of the highest rank <note>original: "Rise"</note>, are among the *worst* when they are gained by *flattery*, *indulging the whims* <note>original: "Feeding Humours"</note> of others, and other *servile conditions*. As for *legacy hunting* <note>original: "Fishing for Testaments"</note> and *executorships*—as *Tacitus* says of *Seneca*, "He caught wills and childless heirs as if in a net" <note>Latin original: "Testamenta et Orbos, tanquam Indagine capi"</note>—it is even worse, because *men* must submit themselves to *lowlier persons* than they do in regular *service*. Do not much *believe* those who seem to despise *riches*, for they only despise them because they despair of attaining them.

<summary>This page explores various strategies for wealth accumulation, distinguishing between steady profits and risky speculation while criticizing the ethics of flattery and legacy hunting.</summary>

Be not **penny-wise** <note>original: "Penny-wise"; being overly concerned with saving small amounts of money while neglecting larger financial risks or opportunities.</note>; ***riches have wings***, and sometimes they fly away of themselves; sometimes they must be set flying to bring in more. Men leave their **riches** either to their **kindred** <note>original: "Kindred"; family or relatives.</note> or to the **public**; and moderate portions prosper best in both. A great estate left to an **heir** is like a lure to all the birds of prey round about, to seize on him, if he is not well-established in years and judgment. 

Likewise, boastful gifts and **foundations** <note>Endowments or charitable institutions established by a donor.</note> are like ***sacrifices without salt*** <note>A biblical reference (Leviticus 2:13) suggesting that an offering lacking the "salt" of sincerity or proper spirit is worthless.</note>; they are nothing but the ***painted sepulchres of alms*** <note>Refers to Matthew 23:27; something that looks beautiful on the outside but is full of corruption within—in this case, charity done for vanity rather than true goodness.</note>, which soon will putrefy and corrupt inwardly. Therefore, do not measure your **advancements** <note>original: "Advancements"; here meaning charitable gifts or bequests.</note> by quantity, but shape them with moderation; and do not defer **charities** until death. For certainly, if a man weighs it rightly, he who does so is generous with another man's money, rather than his own.

# Of Prophecies.

## XXXV.

I do not mean to speak of divine prophecies, nor of heathen oracles, nor of natural predictions; but only of prophecies that have been recorded in reliable memory and arise from hidden causes. The medium <note>original: "Pythonissa," a woman who channeled spirits; specifically the Witch of Endor</note> says to Saul: *Tomorrow you and your son shall be with me.* Homer has these verses:
> <note>original: "At Domus Æneæ cunctis dominabitur Oris, Et Nati Natorum, & qui nascentur ab illis."</note>
> *But the house of Aeneas shall rule over every shore,*
> *And his children's children, and those who shall be born from them.*
This was a prophecy, it seems, of the Roman Empire. Seneca the tragedian has these verses:

-> —Years will come <-
-> in the later ages, when the Ocean <-
-> shall loosen the bonds of the world, and a vast <-
-> land shall lie open, and Tiphys <note>The pilot of the ship Argo in Greek mythology</note> shall [reveal] new <-

*A prophecy of the discovery of America.*

The daughter of Polycrates <note>Polycrates was the ruler of Samos in the 6th century BCE.</note> dreamed that Jupiter bathed her father and Apollo anointed him. It later came to pass that he was crucified in an open place, where the sun made his body run with sweat and the rain washed it. Philip of Macedon <note>The father of Alexander the Great.</note> dreamed he sealed up his wife’s belly. From this, he concluded that his wife would be barren. But Aristander the soothsayer told him his wife was pregnant, because people do not usually seal vessels that are empty. 

A ghost that appeared to Marcus Brutus in his tent said to him, "You shall see me again at Philippi" <note>original: *Philippis iterum me videbis*. This refers to the site of the battle where Brutus was ultimately defeated.</note>. Tiberius said to Galba, "You too, Galba, shall taste of power" <note>original: *Tu quoque, Galba, degustabis Imperium*.</note>.

[Domitian dreamed, the night before he] was slain, that a golden head was growing out of the nape of his neck; and indeed, the succession that followed him for many years brought about a "Golden Age" <note>original: "Golden Times." This refers to the period of the Five Good Emperors who followed the tyrant Domitian.</note>. Henry the Sixth of England said of Henry the Seventh, when he was a lad and served him water: "This is the lad who shall possess the crown for which we strive." When I was in France, I heard from a certain Dr. Pena that the Queen Mother <note>Catherine de' Medici (1519–1589)</note>, who was addicted to "curious arts" <note>the occult and astrology</note>, had the nativity <note>horoscope</note> of the King her husband <note>Henry II of France</note> calculated under a false name. The astrologer gave the judgment that he would be killed in a duel. The Queen laughed at this, thinking her husband was above challenges and duels; but he was killed in a tournament <note>original: "vpon a Course at Tilt"</note> when the splinters of Montgomery's spear went through his visor <note>original: "Beuer" (the movable part of a helmet)</note>. The common <note>original: "triuiall"</note> prophecy which I heard when I was a child, while Queen Elizabeth was in the prime <note>original: "Flower"</note> of her years, was:

> *When Hemp is spun,*
> *England is done.*

...greatest in strength, though not in number, of all that ever swam upon the sea. As for *Cleon’s Dream*, I think it was a jest. It was that he was devoured by a long dragon; and it was interpreted <note>original: "expounded"</note> to refer to a sausage-maker <note>A reference to Aristophanes' play *The Knights*, where a sausage-seller out-maneuvers the politician Cleon.</note> who troubled him exceedingly. There are numbers of the same kind, especially if you include *dreams* and *predictions of astrology*. 

But I have set down these few only of reliable credit <note>original: "certaine Credit"</note> as examples. My judgment is that they ought all to be **despised**; and they ought to serve only for "winter talk" <note>original: "Winter Talke"; meaning idle tales or folklore told for entertainment by the fireside</note> by the fireside. Though when I say **despised**, I mean in terms of belief. For otherwise, the spreading or publishing of them is in no way to be despised, for they have done much mischief; I see many severe laws have been made to suppress them. 

Three things have given these prophecies favor and some credit. First, that men notice when they "hit" (come true) and never notice when they "miss"—as they also generally do with dreams. The second is that probable...

<summary>Bacon argues that while prophecies and astrological predictions should be dismissed as idle superstitions, their social impact is dangerous enough to warrant legal suppression. <meta>This page continues Bacon's skeptical analysis of historical prophecies and the psychological reasons why people believe in them.</meta>

<page-num>217</page-num>
<header>Of Prophecies</header>

# Of Prophecies

Conjectures or obscure traditions often turn themselves into *prophecies*. This happens because human nature, which craves *divination* <note>divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</note>, thinks there is no danger in foretelling things that people are actually just inferring from evidence. 

An example is *Seneca's* verse <note>original: "Seneca's Verse"; a passage from the play *Medea* that seemed to predict the discovery of the New World</note>. At that time, it was already possible to demonstrate that the globe had large landmasses beyond the *Atlantic*, which might reasonably be conceived not to be entirely sea. By adding to this the traditions found in *Plato's Timaeus* and his *Atlanticus* <note>original: "Atlanticus"; the *Critias*, Plato's dialogue concerning the story of Atlantis</note>, a person might be encouraged to turn a logical deduction into a *prediction*. 

The third and last reason (which is the most significant one) is that almost all prophecies—which are infinite in number—have been impostures <note>impostures: deceptions or frauds</note>. They were merely contrived and faked by idle and crafty minds after the event had already occurred.

<summary>Bacon argues that many famous "prophecies" are actually just logical deductions based on geographical knowledge or fraudulent claims created after the fact.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, divination, Seneca, Plato, Atlantis, Timaeus, skepticism, imposture</keywords>

# Of Ambition

## XXXVI.

Ambition is like *choler* <note>original: "Choler"; in the theory of the four humors, this was yellow bile, associated with a hot, dry, and active temperament</note>; which is a humor that makes men active, earnest, full of alacrity, and stirring, if it is not stopped. But if it is stopped and cannot have its way, it becomes *adust* <note>original: "Adust"; a medical term for humors "scorched" by excessive heat, leading to a bitter or scorched temperament</note>, and thereby *malignant* and *venomous*. So *ambitious men*, if they find the way open for their rising and continually move forward, they are more busy than dangerous. But if they are checked in their desires, they become secretly discontented and look upon men and matters with an *evil eye*; and they are best pleased when things go backward, which is the worst quality in a servant of a *prince* or *state*. Therefore it is good for *princes*, if they use *ambitious men*, to handle it so that they are always *progressive* <note>meaning: moving forward or advancing in rank</note>, and

<summary>Francis Bacon compares ambition to the humor of choler, arguing that while it drives men to be productive when encouraged, it becomes dangerous and spiteful if their advancement is blocked.</summary>
<keywords>ambition, choler, humors, political psychology, statecraft, Francis Bacon, advancement</keywords>

# Of Ambition.

...not retreat. Because this cannot happen without inconvenience, it is best not to use such temperaments at all. For if they do not rise through their service, they will take measures to ensure their service fails along with them. 

But since we have said it is best not to use men of *ambitious natures* except out of necessity, it is appropriate to discuss in which cases they are necessary. Good commanders in wars must be employed, no matter how *ambitious* they are; for the value of their service outweighs the rest. To employ a soldier without *ambition* is like pulling off his spurs. 

There is also great use for *ambitious men* in acting as screens for princes in matters of danger and envy; for no man will take that role unless he is like a <term>sealed dove</term> <note>original: "seel'd dove." In falconry, "seeling" was the practice of sewing a hawk's or dove's eyelids shut with a fine thread to make it fly higher or become more manageable.</note> that mounts higher and higher because he cannot see what is around him. There is also a use for *ambitious men* in pulling down the greatness of any subject who becomes too prominent, just as the Emperor *Tiberius* used *Macro* to pull down *Sejanus* <note>Sejanus was the powerful and ambitious head of the Praetorian Guard under Emperor Tiberius. When Sejanus became too powerful, Tiberius used Macro to orchestrate his arrest and execution.</note>. Since, therefore, they must be used in such cases, it remains to speak...

...how they are to be bridled <note>original: "brideled"</note>, so that they may be less *dangerous*. There is less *danger* from them if they are of humble <note>original: "Meane"</note> birth than if they are noble; and if they are harsh in nature, rather than gracious and popular; and if they have been recently promoted <note>original: "New Raised"</note> rather than having grown cunning and fortified in their greatness. 

It is considered by some to be a weakness in princes to have *favorites*; but it is, above all others, the best remedy against *ambitious great ones*. For when the power to grant favors or show displeasure lies with the *favorite*, it is impossible for anyone else to become *over-mighty* <note>original: "Over-great"</note>. 

Another means to curb them is to balance them with others who are just as proud as they are. But then, there must be some moderate <note>original: "Middle"</note> counselors to keep things steady; for without that ballast, the ship of state will roll too much. At the very least, a prince may encourage and accustom <note>original: "inure"</note> some lower-ranking <note>original: "Meaner"</note> persons to act, as it were, as scourges <note>term: Scourges; here, people used as instruments to punish or check the power of others</note> to *ambitious men*. 

As for keeping them vulnerable <note>term: Obnoxious; in this 17th-century context, it means liable, subject, or exposed to a particular power or ruin</note> to ruin, this may work well if they are of a timid nature; but if they are bold <note>original: "Stout"</note> and...

# Of Ambition.

...and daring, it may precipitate their designs and prove dangerous. As for pulling them down <note>In this context, "pulling them down" refers to removing ambitious men from power or lowering their status.</note>, if affairs require it and it cannot be done safely all at once, the only way is the continual interchange of favors and disgraces. By this method, they may not know what to expect and will be, as it were, in a wood <note>original: "in a Wood"; a metaphor for being lost in a maze or a state of uncertainty.</note>. 

Of *ambitions*, it is less harmful to have the *ambition* to prevail in great things than the ambition to appear in everything; for the latter breeds confusion and ruins business. But yet, there is less danger in having an *ambitious man* who is active in business than one who is great in dependences <note>original: "Dependances"; referring to a man who maintains a large network of followers, clients, or political subordinates who owe him loyalty.</note>. 

He who seeks to be eminent among able men has a great task, but that is always good for the public. But he who plots to be the only figure among ciphers <note>original: "Ciphars"; a mathematical metaphor meaning people of no value, like zeros standing next to a single digit.</note> is the ruin of a whole age. 

*Honor* has three things in it: the vantage ground to do good; the access to kings and principal persons; and the raising of a man's own fortunes. He who has the best of these intentions when he aspires...

<header>Of Masques and Triumphs</header>

# Of Masques and
## Triumphs.
### XXXVII.

These things are but toys <note>original: "Toyes"; here meaning trifles or light entertainments</note>, to be included among such serious observations. Yet, since princes will have such things, it is better that they should be graced with elegance than daubed with cost. Dancing to song is a thing of great state and pleasure. I mean that the song should be performed by a choir <note>original: "Quire"</note> placed on high, and accompanied by some broken music <term>broken music: music played by an ensemble of different types of instruments (a "consort") rather than instruments of the same family</term>. The lyrics <note>original: "Ditty"</note> should also be fitted to the theme <note>original: "Device"</note> of the performance.

Acting in song, especially in *dialogues*, has an extremely good grace. I say *acting*, not *dancing* (for that is a mean and vulgar thing). Also, the *voices* of the *dialogue* should be strong and manly—a bass and a tenor, but no treble <note>the highest vocal range, often associated with children or women; Bacon preferred the gravity of lower voices for these performances</note>.

<summary>In this essay, Francis Bacon provides guidance on the production of courtly masques and entertainments, arguing that they should favor artistic elegance and "manly" musical arrangements over mere expensive display.</summary>
<keywords>Masques, Triumphs, Francis Bacon, court entertainment, Renaissance music, aesthetics, choir, broken music</keywords>

--- Page 331 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of masques, likely concluding a sentence from the previous page regarding what makes a performance "admirable."</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

# Of Masques and Triumphs.

...able.) And the *lyrics* <note>original: "Ditty"</note> should be grand and tragic, not overly delicate or fussy. Several *choirs*, placed opposite one another and passing the melody back and forth in a round, <term>antiphonally</term> <note>original: "Antheme wise"</note>, give great pleasure. Turning *dances* into complex *geometric patterns* <note>original: "Figure"</note> is a childish distraction. Generally, it should be noted that those things which I list here are those that naturally appeal to the senses, and do not rely on trivial gimmicks.

It is true that *scene changes*, provided they are done quietly and without noise, are things of great beauty and pleasure: for they provide variety for the eye before it becomes tired of looking at the same object. Let the *scenes* be filled with *light*, especially *colored* and *varied* light. And let the performers <note>original: "Masquers"</note>, or any others who are to come down from the *stage* <note>original: "Scene"</note>, move about on the *stage* itself before their descent; for this draws the eye compellingly and creates a pleasant anticipation to see that which cannot yet be perfectly discerned. Let the *songs* be loud and cheerful, and not weak *chirping* or *whining* <note>original: "Pulings"</note>. Let the *music* likewise be *clear*, *loud*, and *well-placed*.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Francis Bacon provides aesthetic advice for courtly masques, emphasizing the importance of grand lyrics, antiphonal music, effective lighting, and smooth scene transitions over trivial complexities.</summary>

<keywords>Masques, Francis Bacon, stagecraft, Renaissance theater, lighting, scenography, choral music, choreography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Masques and Triumphs," where Francis Bacon offers practical advice on the staging and aesthetics of courtly entertainments.</meta>

# Of Masques and Triumphs.

The *colors* that show best by *candlelight* are *white*, *carnation* <note>a pinkish-red color</note>, and a kind of *sea-green*. Also, <term>Oes</term> or <term>spangles</term> <note>original: "Oes, or Spangs"; small, shiny metal discs or sequins sewn onto clothing to catch the light</note>, as they are not of great cost, provide the most brilliance. As for *rich embroidery*, it is a wasted effort because it cannot be clearly seen in the dim light. Let the *suits* of the *masquers* <note>the performers in a masque, a festive courtly entertainment involving music, dance, and acting</note> be graceful and suit the person even after the <term>visors</term> <note>original: "Vizars"; masks</note> are removed. They should not follow the examples of well-known costumes, such as *Turks*, *soldiers*, *mariners*, and the like.

Let <term>anti-masques</term> <note>humorous or grotesque interludes performed between the main acts of a masque</note> not be too long. They have commonly featured *fools*, *satyrs*, *baboons*, *wild men*, *antiques* <note>grotesque or clownish figures</note>, *beasts*, *spirits*, *witches*, *Ethiopians*, *pygmies*, <term>Turquets</term> <note>likely small figures dressed in Turkish fashion or puppets</note>, *nymphs*, *peasants*, *Cupids*, *moving statues*, and similar characters. As for *angels*, it is not comical enough to put them in *anti-masques*; on the other hand, anything hideous, such as *devils* or *giants*, is equally unfit. 

But above all, let the *music* for these performances be refreshing and include some unusual variations. Sweet scents suddenly wafting out—without any liquid drops falling—are a source of great pleasure in such a large company where there is steam and heat.

Pleasure and refreshment. *Double masques* <note>A masque was a form of courtly entertainment involving music, dancing, singing, and acting, often with elaborate costumes and sets.</note>, one consisting of men and another of ladies, adds dignity and variety. But it all amounts to nothing unless the room is kept clear and tidy.

As for *jousts*, *tournaments*, and *barriers* <note>original: "Barriers"; a form of tournament combat fought on foot across a waist-high fence.</note>, their glory lies chiefly in the chariots in which the challengers make their entrance—especially if they are drawn by exotic beasts, such as lions, bears, camels, and the like—or in the ingenious designs <note>original: "Devices"</note> of their entrance, or in the splendor <note>original: "Bravery"</note> of their uniforms <note>original: "Liveries"</note>, or in the fine equipment and trappings of their horses and armor. # Of Nature in Men.

## XXXVIII.

*Nature* is often hidden, sometimes overcome, but seldom extinguished. Force makes *nature* more violent when it returns; instruction <note>original: "Doctrine"</note> and discourse make *nature* less troublesome <note>original: "Importune"</note>; but habit <note>original: "Custome"</note> alone actually alters and subdues *nature*. 

He who seeks victory over his *nature* should not set himself tasks that are too large or too small. For the first will make him discouraged by frequent failures, and the second will result in little progress, even though he succeeds often. 

At first, let him practice with aids, just as swimmers use floats <note>original: "Bladders, or Rushes"; refers to inflated animal bladders or bundles of buoyant reeds used as early swimming aids.</note>. But after a time, let him practice under difficult conditions, as dancers do with heavy shoes. For it leads to great perfection if the practice is harder than the actual performance <note>original: "vse"</note>.

<summary>Francis Bacon discusses the persistence of human nature, arguing that while it can be hidden or temporarily suppressed, only the consistent application of habit and graduated practice can truly transform it.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Essays, human nature, habit, self-discipline, custom, behavioral change</keywords>

--- Page 335 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on human nature and self-discipline. The catchword "euer" at the bottom of the page indicates that the sentence continues on the next page with the word "ever."</meta>

# Of Nature in Men.

Where *nature* is powerful, and therefore victory over it is difficult, there needs to be a series of steps. First, one should halt and restrain *nature* in the moment—similar to a *person* who would recite the *twenty-four letters* <note>original: "Foure and Twenty Letters". In the 17th century, the English alphabet was often considered to have 24 letters, as 'I' and 'J' were frequently treated as variants of the same letter, as were 'U' and 'V'.</note> of the alphabet when he was *angry*. Then, one should reduce the quantity of the habit; for instance, if someone were abstaining from *wine*, they might move from *drinking toasts* to having only a *single drink at a meal*. And lastly, one should stop the habit altogether. But if a *man* has the *fortitude* and *resolution* to set *himself* free all at once, that is the best approach:

> *He is the best liberator of the mind who breaks the chains that gall his breast, and ceases to grieve all at once.*
<note>original: "Optimus ille Animi Vindex, lædentia pectus Vincula qui rupit, dedoluitque semel." From Ovid, *Remedia Amoris* (Remedies for Love), 293-294.</note>

Nor is the *ancient rule* wrong to bend *nature* like a *wand* toward the *opposite extreme* in order to straighten it—provided that the *opposite extreme* is not itself a *vice*. A man should not force a *habit* upon himself with *uninterrupted persistence*, but rather with some *intervals*. For the *pause* both strengthens the new *effort*, and if a *man* who is not yet perfect in his habit is...

<summary>Francis Bacon outlines strategies for overcoming one's natural inclinations or bad habits, recommending either a gradual reduction in the behavior or a sudden, resolute break.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, self-discipline, habit, Ovid, temperance, behavioral change, Francis Bacon, Remedia Amoris</keywords>

...always in practice, he will practice his errors as much as his abilities, and develop a habit of both. There is no way to fix this except through well-timed breaks <note>original: "Seasonable Intermissions"</note>.

But a man should not trust his victory over his nature too much; for nature will lie buried for a long time and yet revive when given the opportunity or temptation. It is just like ***Aesop’s Damsel*** <note>This refers to Aesop's fable "The Cat and Venus," in which a cat is transformed into a woman but reveals her true nature when she leaps out of bed to catch a mouse.</note>, who was turned from a cat into a woman; she sat very modestly at the end of the table <note>original: "Boards End"</note> until a mouse ran in front of her. Therefore, a man should either avoid the temptation altogether or expose himself to it often so that he becomes little affected by it. 

A man’s nature is best seen in privacy, for there is no pretension <term>original: "Affectation"; the act of behaving unnaturally to impress others</term>. It is also seen in times of passion, for that makes a man forget his rules <note>original: "Precepts"</note>; and in a new situation or experiment, for there habit <note>original: "Custome"</note> fails him. 

Those men are happy whose natures match their professions <note>original: "Vocations"</note>. Otherwise, they may say, ***Multùm Incola fuit Anima mea*** <note>Latin: "My soul has long been a stranger" (Psalm 120:6). Bacon uses this to describe the feeling of one's inner spirit being alienated from their daily work.</note>, when they are occupied with things they do not enjoy <note>original: "Affect"</note>. <page-num>231</page-num>
<header>Of Custom and Education.</header>

-># Of Custom and<-
->## Education.<-
->### XXXIX.<-

Men’s thoughts are very much in line with their natural inclinations; their discourse and speeches are based on their learning and the opinions instilled in them; but their actions follow what they have been accustomed to doing. And therefore, as *Machiavelli* <note>original: "Macciauel"; Niccolò Machiavelli (1469–1527), the Italian political philosopher</note> rightly notes (though he uses an unpleasant example), one cannot trust in the force of nature nor in bold words unless they are strengthened by custom <term>habitual practice or habit</term>. His example is that for the carrying out of a desperate conspiracy, a man should not rely upon the fierceness of any person’s nature or their determined promises, but should choose someone who has previously had their hands in blood <note>meaning someone who has already committed a violent act or murder</note>. But *Machiavelli* did not know of a *Friar Clément* <note>Jacques Clément, a monk who assassinated King Henry III of France in 1589</note>, nor a *Ravaillac* <note>François Ravaillac, who assassinated King Henry IV of France in 1610</note>,

<summary>Bacon argues that while nature and education influence a person's thoughts and speech, their actual behavior is governed primarily by habit (custom). He critiques Machiavelli's view on the reliability of hardened criminals in conspiracies by referencing more modern religious assassins.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Machiavelli, habit, custom, behavior, Jacques Clément, François Ravaillac, conspiracy, education</keywords>

# Of Custom and Education:

...nor a *Juan de Jáuregui*, nor a ***Balthazar Gérard*** <note>original: "Iaureguy" and "Baltazar Gerard." Both were assassins who targeted William the Silent in the 1580s, driven by religious zeal</note>; yet his rule still holds that neither *nature*, nor the commitment of words, are as powerful as *custom*. Only *superstition* is now so well-advanced that men committing their first murder are as steady as *butchers* by trade. And a sworn resolution is made equal <note>original: "Equipollent"</note> to *custom*, even in matters of bloodshed. 

In other things, the dominance of *custom* is visible everywhere; so much so that a man would marvel to hear men profess, protest, commit themselves, and speak great words, and then do exactly as they have done before, as if they were lifeless images and machines <note>original: "Engines"</note> moved only by the wheels of *custom*. 

We also see what the reign or tyranny of *custom* is. The *Indians* (I mean the sect of their wise men <note>Bacon refers to the Gymnosophists, ancient Indian philosophers known for their asceticism</note>) lay themselves quietly upon a stack of wood and sacrifice themselves by fire. Indeed, the wives strive to be burned with the corpses of their husbands <note>A reference to the practice of sati</note>. In ancient times, the boys of *Sparta* were accustomed to being whipped upon the altar of *Diana* <note>Bacon uses the Roman name for the Greek goddess Artemis. I remember at the beginning of *Queen Elizabeth’s* reign in *England*, a condemned *Irish rebel* submitted a petition to the *Deputy* <note>The Lord Deputy of Ireland, the English crown's representative</note>, asking that he might be hanged with a *withe* <note>original: "With," a flexible branch or twig, usually willow, used as a rope</note> and not with a rope halter, because that had been the custom for previous *rebels*. There are *monks* in *Russia* who, as penance, will sit a whole night in a vessel of water until they are frozen fast in hard ice. Many examples may be given of the force of *custom* upon both the mind and the body. 

Therefore, since custom is the principal ruler of a person's life, let men strive by all means to obtain good *customs*. Certainly, *custom* is most effective when it begins in one's younger years; this is what we call *education*, which is, in effect, but an *early custom*. So we see that in languages, the tongue is more adaptable to all expressions and sounds, and the joints are more supple for all feats of activity and motion in youth than they are afterward. <page-num>234</page-num>
<header>Of Custom and Education.</header>

## *Of Custom and Education.*
---

They have not allowed themselves to become fixed, but have kept themselves open and prepared to receive continual **improvement** <note>original: "Amendment"</note>, which is exceedingly **rare**. But if the **force** of *custom*, when **simple and separate** (individual), is **great**, the **force** of *custom* when **combined, joined, and collective** <note>original: "Copulate, and Conjoined, & Collegiate"</note> is far **greater**. For in those circumstances **example** teaches; **company** comforts; **emulation** <note>The drive to equal or excel others</note> quickens; and **glory** raises the spirit. So it is that in such **places**, the **force** of *custom* is at its **height** <note>original: "in his Exaltation"</note>. **Certainly**, the great **multiplication** of **virtues** within **human nature** depends upon **societies** that are well-**ordered** and **disciplined**. For **commonwealths** and **good governments** do nourish **virtue** once it has **grown**, but they do not do much to improve the seeds. But the **tragedy** <note>original: "Misery"</note> is that the most **effective means** are now applied to the **ends** that are least to be desired.

---

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that while individual habit is powerful, the collective "collegiate" custom of a disciplined society is far more influential in shaping human virtue. # Of Fortune.

## XL.

It cannot be denied that outward accidents <note>events or circumstances beyond one's control</note> contribute much to *fortune*. Favor, opportunity, the death of others, and occasions that suit a person's virtue <note>original: "Vertue"; here meaning one's specific talents or character</note>. But chiefly, the mold of a man's *fortune* is in his own hands. *Every man is the maker of his own fortune* <note>Latin original: "Faber quisque Fortunae suae"; attributed to the Roman censor Appius Claudius Caecus, though Bacon refers to him as "the Poet"</note>, says the poet. And the most frequent of external causes is that the folly of one man is the *fortune* of another. For no man prospers so suddenly as he does by the errors of others. *A serpent, unless it has eaten a serpent, does not become a dragon* <note>Latin original: "Serpens nisi serpentem comederit non fit Draco"; a proverb suggesting that to achieve greatness, one often has to consume or overcome competitors</note>. Overt <note>original: "Ouert"; public or visible</note> and apparent virtues bring forth praise; but there are secret and hidden virtues that bring forth *fortune*. Certain ways of carrying oneself <note>original: "Deliveries of a Mans Selfe"</note> which have no name. The Spanish name, *Desemboltura* <note>meaning a graceful ease, dexterity, or lack of inhibition in behavior</note>, partly ex—

# Of Fortune

...fortunate qualities; such as having a *little* of the *fool* and not *too much* of the *honest* <note>The author suggests that absolute integrity or perfect wisdom can sometimes hinder worldly success.</note>. Therefore, extreme lovers of their country or their masters were never *fortunate*, nor can they be. For when a man places his thoughts outside of himself, he does not follow his own path. A hasty *fortune* makes a man an "enterpriser" or "remover"—the *French* terms describe it better: *entreprenant* <note>bold or adventurous</note> or *remuant* <note>restless or stirring</note>—but a practiced and steady *fortune* makes the *able man*.

*Fortune* is to be honored and respected, if only for her daughters: *Confidence* and *Reputation*. For good luck <note>original: "felicity"</note> breeds these two: the first within a man's self, and the latter in others toward him. All wise men, to deflect the envy of their own virtues, tend to attribute them to Providence and *Fortune*; for in this way, they may claim their successes more easily. Besides, it is a sign of greatness in a man to be the special care of the higher powers. So *Caesar* said to the pilot in the tempest, "You carry Caesar and his fortune" <note>original Latin: *Cæsarem portas, & Fortunam eius*</note>. **M**any have made clever attacks <note>original: "witty invectives"</note> against **usury** <term>usury: the practice of lending money at interest; in this period, it referred to any interest, not just excessive rates</term>. This only: that *usury* is a concession granted because of the hardness of men's hearts <note>original Latin: *Concessum propter Duritiem Cordis*</note>. For since there must be borrowing and lending, and men are so hard-hearted that they will not lend money for free, *usury* must be permitted. Some others have made suspicious and cunning propositions regarding banks, the public discovery of men’s private wealth, and other inventions. But few have spoken of *usury* in a useful way. It is good to set before us the *disadvantages* <note>original: *incommodities*</note> and the *advantages* <note>original: *commodities*</note> of *usury*, so that the good may be either weighed or selected; and we must warily provide that, while we pursue that which is better, we do not encounter that which is worse.

The *disadvantages* of *usury* are: First, that it results in fewer merchants. For were it not for this lazy trade of *usury*, money would not lie idle, but would, for the most part, be employed in commerce <note>original: *merchandizing*</note>, which is the "gate-vein" <note>original: *Vena Porta*; an anatomical term referring to the vein that carries blood to the liver, used here to mean the primary channel of wealth</note> of wealth in a state. ## Of Usury.

In some respects usury hinders merchandising, yet in others it advances it. For it is certain that the greatest part of trade is driven by young merchants borrowing at interest. So, if the *usurer* either calls in or keeps back his money, there will immediately follow a great standstill in trade. The second benefit is that, were it not for this easy borrowing at *interest*, men’s necessities would bring upon them a most sudden ruin; they would be forced to sell their means (be it lands or goods) far below their value <note>original: "under Foot"</note>. And so, whereas *usury* only gnaws upon them, bad markets would swallow them up entirely. As for mortgaging or pawning, it will do little to mend the matter; for either men will not take pawns <note>collateral</note> without interest <note>original: "Use"</note>, or if they do, they will look precisely for the forfeiture. I remember a cruel moneyed man in the country who would say, "The Devil take this *usury*; it keeps us from the forfeitures of mortgages and bonds." The third and last benefit is that it is a vanity to conceive 

<summary>Bacon outlines the practical benefits of usury, noting that it provides essential capital for young merchants and prevents the ruinous forced sale of assets at low prices.</summary>

Therefore, to speak of the abolishing of *usury* <note>In the 17th century, "usury" referred to all lending at interest, not just lending at excessive or illegal rates.</note> is futile. All states have always had it, in one kind or rate or another. Therefore, that opinion must be sent to *Utopia* <note>Referring to Sir Thomas More's *Utopia* (1516); Bacon means the idea of an interest-free economy is a fantasy that could only exist in a fictional, perfect society.</note>.

To speak now of the reformation and *regulation* <note>original: "reiglement"</note> of *usury*; how its *disadvantages* <note>original: "discommodities"</note> may be best avoided and the *advantages* <note>original: "commodities"</note> retained. It appears by balancing the advantages and disadvantages of *usury* that two things must be reconciled. One is that the "tooth" of *usury* be ground down so that it does not bite too much; the other is that a means be left open to invite wealthy men to lend to merchants, for the continuing and stimulating <note>original: "quickening"</note> of trade. This cannot be done unless you introduce two different sorts of *usury*: a *lesser* and a *greater* rate. For if you reduce *usury* to a single low rate, it will ease the common borrower, but the merchant will be at a loss <note>original: "to seek"</note> for money. And

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the author discussed the various "discommodities" (disadvantages) and "commodities" (advantages) of usury, concluding that since usury cannot be fully abolished, it must be regulated. Catchword: kinde.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>
-># Of Usury<-

It should be noted that the trade of merchandising, being the most lucrative, can support *usury* <note>original: "Usury"; the practice of lending money at interest</note> at a high rate; other contracts cannot do so.

To serve both goals, the method would briefly be this: there should be *two rates of interest*. The first should be *free* and *general* for everyone; the second should be allowed under *license* only to *certain persons* and in *certain places* of *merchandising*.

First, therefore, let interest in general be reduced to *five percent*. Let that rate be proclaimed as *free and current*, and let the State waive its right to take any penalty for it. This will prevent borrowing from suffering any general halt or "dryness" <note>original: "Drinesse"; a lack of available capital in the market</note>. This will provide relief to countless borrowers in the country. This will, in large part, raise the price of land; because land bought at "sixteen years' purchase" <note>a valuation where the price is sixteen times the annual rental income</note> will yield over six percent, whereas this new rate of interest yields only five. For the same reason, this will encourage and sharpen the drive for industrious and profitable improvements, because many people will prefer to venture their money in that way...

<summary>Bacon proposes a dual-rate system for interest, suggesting a general cap of five percent to ease the burden on borrowers and increase land values.</summary>

# Of Usury.

...kind, then take five percent <note>original: "five in the hundred"</note>, especially having been used to greater profit.

Secondly, let there be certain persons licensed to lend to known merchants at a higher rate of interest <note>original: "usury"</note>; and let this be done with the following precautions. Let the rate be somewhat more manageable <note>original: "easier"</note> even for the merchant himself than what he was previously accustomed to pay. By this means, all borrowers shall find some relief through this reform, whether they are merchants or anyone else.

Let it be no bank or common stock <note>a "common stock" refers to a shared pool of capital, similar to a modern investment fund</note>, but let every man be master of his own money. It is not that I altogether dislike banks, but they will hardly be tolerated <note>original: "brooked"</note> because of certain suspicions. Let the state be paid <note>original: "answered"</note> a small fee for the license, and the rest of the profit be left to the lender. For if the reduction <note>original: "abatement"</note> in profit is only small, it will not discourage the lender at all. For example, a person who previously took nine or ten percent will sooner descend to eight percent than give up his trade of moneylending <note>original: "usury"</note> and move from certain gains to gains of risk <note>original: "hazard"</note>. <page-num>246</page-num>
# Of Usury

These licensed lenders are indefinite in number but limited to certain principal cities and towns of trade <term>merchandizing: commerce or trade</term>. Because then they will hardly be able to "color" <note>original: "Colour"; to disguise or pass off someone else's money as their own to take advantage of the legal rate</note> other men's money in the country. In this way, the *license of nine percent* will not drain away the current *rate of five percent*, for no man will send his money far away, nor put it into *unknown* hands.

If it is *objected* that this does, in a way, authorize *usury*, which before was in some places merely *permitted* <note>original: "Permissiue"; allowed by a lack of enforcement rather than by law</note>, the answer is: it is better to *mitigate usury* by *formal declaration* than to allow it to *rage* by *connivance* <note>original: "Conniuence"; the act of pretending not to see something, or turning a blind eye</note>.

# Of Youth
## and Age.
### XLII.

**A** man who is *young* in years may be *old* in *hours* if he has lost no *time*. But that happens rarely. Generally, *youth* is like first thoughts <note>original: "Cogitations"</note>, which are not as wise as second thoughts. For there is a youth in thoughts as well as in age. And yet the inventiveness <note>original: "Invention"</note> of *young men* is more lively than that of the old; and imaginations stream into their minds better and, as it were, more divinely. Personalities <note>original: "Natures"</note> that have much heat, and great and violent desires and agitations <note>original: "Perturbations"</note>, are not ripe for action until they have passed the peak <note>original: "Meridian"</note> of their years; as it was with *Julius Caesar* and *Septimius Severus*. Of the latter, it is said: "He spent his youth full of errors, or rather, of madness" <note>original Latin: "Iuventutem egit, Erroribus, imò Furoribus, plenam"</note>. And yet he was the most capable <note>original: "Ablest"</note> emperor,

<meta>Catchword: almost,</meta>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that while youth is characterized by lively imagination and divine-like inspiration, it often lacks the wisdom of "second thoughts" and requires the passage of time to temper violent passions for effective leadership.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, youth, old age, wisdom, Julius Caesar, Septimius Severus, temperament, leadership</keywords>

## Of Youth and Age.

...almost of all the list. But calm <note>original: "reposed"</note> natures may do well in *youth*. This is seen in *Augustus Caesar*, *Cosimo* <note>original: "Cosmus"</note> Duke of Florence, *Gaston de Foix* <note>original: "Gaston de Fois"</note>, and others. On the other hand, heat and vigor <note>original: "vivacity"</note> in *old age* is an excellent combination <note>original: "composition"</note> for business. 

*Young men* are better suited to invent than to judge; better for execution than for counsel; and better for new projects than for established <note>original: "settled"</note> business. For the experience of *age* directs them in things that fall within its scope, but in new things, it misleads <note>original: "abuseth"</note> them. The errors of *young men* are the ruin of business; but the errors of *aged men* amount only to this: that more might have been done, or done sooner. 

*Young men*, in the conduct and management of actions, embrace more than they can hold and stir up more than they can quiet. They fly to the end without considering the means and the steps <note>original: "degrees"</note> required. They pursue a few principles which they have happened upon absurdly. They are reckless in innovation <note>original: "care not to innovate," meaning they innovate without due caution</note>, which brings about unknown inconveniences.

<summary>Bacon compares the practical strengths and weaknesses of young and old men, arguing that while youth is better for innovation and action, age provides necessary judgment and stability.</summary>

# Of Youth and Age.

<sig>K k</sig>

<meta>catchword: then</meta>

[Young men] use extreme remedies at first; and, what doubles all errors, they will not acknowledge or retract them; like an unready horse <note>A horse that has not been properly trained or "broken," making it difficult to control</note> that will neither stop nor turn. *Older men* object too much, consult too long, adventure too little, repent too soon, and seldom drive business home to its full conclusion <note>original: "full Period"</note>; instead, they content themselves with a mediocrity of success.

Certainly, it is good to combine <note>original: "compound"</note> the employment of both; for that will be good for the *present*, because the virtues of either *age* may correct the defects of both. It is also good for the future, so that *young men* may be learners while *older men* are the actors <note>The primary executors or doers of business</note>. And lastly, it is good for *external circumstances* <note>original: "Externe Accidents"; refers to outward events or social perceptions</note>, because authority follows *old men*, while favor and popularity follow *youth*. 

But for the moral part, perhaps *youth* will have the preeminence, just as *age* has for *politics* <note>original: "the Politique"</note>. A certain *Rabbi* <note>Likely referring to a commentary on the Book of Joel</note>, commenting upon the text:

> *Your young men shall see visions, and your old men shall dream dreams*;

infers that *young men* are admitted nearer to God

### Of Youth and Age.

than *Old*; because *vision* is a clearer revelation than a *dream*. And certainly, the more a man drinks of the world, the more it intoxicates him; and *age* improves more in the powers of understanding than in the virtues of the will and the emotions <note>original: "Affections"</note>. There are some who have an over-early ripeness in their years, which fades early: these are, first, those who have brittle wits, the edge of which is soon dulled <note>original: "turned"</note>. An example of this was *Hermogenes* the *Rhetorician*, whose books are exceedingly subtle, but who afterwards grew dull <note>original: "waxed Stupid"</note>. 

A second type consists of those who have certain natural dispositions that are more graceful in *youth* than in *age*, such as fluent and luxuriant speech; this suits *youth* well, but not *age*. As *Tully* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator</note> says of *Hortensius*: "He remained the same, but the same was no longer becoming" <note>original Latin: "Idem manebat, neque idem decebat"</note>. 

The third type is those who take too high a tone at first, and are more high-minded <note>original: "Magnanimous"</note> than the passage of years can sustain. This was the case with *Scipio Africanus*, of whom *Livy* says in effect: "His later years fell short of his first" <note>original Latin: "Ultima primis cedebant"</note>.

<summary>Francis Bacon explores why some individuals achieve early success but fail to sustain it into old age, citing examples from classical history.</summary>

# Of Beauty.
## XLIII.

Virtue is like a rich stone, best set plainly. And surely virtue is best in a body that is comely <term>handsome or attractive in a wholesome way</term>, even if it does not have delicate features; and one that has dignity of presence rather than *beauty* of *aspect* <note>original: "Aspect"; meaning facial appearance or expression</note>. Nor is it often seen that very *beautiful persons* are otherwise of great virtue; as if Nature were more concerned <note>original: "Busy"</note> with avoiding error than in laboring to produce excellence. And therefore, they turn out to be accomplished, but not of great spirit; and they study behavior rather than virtue. 

But this does not always hold true; for *Augustus Caesar*, *Titus Vespasian*, *Philip the Fair* <note>original: "Philip le Belle"</note> of France, *Edward the Fourth* of England, *Alcibiades* of Athens, and *Ismael the Sophy* <note>Ismail I, the first Shah of the Safavid dynasty</note> of Persia, were all high and great spirits; and yet they were the most *beautiful men* of their times.

<summary>In this opening to his essay on beauty, Bacon argues that physical beauty and moral virtue rarely coexist, as nature often focuses on outward perfection at the expense of inner character, though he lists several historical exceptions.</summary>
<keywords>Virtue, Beauty, Augustus Caesar, Titus Vespasian, Philip the Fair, Edward IV, Alcibiades, Ismael the Sophy, Aesthetics, Character</keywords>

--- Page 359 ---
<meta>This page continues the essay "Of Beauty." The text begins mid-sentence, following a discussion of how beauty and virtue relate across different eras.</meta>
