# Of Beauty.

Times. In *Beauty*, the harmony of features <note>original: "favor"</note> is more important than complexion <note>original: "color"</note>. Furthermore, graceful and dignified movement is more important than features. The best part of *Beauty* is that which a painting cannot capture—nor even the first glimpse of a living person. There is no exceptional *Beauty* that does not have some strangeness in its proportions. 

One cannot tell whether Apelles <note>A renowned painter of ancient Greece; Bacon may be conflating him with Zeuxis, who famously combined the best features of five models</note> or Albrecht Dürer <note>A famous German Renaissance artist known for his complex mathematical studies of human proportion</note> was the greater trifler. One of them would create a figure using geometric proportions, while the other would take the best features from various faces to create one excellent individual. Such figures, I believe, would please no one except the painter who made <gloss>them</gloss>. 

This is not to say that a painter cannot create a face better than any that ever existed; however, he must do it by a kind of instinctive grace <note>original: "felicity"</note>—much like a musician who creates an excellent melody <note>original: "air"</note> in music—and not by following a rigid rule. One will see faces that, if you examine them part by part, you will find not a single good feature; and yet, taken all together, they look well. If it is true that the principal part of *Beauty* lies in graceful movement, it is certainly no surprise that

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that true beauty consists more in graceful movement and overall harmony than in individual features or mathematical proportions. He suggests that artistic excellence comes from a painter's intuition rather than from following rigid geometric rules.</summary>

## Of Beauty.

---

People of advanced age often seem more attractive; <note>original Latin: *Pulchrorum Autumnus pulcher* — "The autumn of the beautiful is beautiful."</note> For no *youth* can be truly handsome without some allowance being made, considering their *youth* itself as part of what makes up their attractiveness. *Beauty* is like summer fruits, which are easy to spoil and cannot last. For the most part, it leads to a reckless *youth* and an *old age* that is a little embarrassed <note>original: "out of countenance"; meaning disconcerted or ashamed of one's diminished state</note>. But certainly, on the other hand, if beauty is found in the right person, it makes virtues shine and vices blush.

# Of Deformity.

## XLIIII.

**D**eformed people are commonly even with Nature; for as Nature has done poorly by them, so they do poorly by Nature. Being for the most part—as the Bible <note>original: "Scripture"</note> says—*void of natural affection*; and so they have their revenge against Nature. Certainly, there is a connection <note>original: "Consent"</note> between the body and the mind, and where Nature errs in the one, she takes a risk in the other: *Where she errs in one, she risks in the other* <note>original Latin: "Vbi peccat in uno, periclitatur in altero."</note>.

But because there is in man a choice <term>Election: the power of choice or free will</term> concerning the framework of his mind, and a necessity in the framework of his body, the "stars" of natural inclination are sometimes obscured by the "sun" of discipline and virtue. Therefore, it is good to consider *deformity* not as a sign (which is often deceptive), but as...

<meta>catchword: a</meta>

<summary>In this opening to his essay, Francis Bacon argues that while physical deformity may predispose a person toward certain behaviors, individual discipline and virtue can overcome these natural inclinations.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, deformity, nature vs nurture, virtue, free will, character, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 362 ---
<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's essay "Of Deformity," exploring the psychological motivations and social advantages that may result from physical disability or "deformity."</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>

# Of Deformity

...a cause, which seldom fails of the effect. Whoever has anything permanent <note>original: "fixed"</note> in his person that induces contempt also has a perpetual spur within himself to rescue and deliver himself from scorn. Therefore, all *deformed persons* are extremely bold. First, as if in their own defense, being exposed to scorn; but in process of time, it becomes a general habit. It also stirs industry within them, especially of this kind: to watch and observe the weaknesses of others, so that they may have something to repay <note>In this context, "repay" suggests having leverage or a way to strike back at those who mock them.</note>. Again, in their superiors, it quenches jealousy toward them, as they are seen as people who may be despised at will. And it lays their competitors and rivals <note>original: "emulators"</note> asleep, as they never believe such people could possibly be advanced until they see them already in possession of power. So that, in effect, for a person of great intellect <note>original: "wit"</note>, *deformity* is an advantage for rising in status. Kings in ancient times (and at present in some countries) were accustomed to place great trust in *eunuchs*; because those who are

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that physical deformity acts as a powerful motivation for individuals to prove themselves, often leading to increased boldness, industry, and a strategic advantage in rising to power because they are underestimated by rivals.</summary>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the social and psychological traits of specific groups (likely eunuchs, based on the opening sentence), transitioning into a dedicated essay on physical deformity.</meta>

<page-num>256</page-num>
<header>Of Deformity.</header>

# Of Deformity.

Those who are envious toward everyone are more subservient <note>original: "obnoxious"</note> and dutiful <note>original: "officious"</note> toward a single ruler. Yet the trust placed in them has been more as good spies <note>original: "spialls"</note> and effective informers <note>original: "whisperers"</note> than as good magistrates and officials. The reasoning is much the same for *deformed persons*. The fundamental reality is that if they possess any spirit, they will seek to rescue themselves from public scorn. This must be achieved either through virtue or through malice. Therefore, do not be surprised if they sometimes prove to be excellent individuals, such as *Agesilaus* <note>Agesilaus II (c. 444–360 BC), a King of Sparta who was famously lame in one leg but a highly successful military leader.</note>, *Zanger* <note>Şehzade Cihangir (1531–1553), the youngest son of Sultan Suleiman the Magnificent; he was born with a physical deformity, often described as a hunchback.</note> the son of *Suleiman*, *Aesop*, and *Gasca* <note>Pedro de la Gasca (1493–1567), a Spanish priest and diplomat who restored order in Peru; he was described as having a very unattractive and disproportionate physical appearance.</note>, the President of *Peru*. *Socrates* may also be included among them, along with others.
